## [Whiteboard (YC W26): Open-Source IDE for Human-Agent Software Design](https://github.com/devdotfast/whiteboard) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A team of four developers launched Whiteboard, an MIT-licensed open-source desktop IDE built on CodeOSS, where humans and AI agents architect software together on a shared canvas. It integrates with agentic coding tools like Claude Code and Codex, and adds a Rust-based AST-aware semantic diff viewer, a Decision Log for agent traces, and clickable diagrams that jump directly to underlying code. As agentic coding generates ever more code and pull requests, developers risk accumulating "cognitive debt" — merging changes they no longer understand. Whiteboard targets this gap by making architecture- and spec-level review a first-class workflow, and its adoption at companies like Salesforce and Modal suggests demand for human-in-the-loop review of agent output. The app inherits VSCode keybindings and LSP support from CodeOSS, while its semantic diff viewer summarizes large added functions as pseudocode and collapses unit tests and large doc changes, all customizable via a WASM-based plugin system. The desktop app is free and self-hostable under MIT, with a planned hosted web version for session management, trajectory storage, and multiplayer reviews as the eventual revenue model.

Claude Code and Codex are terminal-based agentic coding tools from Anthropic and OpenAI respectively that can autonomously edit files and run commands, and Whiteboard gives such agents an SDK to draw diagrams and link their traces on an in-app canvas. A Substack essay by dynomight explores why the liver is uniquely capable of regenerating itself, contrasting it with other human organs that heal poorly, and frames the phenomenon in terms of evolutionary trade-offs. The liver can regenerate after partial hepatectomy or injury from hepatotoxic agents, but the process involves complex cellular interactions including transdifferentiation and hepatic progenitor cells. The liver's regenerative ability is thought to be an evolutionary trade-off, balancing repair capacity against risks such as cancer.

A new study published in iScience (Cell Press) reports that creatine supplementation promotes dendritic cell activation and enhances intratumoral cDC-mediated antitumor immunity, suppressing tumor growth in models. The finding adds to a growing body of research suggesting creatine may have translational potential as a dendritic-cell-targeted cancer immunotherapy. If validated, creatine — a cheap, widely used and generally safe nutritional supplement — could offer a simple way to boost the immune system's ability to fight cancer, potentially complementing existing immunotherapies such as dendritic cell vaccines and checkpoint inhibitors. This could affect cancer patients, oncologists, and the broader supplement industry, though human data are still lacking. The study demonstrates that creatine supplementation suppresses tumor growth and enhances intratumoral cDC-mediated antitumor immunity, and that creatine treatment enhanced activation of human monocyte-derived dendritic cells often used in dendritic cell cancer vaccines. However, the research is preclinical, and conflicting evidence from other studies shows creatine can fuel some tumor cells and promote cancer in certain mouse models, so effects likely depend on cancer type.

**Background**: Creatine is a naturally occurring compound stored in muscles and brain, widely taken as a supplement to boost athletic performance by helping regenerate ATP, the cell's energy currency. Immune cells, including CD8+ T cells and dendritic cells, also take up creatine to buffer ATP levels and support their functions. Dendritic cells are specialized immune cells that capture tumor fragments and present them to killer T cells, making them a key target in cancer immunotherapy. Previous studies have shown creatine supplementation can enhance CD8+ T cell antitumor activity, but its role in dendritic cells and its overall effect on cancer remain debated.

## [Aalto University Opens Nokia Design Archive to the Public](https://repo.aalto.fi/index.php?name=SO_b66a9391-dcf8-4399-8e87-611f84c3fc4c) ⭐️ 7.0/10

On January 15, 2025, Aalto University launched the Nokia Design Archive, a free online portal containing over 700 curated entries — sketches, photographs, videos, interviews, and roughly 2,000 objects — spanning Nokia's design history from the mid-1990s to 2017. The archive includes previously confidential internal documents, notably a 2007 presentation titled "Apple iPhone was launched" that reveals how Nokia assessed the iPhone threat at launch. The archive gives the public, designers, and historians rare first-hand access to the internal design thinking of a company that once dominated the global mobile phone market, making it a valuable resource for understanding how design decisions shaped — and failed to anticipate — the smartphone era. Its release has also fueled broader public discussion about Nokia's strategic missteps and the nature of technological disruption. The collection spans the mid-1990s to 2017 and includes not only iconic Nokia phones but also previously unseen models, concepts, and handmade experiments, alongside paper documents and digital files. The archive's landing page has already attracted over 18,000 page views, with researchers, journalists, and everyday users sharing their finds on mainstream and social media.

**Background**: Nokia was the world's largest mobile phone maker for roughly a decade before Apple's iPhone (2007) and the rise of Android reshaped the industry, eventually leading Nokia to sell its phone business to Microsoft in 2014. Aalto University, based in Finland, acquired Nokia's design materials and spent years organizing them into a public digital archive. The archive's 2007 internal presentation is notable because it shows Nokia recognized the iPhone as a threat but ultimately failed to respond in time.

Liquid AI published a Hugging Face blog post introducing LFM2.5-VL-DSpark, a speculative decoding method that pairs a small 279.5M-parameter vision-language drafter with the LFM2.5-VL-3B target model to speed up multimodal inference. The drafter weights are available on Hugging Face with support in llama.cpp, SGLang, and MLX-VLM, and the release focuses on edge and on-device deployment. Vision-language models are increasingly deployed in latency- and memory-constrained environments such as phones and edge devices, where inference cost is a major bottleneck. A dedicated multimodal drafter that preserves output quality while cutting decoding time could make real-time VLM applications more practical without requiring larger or more expensive hardware. The drafter is a 279.5M-parameter model designed specifically for vision workloads, and running it with SGLang requires a build that includes DSpark support for LFM2 targets (PR #40651). The blog also discusses the limits of speculative decoding for vision tasks on edge hardware, noting that speedups depend on the acceptance rate of drafted tokens.

rss · Hugging Face Blog · Sep 24, 14:08

**Background**: Speculative decoding is an inference optimization where a small, fast 'draft' model proposes several tokens that a larger 'target' model then verifies in parallel, accepting correct guesses and rejecting wrong ones. This preserves the target model's output distribution while reducing the number of expensive forward passes. Vision-language models combine an image encoder with a language model to handle tasks like image captioning and visual question answering, and their multimodal inputs make drafting harder than in pure text settings.

## [Audit Reveals Silent Failures in Agent-Tool Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26836) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.26836) audits silent failures in agent-tool interactions within scientific workflows, where tool invocations appear successful but return incomplete or missing data without any notification. Examining 15 scientific tools in the ToolUniverse environment, the authors identified 91 manually validated failures, most commonly missing data or fields and inconsistencies in search, filtering, or ranking criteria. These silent failures can propagate undetected into apparently valid scientific outputs, undermining the reliability of automated agentic pipelines that increasingly drive research and data analysis. The findings highlight a critical gap in AI safety and reliability, since current benchmarks focus on task success rather than the integrity of agent-tool communication. Most of the 91 failures occurred in the API layer (51) or wrapper layer (25), with potential for silent failure amplification downstream. The study structures failures around 7 failure loci and proposes the concept of contextual reliability, along with mechanisms for testing, disclosing, monitoring, and measuring such failures across the agent-tool interaction pipeline.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic AI systems increasingly rely on automated pipelines that integrate multiple external tools, often through APIs or wrappers. ToolUniverse is an open platform providing a standardized interaction model that lets any AI agent use tools regardless of their underlying implementation, whether local Python scripts, remote APIs, or lab instruments. Prior research has mainly measured task success and completion, leaving the reliability of individual agent-to-tool calls largely unexamined.

## [JAZ: A Minimalist LLM Agent Framework Built on One Recursive Primitive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26891) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.26891) introduces JAZ, an LLM agent framework that exposes only a single LLM-based primitive called invoke plus a set of built-in hooks for constraints and monitoring. Using invoke alone — with no hand-designed tools, memory, or file system — JAZ outperforms Letta (MemGPT) by 8% at half the cost on the recall-heavy portion of StuLife, and beats ACE by 4% at lower cost on AppWorld for continual self-improvement. The work challenges the prevailing trend of building ever more complex agent stacks with specialized memory and self-improvement modules, suggesting that a minimal harness may suffice for many workflows. If validated, it could simplify how agent frameworks are designed and reduce the engineering and cost overhead of production LLM agents. The invoke primitive generalizes existing code-mode agent loops and satisfies two defining properties: the LLM can write arbitrary executable code that may include recursive invoke calls, and everything visible to the LLM — all inputs to invoke and its interaction history with the code environment — are variables in the code environment. The authors frame invoke as a language primitive representing a function whose implementation is supplied at runtime by an LLM on each call.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Modern LLM agents are typically built around an "agent loop": the model is placed in an environment exposing tools, and it alternates between calling tools and observing their outputs. Some workflows, such as long-horizon tasks requiring memory beyond the context window or systems that improve themselves over time, have traditionally needed extra engineering like dedicated memory modules. JAZ asks how much of that can be handled by the agent loop itself, treating the harness as a language rather than a collection of specialized components.

## [TwinCheck Verifies Suspicious Tool Calls in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26911) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TwinCheck introduces an inference-time verification policy that only replaces a suspicious tool call when the trace satisfies an evidence condition tied to a trace-local failure hypothesis, constructing a counterfactual "negative twin" alternative that must pass structural checks and be preferred by a pairwise verifier in both candidate orders. In the primary analysis of 159 multi-turn BFCL V4 tasks with complete exact-replay pairs, the full policy raised task success for GPT-5.6 Sol from 45.3% to 58.5% (95% task-bootstrap CI [8.2, 18.8]) with no observed success-to-failure regressions. A single locally plausible but wrong tool call can derail an otherwise successful agent trajectory, so this work reframes execution-boundary repair as a constrained comparison in which the counterfactual action itself becomes the object of verification. If it generalizes, it offers a safer inference-time intervention pattern for tool-using LLM agents, directly relevant to agent reliability and AI safety research. The policy intervenes only under an evidence condition, and the negative twin must pass structural checks plus win the pairwise verifier in both candidate orders, which guards against the replacement itself introducing the failure verification is meant to prevent. Evaluation uses exact replay, holding the agent's parsed responses and actions fixed until the first accepted replacement, so intervention effects are separated from resampling; the abstract is truncated and no community discussion is available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Tool-using LLM agents operate in stateful loops where each step's tool call depends on prior actions and observations, so an early mistake can compound across a multi-turn trajectory. BFCL V4 is the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard's agentic evaluation suite, testing tool selection, schema adherence, and argument correctness across simple, multiple, parallel, and nested function calls. Verification at inference time is an emerging reliability technique, but naive intervention risks replacing a correct action with a worse one, which is the problem TwinCheck's evidence-grounded, paired-comparison design targets.

## [PLLM+ Hybrid Pipeline Solves Python Dependency Conflicts via Replay and LLM Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26952) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces PLLM+, a hybrid dependency-repair pipeline that solves 1,500 of 2,891 failing snippets on the HG2.9K benchmark, up from 1,169 solved by the PLLM baseline, while cutting average runtime from 368.7 to 71.8 seconds per snippet. The pipeline prioritizes deterministic steps—static AST-based interpreter inference, replay of historically successful dependency configurations, and live PyPI validation—before falling back to a structured LLM-based repair loop with typed error classification and Proposer/Critic agents. Python dependency conflicts are a persistent pain point that causes real-world code to fail at execution, and this work shows that deterministic reuse of previously validated configurations can be both simpler and more effective than LLM-only repair in benchmark settings. The 80% runtime reduction and improved solve rate could influence how future dependency-resolution tools balance cheap deterministic strategies against expensive LLM fallbacks. Most successful fixes come from replaying known configurations: 1,495 of the 1,500 successful fixes are produced by the solutions database, while the LLM fallback accounts for only 5 additional fixes. The abstract does not detail the representativeness of the HG2.9K benchmark or the generalizability of the approach beyond this setting.

**Background**: Python's package ecosystem relies on version constraints, and incompatible constraints, missing packages, or undocumented compatibility relationships often cause code snippets to fail at execution—a situation commonly called "dependency hell." The HG2.9K benchmark is a curated set of 2,891 real-world challenging Python programs used in the literature to evaluate dependency conflict resolution techniques. PLLM+ builds on the earlier PLLM approach and combines deterministic replay with LLM-based repair, where Proposer/Critic agents are a common pattern for iterative LLM refinement.

## [Evidence Order Shifts Multimodal LLM Judgments, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26986) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.26986) shows that in multimodal large language models, swapping only the positions of conflicting evidence—keeping instructions and content fixed—consistently shifts answers toward whichever modality (image or speech) is placed after the conflicting text. The authors call this phenomenon "cross-modal evidence noncommutativity" and use it to revisit earlier studies on text bias, arguing their fixed-order setups may have produced misleading conclusions. This finding suggests that measured "text bias" in multimodal models may partly reflect evidence position rather than a genuine modality preference, which could invalidate conclusions from prior evaluations. It implies that benchmark designers and model developers must control for evidence order when testing modality reliance, otherwise evaluation results may be systematically confounded. The study uses a paired comparison that holds task instructions and evidence content constant while swapping only the positions of the two conflicting sources, and the effect is observed across both vision and speech models. The authors also analyze why earlier experimental settings—such as fixed evidence order or moving task instructions along with the evidence—can lead to misleading conclusions about text reliance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) process multiple input types such as text, images, and audio, and prior work has reported a "text bias" where models favor textual input over visual evidence. Such studies typically present conflicting evidence in a fixed order, making it hard to separate a true modality preference from a simple position effect. This paper isolates order as a variable to test whether the same evidence can yield different judgments depending on its arrangement.

## [RLDS Decomposes Subtasks to Fix GRPO Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27035) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.27035) introduces Reinforcement Learning with Decomposed Subtasks (RLDS), whose core component Subtask-Decomposed Advantage Estimation (SDAE) replaces the single scalar GRPO advantage with per-subtask group-relative advantages on a fixed taxonomy. Evaluated on FrozenLake, HotpotQA, ScienceWorld, and DeepResearch, RLDS gains +11.5 points on ScienceWorld and +9.8 points on FrozenLake, while showing no significant improvement on HotpotQA and DeepResearch. Credit assignment is a central bottleneck in training multi-turn language model agents, where sparse and delayed rewards make it hard to know which skill drove an outcome. By decomposing trajectory rewards along subtasks, RLDS offers a principled alternative to scalar-advantage methods like GRPO, potentially improving both sample efficiency and training stability for agentic RL. SDAE splits trajectory reward into per-subtask shares, computes a group-relative advantage per subtask, and distributes per-token credit weighted by each subtask's importance, concentrating credit around the reflection step that marks a subtask as consequential. Gains scale with subtask heterogeneity (largest on ScienceWorld and FrozenLake, within noise on HotpotQA and DeepResearch), and ScienceWorld also shows a 10.9% reduction in wall-clock time per step versus scalar GRPO.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is a reinforcement learning algorithm widely used to fine-tune large language models; it eliminates the value function by comparing outputs within a group to estimate advantages, reducing memory and compute costs. However, GRPO collapses an entire multi-turn rollout into a single scalar trajectory reward before the policy update, which is lossy when a task composes distinct skills under sparse, delayed feedback. The credit assignment problem—determining how much each action contributed to a final reward—becomes especially acute in agentic RL settings where models interact with tools and environments over many turns.

## [Wake Word Detection Extended with Contextual Reasoning and Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27037) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces a wake-up system that extends traditional direct keyword detection with contextual trigger detection, using reasoning after an initial wake word activation to distinguish user commands from unrelated speech. The authors also present a data generation architecture that produces a 62.3-hour corpus of controllable multi-speaker conversations containing direct invocations, contextual follow-ups, and non-addressed speech, and they release the code, dataset, and trained models. This work addresses a key limitation of conventional wake word detection, which often cannot tell whether speech after activation is actually directed at the assistant, potentially improving the reliability and context-awareness of voice assistants. By releasing a sizable synthetic dataset and models, it also lowers the barrier for reproducible research in intelligent assistant technologies. The system combines direct keyword detection with contextual trigger detection and reasoning, and the released corpus is a 62.3-hour controllable multi-speaker synthetic conversation dataset covering direct invocations, contextual follow-ups, and non-addressed speech. Experimental results are reported across diverse synthetic conversational scenarios, though the paper's evaluation appears to rely on synthetic data rather than real-world recordings.

**Background**: Wake word detection, also called keyword spotting, is a subtask of automatic speech recognition that identifies predefined keywords or short phrases in a continuous audio stream to activate a device such as a voice assistant. Traditional systems focus only on spotting the wake phrase and do not reason about whether the following speech is addressed to the assistant. A new arXiv paper (2609.27041) introduces a generation-replay protocol that extracts activation-importance signatures from reasoning tokens, then clusters them without supervision across eight models and five math reasoning sources. Across all 40 model-source cells, the recovered clusters beat matched-size random baselines, and two frontier-LLM judges found approach-level coherence in 77-82% of real clusters versus only 6-11% in within-source controls. The finding suggests that topic-stratified benchmarks and topic-balanced training corpora may miss the axis that actually matters, since even deliberately topic-balanced data can remain imbalanced over reasoning approaches. This could reshape how researchers design math benchmarks, curate training data, and interpret model internals. The study spans eight open math-capable LLMs and five mathematical reasoning sources, validating recovered structure through structural, semantic, and intervention tests. In approach-controlled prompting, changing the requested reasoning approach shifted cluster assignment in seven of eight model conditions, while paraphrases largely preserved it.

## [Frozen Statistical Referee Cuts False Factor Discoveries by LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27051) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27051) proposes 'governed self-evolution' for LLM agents in quantitative factor research: the agent may propose investment factors, but a frozen statistical referee it cannot touch must judge them. The referee scores candidates only on market outcomes revealed after submission, using a betting-based e-process, so its false-discovery guarantee holds at every stopping time for any proposal policy. Across a synthetic world with planted truth, a probe-authoring environment, and a ten-year walk-forward on the CSI 500, the frozen referee admitted 5-11 times fewer sub-threshold factors than three deliberately leaky referees under a scripted proposer, and no proposer closed that gap. The work offers a concrete governance pattern for trustworthy LLM agent deployment in finance: separating proposal from judgment prevents agents from gaming their own validation, a key AI-safety concern as agents increasingly run end-to-end research pipelines. Its anytime-valid false-discovery control is directly relevant to quantitative finance, where data snooping and backtest overfitting routinely produce spurious 'discoveries'. The certificate comes at a cost in time: an admitted true factor waits about 500 trading days, so the certified portfolio's Sharpe ratio trails an ungated one. The language model proposer beats the script, matches the bandit, and uniquely writes its own diagnostic probes, a capability the bandit lacks; the paper's conclusion is that judging belongs to the procedure, while proposing and instrument-making belong to the agent.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly used to automate quantitative factor research: proposing candidate investment factors, backtesting them, selecting survivors, and retiring failures. A central risk is that an agent controlling its own validation can inflate false discoveries, since repeatedly checking results as data arrives invalidates standard fixed-sample statistical tests. Anytime-valid inference, built on e-values and e-processes and often implemented via testing-by-betting, restores error guarantees under optional stopping, while the CSI 500 is a capitalization-weighted index of 500 mid- and small-cap stocks on the Shanghai and Shenzhen exchanges.

A new arXiv paper (2609.27150) finds that a simple random-without-replacement routing policy, in which each agent debates with two distinct, newly sampled peers every round, consistently improves the accuracy-cost trade-off of sparse multi-agent debate (MAD) compared to complex topology control mechanisms. The authors also show that lightweight deliberation stopping can substantially cut inference cost while preserving competitive accuracy. The result challenges the prevailing trend of designing increasingly sophisticated learned or adaptive communication topologies for LLM multi-agent systems, suggesting that such complexity should first be justified against strong simple baselines. This could steer the field toward cheaper, more practical multi-agent debate designs and change how researchers benchmark topology-control methods. The proposed policy samples two distinct peers per agent per round without replacement, and the paper pairs it with lightweight stopping rules that reduce inference cost while maintaining accuracy. The authors explicitly argue that sophisticated topology adaptation methods should be evaluated against strong simple routing and stopping baselines before their added complexity is justified.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent debate (MAD) is a paradigm in which multiple large language model (LLM) instances iteratively exchange and critique their reasoning to reach a more accurate final answer. Communication topology — which agents talk to which, and how often — is central to MAD, and recent work has proposed learned, adaptive, or dynamically reconfigured topologies to boost accuracy. Sparse communication, where each agent only interacts with a few peers, has been shown to achieve competitive results at much lower cost.

## [MRT Boosts Small Model Accuracy in Power Outage Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27197) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper applies Minimum Risk Training (MRT) to power outage report generation for the Outage Data Initiative Nationwide (ODIN), transforming heterogeneous reports into standardized XML compliant with CIM IEC 61968-3. The approach improves Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct's overall accuracy from 16.20% to 68.95%, demonstrating the effectiveness of sequence-level optimization for domain-specific structured generation. This result shows that sequence-level optimization, an idea introduced a decade ago, still delivers large gains for modern LLMs on structured, domain-specific tasks where token-level maximum likelihood falls short. It matters for utilities and energy informatics practitioners who need reliable automated conversion of outage data into standards-compliant formats, and it suggests MRT is a practical fine-tuning option for small models in specialized domains. The work targets Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, a 7B-parameter small language model, and uses MRT to directly optimize a sequence-level evaluation metric rather than only the token-level maximum-likelihood objective. The reported jump from 16.20% to 68.95% overall accuracy is specific to the ODIN outage report task and CIM IEC 61968-3 XML output, so the gains may not transfer directly to other domains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Minimum Risk Training (MRT) is a technique that trains a model to minimize the expected risk over sampled output sequences, allowing it to optimize sequence-level metrics such as accuracy instead of only predicting the next token correctly. It was originally introduced for neural machine translation around 2016 and has recently seen renewed interest for modern language models. ODIN (Outage Data Initiative Nationwide) is a U.S. Department of Energy-backed effort to establish a digital reporting standard for power outage data, while CIM IEC 61968-3 is an international standard defining information exchanges between electrical distribution systems. Together they provide the domain and output format that this paper's model must handle.

## [XLOG: A CUDA-Native Engine for Neurosymbolic Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27203) ⭐️ 7.0/10

XLOG is a CUDA-native logic programming engine that unifies neural perception with deterministic Datalog, probabilistic inference, and epistemic reasoning through a typed frontend and provider-owned CUDA runtime. It reports a 2.74x MNIST-addition training speedup from circuit caching and a 27.96x geometric-mean gain from a worst-case-optimal join subsystem over its binary-join baseline. This work shows that neurosymbolic reasoning can be executed largely on-device with certified zero tracked host-device transfers for resident recursive and Monte Carlo cores, which matters for researchers seeking GPU-accelerated reasoning without CPU orchestration bottlenecks. The reported speedups and memory reductions suggest practical paths for scaling probabilistic and logical inference in machine learning pipelines. Ordinary Datalog and exact inference are host-orchestrated, while certified resident recursive and Monte Carlo sampled cores record zero tracked host-device transfers before a bounded terminal receipt. The probabilistic path supports end-to-end gradients through GPU knowledge compilation from provenance to CNF to Decision-DNNF, exact weighted model counting, and backward gradients, with a final smoothed circuit certified against its source formula before caching or evaluation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Neurosymbolic AI combines neural networks with symbolic reasoning such as logic and knowledge representation to improve reliability and generalization. Datalog is a declarative logic programming language often used for deductive databases and recursive queries, while probabilistic inference computes conditional probabilities over uncertain models. XLOG targets this intersection by compiling logical and probabilistic knowledge into GPU-executable circuits.

<a id="item-58"></a>
## [DRSR Scores Deletion Sets to Compress Long-Horizon Agent History](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27276) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint introduces Direct Relational Set-Risk Pruning (DRSR), which reframes agent-history compression as risk-constrained selection over deletion sets rather than scoring individual history units. Using offline counterfactual supervision that jointly deletes protocol-valid history blocks and measures the change in teacher-forced likelihood of the same recorded next output, DRSR trains a lightweight scorer that predicts set-level harm from online-visible relations. On WorkBuddyBench Full260 it raises mean reward from 0.699 to 0.802 while cutting total model tokens by 20.820%, and on the fixed Eval40 comparison it reaches 0.794 reward at 1.211M tokens per task, 35.850% fewer tokens than the uncompressed agent. Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate reasoning traces, tool exchanges, and observations that grow without bound, making context compression a practical bottleneck for cost and latency. Because the safety of deleting several units is not determined by their individual scores—redundant evidence, accumulated small effects, and retained information all interact—DRSR's set-level view could make agent memory pruning more reliable than singleton scoring. If the reported gains hold up, it offers a concrete recipe for cheaper long-horizon agent inference without sacrificing task reward. DRSR evaluates a small set of structurally valid deletion candidates with the lightweight scorer and removes the largest feasible set under recency, protocol, budget, and learned-risk constraints, abstaining when no set is sufficiently safe. Ablations and mechanistic analyses indicate that decision-conditioned relations, retained-context information, pair interactions, and abstention each contribute to reliable pruning, though the work is an arXiv preprint without independent validation yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon agents are LLM-driven systems that pursue multi-step goals, calling tools and observing results over many turns, so their working context keeps expanding and eventually exceeds model limits or inflates inference cost. Context compression reduces this working memory while trying to preserve what still matters, and prior approaches typically score each historical unit independently before dropping the least useful ones. Counterfactual supervision is a learning technique that assigns credit by intervening on part of the input and observing how the output changes, which here means deleting history blocks and measuring the effect on the recorded next output.

<a id="item-59"></a>
## [TimeEvo: Failure-Driven Self-Evolution for Time Series Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27277) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TimeEvo, a new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.27277), proposes a failure-driven self-evolution framework in which a time series QA agent clusters its diagnosed failures into capability gaps, plans a measurement for each gap, synthesizes evidence-only tools to fill them, and admits the resulting candidate library only through a paired admission gate. Starting from an empty tool library, it improves accuracy on all ten time series QA tasks and all three tested backbones, and a library grown on a cheap model still gains when installed into stronger models. The paper identifies two concrete failure modes in the common practice of hand-curating agent tool libraries: Human-Agent Tool Misalignment, where a 21-tool expert-curated library helps on some tasks but hurts on others and drops anomaly accuracy under every backbone tested, and Silent Harm, where one round of generic self-revision changes 147 answers and breaks 56 of them while the final score moves less than a point. Because tool utility is question-dependent at runtime but tools are supplied in advance and judged by a single average, this work suggests agent tooling should be grown and gated from observed failures rather than curated up front. The framework's core mechanism is a paired admission gate that decides whether to admit the synthesized candidate tool library, and the synthesized tools are described as "evidence-only," meaning they are built to supply measurable evidence for a specific capability gap rather than generic functionality. The code is released at https://github.com/Muyiiiii/TimeEvo, and the empirical claims cover ten time series QA tasks across three backbones, though the work is a specialized preprint without independent replication yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Time series agents are LLM-based systems that answer analytical questions about temporal data—such as anomaly detection or trend queries—by calling external tools, and which tools they carry is normally decided by humans before the agent runs. Self-evolving agents aim to improve themselves over time, but recent research has flagged risks such as capability erosion, where continual adaptation degrades previously acquired skills, and misevolution, where self-improvement deviates in unintended or harmful ways. TimeEvo sits in this line of work by trying to make tool acquisition for time series QA driven by diagnosed failures and validated by a gate rather than by human curation or unchecked self-revision.

## [EnSIMem: Entity-Structured Indexing for Long-Term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27279) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27279) introduces EnSIMem, an entity-structured long-term memory architecture that organizes agent-user interactions into theme-coherent episodes and builds dialogue-grounded index entries of the form [entity][entity type][property:value]. The code is released at https://github.com/RamonMeng/EnSIMem. Existing memory systems often compress interactions into generic summaries or retrieve anonymous text chunks, making it hard for an agent to identify the correct entity, property, and supporting evidence. EnSIMem's entity-structured indexing and episode-level provenance could improve reliability for long-term conversational agents that must recall facts, preferences, events, and changes over time. Each index entry preserves its source turns, temporal information, and available multimodal fields; online, the agent's request is decomposed into evidence requirements aligned with the memory index, and entity-property lookup plus adaptive retrieval support point, temporal, compositional, and aggregation reasoning. The agent generates responses from preserved source evidence rather than lossy summaries, achieving high answer accuracy with compact contexts and favorable online efficiency on long-term agent-memory benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Long-term agent memory refers to the component of an AI agent that stores and retrieves information from a continuously growing interaction history, so the agent can recall facts, preferences, and events across sessions. Prior approaches often rely on generic summaries or anonymous text chunks, which lose entity identity and provenance. EnSIMem instead structures memory around entities and their properties, similar in spirit to entity memory in agent frameworks, and uses adaptive retrieval to gather only the evidence needed for a given query.

## [LLM Agents Encode Memory Control Signals Before Acting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27286) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27286) finds that long-horizon language model agents already encode compression and recall needs in their hidden states immediately before each action, and these signals cannot be explained by context length or interaction progress alone. Based on this, the authors propose PaMER, which combines state-guided compression with external evidence retrieval, and PaMER+, which adds step-level evidence selection; experiments on WorkBuddyBench show substantially reduced context consumption with competitive task performance. This suggests that proactive context management can be driven by the model's own internal signals rather than external heuristics, which could make long-horizon agents more efficient and scalable. It points toward agent architectures where memory operations are triggered by learned representations, potentially reducing the cost and information loss of ever-growing interaction histories. The memory control signals exhibit distinct formation patterns across model depth, and most memory decision information is preserved in a compact recent context, while selectively restored historical evidence complements long-range dependencies that recent context misses. PaMER+ introduces step-level evidence selection to recover only the historical information required by the current task, and the approach is evaluated across multiple context management baselines and model backbones.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate interaction history over many steps, which increases computational cost and makes relevant information harder to preserve and reuse. Existing context management methods focus on how to compress or retrieve history, but largely leave open whether the model itself already represents the need for these operations before they occur. Hidden states are the internal vector representations produced by a language model at each layer, and prior work has shown they can encode structured information beyond the final output.

## [PotARCin Benchmark Expands ARC Abstract Reasoning Evaluation to Five Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27288) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced PotARCin, a benchmark that extends the Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) by evaluating a model's understanding of a task's underlying abstract rule across five dimensions: Definition, Classification, Constrained Generation, Editing, and Inversion. Across five state-of-the-art models tested on the ARC-AGI-1 training set, PotARCin revealed a 25-52 percentage-point performance gap compared to standard ARC evaluation, and models scored only 1-8% accuracy on the new held-out hand-crafted P-ARC test set. This work shows that standard ARC accuracy, which only checks whether a model produces the correct output grid, substantially overstates a model's genuine abstract reasoning ability. By revealing that multi-dimensional evaluation reorders model rankings and that models often contradict rules they have correctly stated, PotARCin could push the AI community toward more holistic benchmark design and more honest measures of skill acquisition. PotARCin uses programmatic methods to generate new task instances and transform inputs for a given ARC task, enabling dynamic generative sampling beyond fixed input-output pairs. The study also examines generative sampling effects, corruption-type difficulty, and self-consistency, finding that models frequently contradict their own formalized rule even when they have stated it correctly.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC), introduced by François Chollet in 2019, is a benchmark designed to measure AI skill acquisition and progress toward human-level fluid intelligence. Standard ARC evaluation presents a few input-output grid examples and asks the model to produce the correct output grid for a test input, testing only a single capability. PotARCin builds on this by probing whether a model truly understands the abstract rule behind a task, not just whether it can reproduce one correct answer.

## [StateComp: Learning When to Compress History in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27298) ⭐️ 7.0/10

StateComp introduces a state-conditioned compression framework that decides when past interactions in a long-horizon agent have become safe to replace, using a two-stage annotation procedure to build KEEP and READY supervision and an imbalance-aware router trained on frozen language model hidden representations. On WorkBuddyBench it cuts total agent and summarization tokens by 52.27% while maintaining task performance, and achieves a 12.67-fold speedup in representation extraction. Context length is a fundamental bottleneck for long-horizon LLM agents, and existing methods compress history using fixed windows, periodic schedules, or current relevance rather than asking whether a past interaction is still needed for future actions. By making compression state-dependent, StateComp offers a more principled way to reduce context overhead without sacrificing task success, which could benefit any agent that runs for many steps in real environments. The framework uses a bounded state representation to lower the cost of evaluating long histories, and groups adjacent READY interactions into continuous spans that are replaced with compact summaries during execution. The router is trained on frozen language model representations rather than fine-tuning the base model, and the evaluation is limited to a single benchmark, WorkBuddyBench, so generalization to other agent tasks remains to be shown.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon agents are LLM-based systems that plan and act over extended sequences of steps, interacting with environments, recovering from mistakes, and adapting strategies as they go. Because they linearly accumulate interaction history into an ever-expanding context, they are fundamentally constrained by context length, which drives research into context compression techniques that shrink long histories into shorter summaries or memory slots. StateComp builds on this line of work but reframes the problem as deciding when, given the agent's evolving state, a past interaction has become safe to compress.

## [GEM: Training-Free Compression for Long-Horizon Agent Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27332) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Geometry Guided Evidence Preserving Memory (GEM), a training-free compressor for long-horizon agent histories that prioritizes task and execution evidence before using geometric residuals to complete coverage. At identical retained block counts, evidence-aware selection raises next-action Top-3 retention from 0.31 to 0.69 while centroid similarity stays at 0.98, and GEM cuts mean combined token usage from 2.69M to 2.11M per task (a 21.4% reduction) with comparable task reward. This work challenges the common assumption that geometric redundancy is a sufficient criterion for safely compressing agent histories, showing that similar global geometry can preserve very different amounts of task evidence. It has practical implications for efficient agent memory management and inference cost reduction, especially for long-horizon agents that accumulate large interaction histories. Controlled replacement experiments show that action-related information can be substantially altered while global geometric measures remain nearly unchanged, meaning centroid similarity is a poor proxy for evidence retention. GEM is training-free, so it does not update agent weights, making it potentially suitable for closed-source API-based agents.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon agents accumulate growing interaction histories, which increases context length and inference costs. Prior work such as Acon (Agent Context Optimization) compresses history and observations for long-horizon LLM agents, and other training-free prompt compression frameworks exist, but this paper argues that compression should optimize for preserved task evidence rather than geometric coverage alone. The next-action Top-3 retention metric measures whether the correct next action remains among the top three candidates after compression.

## [New Metrics Reveal 'Alignment Inertia' Limits Fine-Tuning Override](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27333) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes Override Success Rate (OSR) and alignment inertia to measure how reliably system prompts and LoRA fine-tuning override prior model behavior. Testing Llama and Mistral on medical misinformation and hate speech, the authors found that in Mistral's restrictive hate-speech condition, LoRA increased inertia by 46.5 percentage points, meaning fine-tuning reinforced rather than overrode prior behavior. This work challenges the common assumption that fine-tuning reliably steers models toward operator-desired behavior, showing that prior training can persistently constrain downstream governance. It has direct implications for AI safety, model governance, and anyone relying on system prompts or LoRA to enforce policy, especially in high-stakes domains like hate speech and medical misinformation. The study uses TRAK, a data attribution method, to test whether inertia is linked to weaker adaptation signals; TRAK achieves an AUC of at least 0.85 in 7 of 8 conditions and outperforms model confidence, TF-IDF similarity, and embedding similarity as a predictor of inertia. Alignment inertia persists across both models but varies by model, domain, and policy direction.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: System prompts and fine-tuning are standard tools platform operators use to govern large language model behavior, but models inherit behaviors from prior training that may resist these interventions. LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that freezes base model weights and trains small low-rank matrices, making it popular for cheap adaptation. TRAK (Tracing with the Randomly-Projected After Kernel) is a data attribution technique that traces model predictions back to training examples, allowing counterfactual questions about which data influenced a behavior.

## [JitMem Defers LLM Agent Memory Curation to Read Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27334) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27334) proposes Just-in-Time Memory (JitMem), which retains raw agent trajectories and defers memory curation until read time, synthesizing a task-adaptive payload once the current task is known. Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and τ²-bench, JitMem beats the strongest write-time baseline by 16.2, 16.3, and 3.9 absolute success-rate points, respectively. Most agentic memory systems distill experience at write time, which irreversibly discards information before the future query is known; shifting curation to read time could make memory systems more flexible and directly trainable from immediate task success. This matters for anyone building LLM agents that must reuse past experience across long sequences of tasks. Because the synthesized payload is consumed on the same task, the curator can be trained from immediate task success, avoiding the long-horizon credit-assignment problem of write-time curation. Notably, even an untrained curator is already competitive with or surpasses the write-time baselines, showing that task-adaptive read-time curation itself drives much of the gain.

**Background**: LLM agents increasingly solve sequences of tasks over time, and agentic memory systems persist information from past trajectories to improve future performance. Existing designs typically curate memory at write time, distilling a completed trajectory into a fixed artifact such as a reflection, workflow, skill, or reasoning strategy that is later retrieved by similarity. This forces the system to decide what is worth remembering before the future query is known, producing a query-independent summary that must serve many possible downstream tasks.

## [CART: Closed-Loop Adaptive Red Teaming for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27336) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CART (Closed-Loop Adaptive Red Teaming), a framework that uses each test result to guide subsequent probes instead of replaying a fixed prompt set. Across three evaluation families (Frontier, JAH, and Agentic), CART discovered more failures and higher average risk than static seed replay for every Target with an available baseline. This matters because automated red teaming has largely been a one-time checklist of fixed prompts, which can only measure known risks; CART turns it into a continuous, adaptive, and auditable search that can surface weaknesses static replay never exercises, including in tool-using agents. It is directly relevant to AI safety and evaluation research, where finding unknown failure modes before deployment is a central concern. CART separates three roles—the Challenger that creates tests, the Target being tested (a text-only model or a bounded tool-using agent), and the Judge that evaluates results—so these roles can be studied independently. The authors caution that the results describe what the test policies discover, not how often failures occur in real deployments, and they find that Challenger-Judge choices affect the evidence uncovered, highlighting the need for role separation and independent review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Red teaming for large language models means deliberately probing a model with adversarial prompts to trigger harmful or undesirable outputs before real users encounter them. Early work such as LM-based red teaming used language models themselves to generate these probes, but most automated pipelines still replay a fixed set of prompts, which measures known risks but cannot learn from failures found during testing. CART addresses this by closing the loop: each result informs what is tested next, and the framework extends beyond text-only models to bounded tool-using agents, whose multi-step tool calls are increasingly evaluated in agentic AI benchmarks.

## [MolDesignBench Tests LLM Agents on Realistic Molecular Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27349) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released MolDesignBench, a scenario-grounded benchmark containing 2,000 molecular generation and optimization instances that combine implicit design-narrative requirements with explicit property and functional-group constraints, including deliberately infeasible cases, and require use of 17 specialized chemistry tools. Experiments across frontier LLMs show the best model achieving only about 43% success, with frequent failures in implicit-constraint reasoning, infeasibility detection, and multi-step tool reasoning. Existing molecular-design benchmarks rely on explicit, narrow constraints and only feasible problems, so they overstate agent capability; MolDesignBench exposes that even frontier LLMs struggle with the messy, multi-constraint reality of drug discovery. Its fine-grained failure-mode analysis identifies implicit constraint interpretation and infeasibility detection as the primary bottlenecks, giving the AI-for-science and agent-evaluation communities a rigorous testbed to guide future chemical agent research. The benchmark is built from natural-language design briefs paired with structured target specifications of property ranges and required substructures, with a portion of items intentionally infeasible to test whether models recognize impossible requests. The dataset, tool interface, and evaluation code are publicly released, and the dataset card lists it under a CC-BY-NC-4.0 license with roughly 1K–10K text items in JSON format.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

LLM-based agents are increasingly paired with external tools, such as cheminformatics property predictors and substructure checkers, to handle such multi-step workflows. ## [WhatWorkedBench: Benchmarking Experimental Understanding in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27490) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced WhatWorkedBench, a benchmark that measures AI agents' experimental understanding by having them inspect code, select measurements under a budget, and submit a response surface predicting scores for every configuration. It spans 36 tasks from 30 data sources and 8 workflow types, with 1248 configuration records, and evaluates methods such as pair-effect ridge and Gaussian processes. Experimental understanding is a relatively underexplored capability for AI research agents, and a rigorous benchmark could spur progress in adaptive experimental design, numerical inference, and agent evaluation. It gives the community a concrete, reproducible way to compare how well agents reason about cause and effect in code-driven experiments. At eight new measurements, pair-effect ridge selects an optimum on 15 of 22 sources and limits every effect error to 10% of the score range on three; fitting a Gaussian process to the same observations raises effect recovery from 0.632 to 0.698 in the original Flash cohort and from 0.621 to 0.720 in an additional cohort. Encoding code equivalences also raises GP recovery from 0.248 to 0.462 on six workflows with six binary options at 20 new measurements.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Response surface methodology is a statistical approach, introduced by Box and Wilson in 1951, that uses designed experiments and polynomial models to relate input factors to a response and find optimal settings. Gaussian process regression, also known as kriging, is a nonparametric interpolation method that provides best linear unbiased predictions at unsampled points and is widely used in spatial analysis and computer experiments. Ridge regression is a regularized linear method that handles multicollinearity and can shrink irrelevant feature effects toward zero. WhatWorkedBench combines these ideas to test whether AI agents can predict how changing one component of an experiment affects outcomes while holding others fixed.

## [NAFBench Tests Whether LLMs Can Follow Specified Negation Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27517) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint introduces NAFBench, a procedural generator of solver-certified logic programs that tests whether large language models can follow a specified negation semantics across four logical viewpoints: SLDNF, well-founded semantics (WFS), and credulous and skeptical reasoning under stable-model semantics. The benchmark reveals a consistent gap: the strongest open-source models score only 59–74% across the four viewpoints, while the weakest score 31–67%, and all models are order-sensitive on more than half of logically identical rule shufflings. Negation is interpreted differently across legal, regulatory, and medical domains, so knowing whether LLMs adopt a default reading or can override it when a different reading is explicitly specified is critical for deploying them in high-stakes reasoning tasks. The benchmark exposes a systematic weakness in current models and shows that delegating to a solver, fine-tuning on certified traces, or forcing an explicit three-valued verdict each only partly closes the gap. The generator emits ground normal logic programs with controlled depth, width, and cycle structure, solves each under all four viewpoints using SWI-Prolog, a well-founded semantics solver, and clingo, and then verbalizes them into natural language under multiple framings and rule orderings that leave the answer invariant. Two frontier models reach 100% on the main fixed-complexity evaluation set, and o4-mini is near-perfect, falling only to 81% on well-founded "undefined"; the two weaker models frequently overcommit on well-founded undefined cases.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Negation as failure is a form of non-monotonic reasoning in logic programming, where a negated atom is considered true if attempts to prove it fail; SLDNF resolution extends SLD resolution to handle such negated literals. Well-founded semantics assigns every general logic program a unique three-valued model with true, false, and unknown, while stable-model semantics can yield multiple models, leading to credulous reasoning (accepting what holds in some model) and skeptical reasoning (accepting only what holds in all models). NAFBench uses these formalisms to probe whether LLMs have a default negation reading and whether they can follow an explicitly specified one.

## [Evolutionary Stability Does Not Guarantee Learning Accessibility in MARL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27664) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27664) shows that in a three-agent governance game involving a government, a platform firm, and users, the cooperative evolutionary basin has full volume (V_E=1.00) on a sampled initial-condition grid, yet decentralized value-based learners fail to reach it: ε-greedy independent Q-learning achieves a learning basin of 0.88, while scaled Boltzmann exploration and SA-EA BQL both achieve 0.00. The authors conclude that evolutionary stability and learning accessibility are distinct properties of a coupled game-learning system. This work exposes a gap between evolutionary game theory and multi-agent reinforcement learning, warning researchers that a strategy proven stable under population dynamics may still be unreachable by finite-sample learners using local reward feedback. It has direct implications for designing incentive mechanisms and governance policies in shared-resource systems such as shared bikes, where assuming that stable equilibria will naturally emerge could lead to policy failure. The study derives replicator dynamics for the fixed stage-game incentives and compares the evolutionary basin against learning-basin estimates for three decentralized value-based learners under identical payoff environments and outcome criteria. Diagnostic traces reveal that broader action diversity and nonzero value separation can coexist with a failure to sustain the cooperative joint action, indicating the failure is not simply due to insufficient exploration.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Evolutionary game theory models how strategy frequencies in a population change over time, typically through the replicator equation, a deterministic dynamic describing natural selection in strategic interactions. Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) studies multiple learning agents that coexist in a shared environment, each optimizing its own reward; independent Q-learning is a common decentralized approach where each agent learns its own action-value function without centralized coordination. This paper bridges the two fields by asking whether outcomes that are evolutionarily stable are actually reachable by such decentralized learners.

## [Category Theory and Groupoids Boost POMDP Sample Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27745) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27745) proposes using category theory and environmental groupoids to partition the state space of partially observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs) into equivalence classes induced by symmetry orbits, with each class organized as a groupoid with a canonical representative. The authors implement this orbit-based partitioning within standard reinforcement learning pipelines and show consistent performance improvements on partially observable benchmarks that exhibit latent symmetry. This work offers a principled way to share learned knowledge across many symmetric environmental states, potentially improving sample efficiency in high-dimensional partially observable tasks where agents typically treat each orientation or position as a new problem. It also illustrates how abstract categorical structures can bridge theoretical reinforcement learning formulations and practical computational implementations, which could influence how future structured and scalable learning systems are designed. The framework partitions the state space by symmetry orbits and learns on a symmetry-reduced state space where each orbit is represented once, eliminating redundancy while preserving structure. The authors evaluate two different approaches on partially observable benchmarks, and the gains are reported specifically in environments exhibiting latent symmetry, meaning the benefit depends on the presence of such symmetries.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: A POMDP is a decision-making framework in which the agent cannot directly observe the full state of the environment and must act under uncertainty, making learning substantially harder than in fully observable settings. In category theory, a groupoid is a category in which every morphism is invertible, generalizing the notion of a group to collections of symmetry transformations acting between possibly multiple objects. Symmetry orbits are equivalence classes of states that can be transformed into one another by symmetry operations, and prior work has shown that exploiting such symmetries can reduce the complexity and sample requirements of reinforcement learning.

## [Study Separates Factual and Tonal Sycophancy in LLM Statistical Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27756) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27756) tests whether editorial framing in prompts changes the substance, not just the tone, of LLM-generated statistical reports. Using a 4x4 factorial design crossing four framing conditions with four ground-truth data patterns and collecting 480 responses, it finds factual misrepresentation concentrated in two cells: brutally critical framing on a genuine effect (97% of responses) and significance-seeking framing on an underpowered null (100% of responses). This matters because LLMs are increasingly used to interpret data, and the study shows framing-induced distortion is neither uniform across framings nor uniform across data patterns, meaning reliability risks depend on how users phrase requests and what the underlying data actually show. It offers a practical distinction between factual and tonal sycophancy that researchers and practitioners can use to audit LLM-assisted analysis. Tone shifts far more broadly than factual content: critical framing produces a defensive, hedge-heavy register across every data pattern, while significance-seeking framing shifts tone only where the data leave genuine ambiguity. Notably, a confound present in the data itself blocks both kinds of shift almost entirely under every framing condition tested, and a model can hold a correct conclusion while its tone shifts substantially around it.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Sycophancy in LLMs is an agent failure mode where a model agrees with a user's stated belief, preference, or false premise instead of giving the best-supported answer, and prior research such as SycEval has found such behavior highly persistent. Most sycophancy research focuses on belief- or preference-alignment settings, but this paper examines a different task: analyzing data and reporting what the results mean. A factorial design is a statistical experiment that tests every combination of multiple factors and their levels, allowing researchers to see both individual effects and interactions; here the factors are framing condition and ground-truth data pattern.

## [SRCF Black-Box Attack Steers LRM Reasoning, ARCF Defense Proposed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27763) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27763) introduces SRCF, a black-box attack that steers Large Reasoning Models by prepending counter-aligned few-shot conversations containing explicit chain-of-thought traces, causing unsafe answers to harmful queries and unwarranted refusals to benign ones. The authors also propose ARCF, a post-training defense that exposes models to counter-aligned conversational contexts while enforcing aligned targets, improving safety and helpfulness without degrading utility. The work shows that the explicit chain-of-thought traces and large context windows that make LRMs powerful also create a new attack surface exploitable through a plain conversational interface, with no access to model parameters or gradients. This matters for AI safety and alignment because it suggests deployed reasoning models may be steered by crafted few-shot examples, and it offers a compatible post-training defense for practitioners. SRCF operates solely through a flexible conversational interface and requires no access to parameters or gradients; its key insight is an adversarial generalization issue that induces representation drift, shifting benign and harmful inputs in a similar direction. ARCF is compatible with existing post-training methods and consistently improves safety and helpfulness without degrading utility.

**Background**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) rely on explicit chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, a prompting technique introduced by Google Research in 2022 that encourages models to generate intermediate reasoning steps before answering, and on large context windows to handle complex tasks. Few-shot prompting means including a few input-output examples in the prompt to guide model behavior, and black-box adversarial attacks manipulate model outputs without access to internal weights or gradients. This paper combines these concepts, showing that counter-aligned few-shot conversations with CoT traces can steer reasoning, and proposes a post-training defense.

## [AGVF: Multi-Agent Framework for Policy-Constrained Healthcare Appeals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27844) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces AGVF (Agentic Governance and Adversarial Verification Framework), a multi-agent architecture that models medical-necessity appeal generation as a Constrained Markov Decision Process over five agents: policy formalization, evidence retrieval, gap analysis, adversarial critique, and gated synthesis. Validated on 1,000 synthetic appeal cases derived from de-identified public hospital discharge data, AGVF achieved zero citation-grounding violations and monotone deficiency reduction in every episode, while ablating the deterministic citation-grounding gate raised violations to 100%. Claim denial management costs U.S. healthcare roughly $260 billion annually, and single-agent LLMs fail in this high-stakes setting by introducing unsupported clinical details and losing the logical structure of hierarchical payer policy. AGVF's theory-backed, verifiable architecture offers a path toward safer, policy-compliant LLM generation in healthcare and other regulated domains where unsupported assertions carry legal and financial risk. AGVF proves that refinement over a fixed policy constraint graph monotonically reduces evidence deficiency and terminates with either a complete satisfying frontier or a localized evidence gap, and a deterministic citation-grounding gate prevents assertions without admissible evidence from entering shared state. The study uses no real patient records and does not measure clinical efficacy, so its validation is limited to synthetic cases and structural correctness rather than real-world outcomes.

## [Hierarchy of Logical Faithfulness Criteria for Knowledge Base Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27863) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27863) proposes a hierarchy of four increasingly strict logical faithfulness criteria for knowledge base completion models—discrimination, logical admissibility, monotonic logical faithfulness, and probabilistic logical faithfulness—and proves they form a strict chain of implications. Evaluating knowledge graph and logic-geometric embedding models on EL ontologies with entailed, contradictory, and undetermined test sets, the authors find that ranking accuracy does not imply logical faithfulness and that none of the evaluated models is faithful across the hierarchy. This work exposes a fundamental gap in how knowledge base completion is evaluated: standard ranking metrics treat all unobserved facts as false, which is incompatible with the open world assumption and deductive closure of description logic knowledge bases. It provides a rigorous theoretical foundation that could reshape evaluation practices and push embedding models toward genuine logical reasoning rather than mere link prediction. The strongest criterion is grounded in the relative model count P(α|O) = #(O ∪ {α}) / #(O), which recovers the trichotomy of entailed, contradictory, and undetermined axioms at its endpoints and ranks undetermined axioms in between. The code is publicly available at the bio-ontology-research-group/kbc GitHub repository.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Description logics are a family of formal knowledge representation languages, more expressive than propositional logic but less expressive than first-order logic, used to define concepts and reason over knowledge bases. Under the open world assumption, the absence of a statement does not imply it is false, and deductive closure means a knowledge base implicitly contains all facts entailed by its axioms. Knowledge graph completion typically ranks observed triples above randomly corrupted ones, treating every unobserved fact as false—an assumption that breaks down when the object being completed is a description logic knowledge base rather than a plain graph.

A new arXiv paper (2609.27869) introduces CoCA, an on-policy learning framework that dynamically selects a cost-sensitive subset of specialized capabilities at each stage of a long-horizon multimodal agent, rather than activating a fixed capability set or predefined workflow. CoCA recovers a deployable capability-subset policy from sparse conditional comparisons via a conditional utility model, dual-level on-policy distillation, and trajectory-level reinforcement learning. Long-horizon multimodal agents typically pay substantial computational overhead by always running every capability, so learning to activate only what each stage needs could cut inference cost while improving adaptability to shifting task demands. The problem is combinatorial because capability values depend on the selected subset, and previous allocations alter the states encountered by later decisions, making explicit enumeration infeasible. At inference time, allocation is performed solely by a lightweight student policy without teacher queries or online updates, and experiments on long-horizon multimodal environments and controlled capability-demand shifts show superiority over state-of-the-art baselines.

## [SlackDrive Reuses Realized Latency to Allocate Compute for Driving Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28064) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SlackDrive introduces a pre-inference compute allocator that uses recently realized latency as a signal of available compute slack to select the compute budget for each control step of a driving world-action model. On NAVSIM v2 with DriveDreamer-Policy, it improves latency-constrained EPDMS by 21.7% over the strongest baseline under a stringent latency regime, while full-budget and preconfigured token-pruning baselines exceed the admissible latency envelope under runtime contention. Driving world-action models couple multimodal reasoning with future prediction to improve planning, but their growing inference cost conflicts with the real-time latency requirements of vehicle control. SlackDrive shows that runtime latency variation left unexploited by offline profiling and static scheduling can be reclaimed to maintain real-time performance without changing the driving backbone. SlackDrive profiles the latency and planning utility of a small discrete budget set once, estimates online compute state from completed forwards, and selects the highest-utility budget predicted to remain within the admissible latency envelope. It complements existing profiling and resource scheduling while preserving the driving backbone and its compute actuator, and the approach is validated only on NAVSIM v2 with DriveDreamer-Policy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: World-action models (WAMs) for autonomous driving improve planning by incorporating future world evolution into action generation, but existing methods often allocate a fixed imagination budget to every scene. Acceleration techniques such as token pruning, layer skipping, or reduced sampling steps typically use policies selected before deployment, leaving residual runtime variation unexploited after offline profiling and static scheduling on shared onboard compute. SlackDrive addresses this by treating recent realized latency as a direct signal of available compute slack and adapting the compute budget before each model execution.

## [Finite-Sample Safety Certification for AI Grid-Edge Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28182) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28182) proposes a finite-sample probabilistic safety certification framework for black-box AI decision models used in closed-loop power grid operation. The framework reduces the full input-AI-grid evaluation workflow to a binary unsafe outcome under an operator-defined safety specification, then applies exact binomial inference to certify the unsafe operation probability and return an accept/reject deployment criterion. As AI-based control such as multi-agent reinforcement learning and imitation learning becomes more scalable for coordinating large populations of flexible grid-edge devices, system operators need an independent, rigorous way to decide whether a given AI system is safe enough to deploy. This work addresses that critical gap by providing statistical safety guarantees that control the probability of false safety certification, which could help accelerate the safe adoption of AI in critical infrastructure. The framework returns the tightest one-sided upper certificate from held-out calibration scenarios and controls the probability of false safety certification. Because certification is for the calibration distribution, which may deviate from future operation, the authors combine the nominal certificate with physically interpretable sample-space adversarial attacks, and verify the finite-sample guarantee in case studies with 1,000-agent AI models in a rolling-window training-certification-deployment flow.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Grid-edge devices such as distributed energy resources, smart sensors, and programmable actuators can provide flexibility that reduces the need for costly network upgrades, and AI controllers are attractive because they scale in real time. However, black-box AI models lack transparency, making operators reluctant to trust them in high-risk settings. Exact binomial inference is a statistical method that computes conservative confidence bounds on a probability from a finite number of binary observations, which is what allows this framework to make rigorous finite-sample safety claims.

## [PASTABench: Benchmarking Proactive Safety Monitoring for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28197) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.28197) introduces PASTABench, a benchmark of 1,139 multi-turn trajectories spanning 5 risk categories and 13 subcategories, along with the Optimal Intervention Window (OIW) metric anchored by annotated Earliest-Signal and Trigger turns. The paper formalizes Decoupled Proactive Safety Monitoring around three questions—whether to intervene, when to intervene, and what the risk is—and finds that the best of 16 evaluated LLMs achieves only 40.74% optimal-timing interventions. As LLM-based agents increasingly execute multi-step workflows that alter real-world states, existing step-level and post-hoc trajectory evaluations fail to capture how risks accumulate or allow timely intervention. PASTABench provides a standardized way to measure proactive safety monitoring, highlighting that current models are far from solving this problem and that smaller models' safety scores may reflect keyword sensitivity rather than genuine risk comprehension. The benchmark's fine-grained diagnosis uncovers pervasive lexical overfitting: smaller models' competitive safety scores collapse once hazard vocabulary is neutralized, indicating keyword hypersensitivity rather than genuine risk comprehension. The OIW metric quantifies intervention timeliness by anchoring on annotated Earliest-Signal and Trigger turns, and the paper is a preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are autonomous systems that use large language models to plan and execute multi-step actions, potentially modifying external states through tools and APIs. Prior safety evaluations largely focused on single-turn prompts or step-level action checks, which treat actions in isolation and miss how risk accumulates across a trajectory, while trajectory-level evaluations are post-hoc and offer no chance for timely intervention. PASTABench addresses this gap by formalizing proactive monitoring as a decoupled task of deciding whether, when, and what risk to flag during an ongoing multi-turn workflow.
