## [Auction-Based Platform Dynamically Prices LLM Inference Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28322) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28322) designs a procurement platform that prices LLM inference tokens through a reverse second-price auction, where providers bid their true average serving cost and the platform learns each provider's quality over time. Experiments with Llama and Qwen models on math reasoning and QA benchmarks show the most cost-competitive provider's pricing margin ranges from 10% to 71% depending on task and quality threshold. The findings suggest substantial inefficiency in today's fixed-price-per-token LLM market, and the platform could let users capture maximum savings whenever competitive market conditions allow. This matters for anyone building or buying LLM inference at scale, from routing platforms to enterprises managing inference costs. The reverse second-price auction incentivizes providers to truthfully bid their best estimate of the average cost to serve a query, and the platform progressively routes queries to the most cost-competitive provider among those meeting a desired quality threshold. The reported 10%–71% margin variation is empirical, based on multiple Llama and Qwen models on popular mathematical reasoning and question-answering benchmarks, and the paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: LLM benchmarking and routing platforms increasingly act as intermediaries connecting model providers with end-users, but providers typically charge a fixed price per token, which prevents users from getting the most competitive price for their specific tasks. A reverse second-price auction is a mechanism where the lowest bidder wins but is paid the second-lowest price, encouraging honest bidding about true costs. Quality-aware routing, meanwhile, selects among models based on both cost and the quality they deliver for a given task.

## [Attention Head Bounds for Multi-Task Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04243) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.04243) proves that computing both the minimum and maximum of a list with a single attention head requires exponentially higher embedding dimension or bit precision than using two heads, and shows that n-bit XOR requires the product of head count and polynomial degree to be at least n. These results give theoretical grounding to the empirical practice of using multiple attention heads in transformers, suggesting that multi-head designs are not merely convenient but fundamentally necessary for efficient multi-task representation. The paper constructs multi-head attention layers that match the lower bound for n-bit XOR, and generalizes the results to arbitrary symmetric Boolean functions with bounds expressed in terms of threshold degree.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-head attention is the core mechanism in transformer models, where several parallel attention heads each learn different relationships in the input. A polynomial threshold function is a Boolean function defined by the sign of a polynomial, and the XOR problem is a classic example of a function that a single linear layer cannot compute. This paper studies how many heads and how much precision are needed to represent such tasks efficiently.

## [Gödel's and Scott's Ontological Argument Ported to Lean 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26806) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper presents a complete, structure-preserving port of the Isabelle/HOL formalization of Gödel's and Scott's ontological arguments to Lean 4, comprising 30 modules that retain the original section structure, declaration order, and names. A comparison tool certifies all 548 statements as identical, and every result previously proved in Isabelle/HOL is reproved, including the inconsistency of Gödel's 1970 axioms, modal collapse, monotheism, and the ultrafilter property of positive properties. This work demonstrates rigorous cross-prover portability between two major interactive theorem provers, showing that a substantial formalized philosophical dataset can be faithfully reproduced in Lean 4. It advances the practical use of Lean 4 for formal philosophy and mathematics, and its explicit reporting of axiom dependencies offers a reusable methodology for tracking which modal-logic assumptions each result requires. Because Lean 4 lacks a sledgehammer and a model finder, the original one-line automated proofs become explicit proof terms and the 72 nitpick invocations are recorded as documentation; the 45 remaining unproved statements are exactly those the original refutes by nitpick (35) or leaves open (10), and they are anonymous sorrys on which nothing depends. The #print axioms command reports the postulates each proof consumes, showing that Scott's necessary-existence theorem and modal collapse need only symmetry of the accessibility relation (logic KB), while the essence and monotheism lemmas and the possible existence of a God-like being need no frame condition, and the development depends on no library beyond Lean 4's core.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Gödel's ontological argument is a formal reconstruction of the classical proof for God's existence, written in modal logic and never published by Gödel himself; a version was later transcribed and circulated by Dana Scott. Isabelle/HOL is a higher-order-logic theorem prover widely used for formal verification, and Lean 4 is a modern interactive theorem prover with dependent type theory and a powerful tactic framework. Porting a formalization between such systems means re-checking every definition and proof in a different logical foundation, which is why certifying statement-by-statement identity is a meaningful technical achievement.

## [Stiffness-Conditioned Neural Operator Predicts Fluid-Structure Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26816) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.26816) introduces a stiffness-conditioned neural evolution operator that jointly predicts the Eulerian flow field and Lagrangian structural state of a flexible plate undergoing flow-induced vibration. Using a hybrid CNN-Transformer architecture with bidirectional cross-attention, a single operator captures three stiffness-dependent regimes—deflected-flapping, deflected, and flapping—and remains bounded over blind 1000-step rollouts while interpolating to stiffness values excluded from training. Accurate long-term FSI prediction is notoriously expensive with conventional CFD solvers, so a differentiable surrogate that generalizes across stiffness regimes could enable fast parameter studies and stiffness optimization for flow-energy-harvesting devices. It also demonstrates how neural operators can move beyond single-regime benchmarks toward multi-regime, coupled multiphysics prediction. The plate is encoded as 101 ordered structural tokens carrying nodal coordinates and velocities, with nondimensional bending stiffness as a global conditioning variable, and training uses staged multi-step autoregressive rollouts plus symmetry-reflected trajectories. To reduce sensitivity to under-resolved near-wall gradients, the authors replace conventional wall-stress surface integrals with a differentiable aerodynamic-force module based on the derivative-moment transformation (DMT), using an enclosed 2D curve integral around the core vortex region with a Signed Distance Function and smoothed Dirac-delta formulation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Fluid-structure interaction (FSI) describes how a deformable or movable solid exchanges momentum with a surrounding or internal flow, and such interactions can be stable or oscillatory, as in flow-induced vibration. Neural operators are machine-learning models that learn mappings between function spaces, serving as fast surrogate solvers for the partial differential equations that govern such physics. This work combines both ideas, conditioning a neural operator on structural stiffness so that one model covers several qualitatively different vibration responses.

## [Signal2Symbol: Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Explainable ECG/EEG Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26820) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Signal2Symbol is a neuro-symbolic framework that converts ECG and EEG signals into symbolic sequences using either a learned VQ-VAE codebook or a SAX baseline, then scores anomalies via minimal rare itemset mining on bigram-enriched token-window transactions. Detected anomalous windows are merged into intervals and related using Allen interval algebra, and a rare temporal concept lattice based on Formal Concept Analysis groups them into interpretable families, evaluated on MIT-BIH, PTB-XL, and the Bonn EEG dataset under noise and baseline-wander perturbations. Deep learning models for biosignal anomaly detection often achieve high accuracy but offer little insight into why a segment is anomalous or how local anomalies relate over time, which limits clinical trust and adoption. By producing symbolic, human-readable explanations such as escalation chains, artifact overlap, and cross-channel synchrony, Signal2Symbol addresses the transparency gap in healthcare AI and could support more accountable clinical decision-making. The framework combines a learned VQ-VAE codebook with a SAX baseline for tokenization, uses minimal rare itemset mining (as implemented by algorithms such as AprioriRare) as the anomaly evidence, and applies Allen interval algebra plus a Galois lattice from FCA to compress many local detections into compact interpretable families. Evaluation covers beat-level MIT-BIH Arrhythmia, record-level PTB-XL, and segment-level Bonn EEG, with robustness stress tests under additive noise and baseline wander.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Physiological time series such as ECG and EEG have complex temporal structure and high acquisition variability, making anomaly detection both important and difficult. VQ-VAE is a generative model that replaces a continuous latent space with a discrete learned codebook, while SAX discretizes time series into symbolic strings; both turn continuous signals into discrete tokens. Rare itemset mining finds infrequent but meaningful co-occurring patterns, and Allen interval algebra plus Formal Concept Analysis provide formal tools for relating and grouping temporal intervals into interpretable structures.

## [LM-CXD Bridges LLM Serving and CXL-SSDs for Chunk-Aware KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26828) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present LM-CXD, a CXL-SSD specialized for LLM prefix caching that makes KV cache chunks device-visible I/O units, exposes NAND-to-DRAM progress to the serving engine, and uses device DRAM as a GPU-accessible buffer. Across five LLM models, LM-CXD reduces average TTFT over a stock CXL-SSD by up to 2.6x with compute-asynchronous prefetching and 4.03x with layerwise prefetching, achieving TTFT within 1.5x of local DRAM on average. Prefix caching is a key technique for reducing LLM inference latency and cost, but scaling it to long contexts requires storage capacity beyond GPU and host DRAM. This work shows that co-designing CXL-SSDs with the serving engine's knowledge of KV chunk consumption can make NAND-backed capacity a practical tier for prefix caching, which could lower infrastructure costs for high-throughput LLM serving. The paper characterizes that NAND-backed storage incurs CPU cache contention and host-DRAM staging costs even when DRAM is the storage medium, and that a stock CXL-SSD remains about 3x slower than local DRAM and no faster than an NVMe SSD, with generic prefetching providing little benefit. LM-CXD addresses this by coordinating request scheduling with windowed prefetching and pipelining layerwise KV movement with GPU computation to hide NAND latency under limited device DRAM.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: CXL (Compute Express Link) is a cache-coherent interconnect that allows byte-addressable access to memory and storage devices, and a CXL-SSD is a solid-state drive that exposes NAND-backed capacity through CXL rather than the block-based NVMe path. In LLM serving, the KV cache stores attention keys and values for previously processed tokens so that prefix caching can reuse them and avoid redundant computation, but long-context requests can overwhelm GPU and host memory. This paper explores using CXL-SSDs as a capacity tier for KV cache while co-designing the device with the serving engine's chunk-level knowledge.

## [Validation and Simulation Catch Different Errors in LLM-Generated Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26830) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint defines four non-nested evaluation levels — schema validity, topological validity, backend executability, and component-set agreement — for LLM-generated circuits, tested on a 150-circuit trilingual benchmark. On gpt-4o-mini, 16 of 150 circuits (10.7%, 95% CI 6.7–16.6) were rejected by the topological validator yet executed in ngspice with no error or warning, while 7 circuits (4.7%) passed validation but were refused by ngspice. The finding that topological validation and SPICE simulation detect distinct error classes means that reporting simulation success alone can hide structural defects in AI-generated hardware designs. This has direct implications for AI-assisted EDA workflows, where engineers may otherwise trust a circuit simply because it runs. The paper's minimal three-component divider example shows the cost of relying on simulation alone: a dangling resistor reports 5.00 V instead of the expected 2.50 V while ngspice stays silent. A paired ablation on a stratified 45-circuit subsample showed model repair raised topological validity from 40.0% to 84.4% (+20 circuits, no regressions) but moved executability by only a net 6 (+7, −1), an effect the sample size cannot resolve.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: SPICE (Simulation Program with Integrated Circuit Emphasis) is the standard family of circuit simulators, and ngspice is its open-source successor used to check whether a netlist actually runs. A netlist is the textual description of components and their connections, and topological validation checks that this graph is structurally sound — for example, that no terminal is left dangling — independently of whether simulation succeeds. LLMs are increasingly used to generate such netlists, but prior evaluations often treat a successful simulation as proof of correctness.

## [QUARTET: New Graph Transformer Boosts Relational Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26855) ⭐️ 7.0/10

QUARTET is a new graph transformer architecture that improves on the state-of-the-art RelGT model for Relational Deep Learning (RDL) by introducing a Causal Random Walk (CRW) sampler based on recency-truncated Personalized PageRank (PPR) and a quad-branch cross-attention module. On RelBench v1 classification tasks, QUARTET matches or outperforms existing baselines (HGT and RelGT), with ablations confirming that the CRW sampler enriches local subgraphs and the global branches provide task-specific gains. Relational Deep Learning is a rapidly growing approach for building predictive models directly on multi-table databases without manual feature engineering, and graph transformers are its leading architecture. QUARTET's improvements to local subgraph sampling and global context modeling could make RDL more accurate and robust, benefiting applications such as recommendation, fraud detection, and healthcare analytics that rely on relational data. The CRW sampler uses recency-truncated Personalized PageRank to extract compact, hub-robust, densely connected local subgraphs without temporal leakage, addressing RelGT's loosely connected subgraphs. The quad-branch cross-attention integrates global context from four perspectives: seed feature, seed topology, temporal dynamics, and collaborative dynamics; however, the work is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Relational Deep Learning (RDL) represents a multi-table relational database as a heterogeneous temporal graph, where each row becomes a node and primary-foreign key links become edges, allowing end-to-end learning without manual feature engineering. RelGT is the first graph transformer designed specifically for such relational data and currently sets state-of-the-art results on RelBench, a benchmark suite for RDL. Personalized PageRank (PPR) is a classic graph proximity measure that scores nodes by the probability that a random walk from a source node terminates at them, and it is widely used for sampling relevant neighborhoods.

Researchers propose FLINT, a lightweight traversability estimator with a 21.6M-parameter backbone that is 38 times smaller than a comparable foundation-model backbone, yet scores higher on held-out terrain probes and runs at 14.7 FPS on CPU alone using only an RGB camera. In closed-loop field trials, its best self-supervised head achieved 99% autonomy over the route, outperforming a human-label-trained baseline deployed live on the same course. This challenges the prevailing trend of using heavy multi-sensor rigs and large foundation-model backbones for off-road navigation, suggesting that cheap, CPU-only perception may be sufficient for reliable traversability estimation. If it generalizes, it could lower the cost and power barriers for deploying autonomous ground robots in unstructured environments. FLINT produces a cheaper and more accurate costmap than a deployed foundation-model system (WildOS) on 23 of 24 replayed field logs, and the paper compares different self-supervised learning signals rather than relying on hand-labeled data. The key caveat is that these results come from a specialized arXiv preprint with no community discussion or independent replication yet.

Because such labels cannot be hand-annotated at scale, modern platforms typically rely on multiple sensors such as RGBD cameras, lidar, radar, and IMU paired with computationally intensive neural network inference. A costmap is the grid-based representation of terrain costs that a navigation planner uses to choose safe paths, so producing an accurate costmap cheaply is central to autonomous navigation.

Researchers introduced Safety Nudges, a browser-based tool that provides lightweight, in-situ flags when concerning behaviors such as hallucination, sycophancy, overconfidence, or anthropomorphism are detected in chatbot conversations. In a two-week field study with 45 frequent chatbot users, nearly all participants reported increased awareness of potential AI harms, but this awareness did not necessarily translate into discernible behavioral changes. However, the finding that awareness alone does not change behavior highlights a key challenge for designing effective AI safety interventions. The authors emphasize that relevance, calibration, and user control are important design considerations for nudges in conversational AI safety.

## [Ajar Measures Open Privilege in Agent Defenses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26900) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Ajar, a method that measures 'open privilege' in agent defenses by attaching to existing agent-security benchmarks and reusing their tasks, tool schemas, reference solutions and goal states. Evaluated on AgentDojo across five defenses (Progent, CaMeL, AC4A, Permission Assistant, and Claude Code's Auto mode), Ajar reveals that defenses leave widely different amounts of unnecessary privilege open, and that this open privilege cannot be derived from attack success or benign utility scores. Current agent-security benchmarks only judge defenses by how much they reduce successful indirect prompt injection attacks while preserving task utility, so a defense can score well on both metrics yet still permit transfers, deletions or broad reads that no task required. Ajar adds open privilege as a third evaluation axis, giving researchers and practitioners a more complete picture of the real security posture of agent defenses. Ajar builds candidate tool calls that a benign task does not need and presents them to the defense at every point where the agent could act, so allowing any such call counts as privilege left open. The evaluation found that two defenses leak by almost the same amount yet differ widely in the benign tasks they finish, and one defense achieves part of its tightness by refusing calls its tasks were entitled to make; the source code is available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Language model agents act through tools, and the data they read while working can redirect how those tools are used, a risk known as indirect prompt injection. To mitigate this, many defenses sit between the agent and its tools to enforce access control, information flow or isolation. Existing benchmarks such as AgentDojo evaluate these defenses mainly on attack success rate and benign utility, but they do not directly measure whether a defense leaves unnecessary permissions open.

## [COMED Adds Selective Escalation Between Multi-LLM Routing and Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26913) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.26913) introduces COMED (Controlled Model Escalation for Multi-LLM Deliberation), a post-anchor controller that uses anchor self-consistency, router margin, and a lightweight peer probe to decide when to escalate a query to peer models. Across medical, scientific, and general reasoning benchmarks, COMED improved fixed and routed anchors in all 16 open-weight settings, with gains up to +10.7 percentage points on MedQA, and raised GPT-5.5 on HLE from 23.1% to 28.1%. The paper identifies a practical gap in multi-LLM systems: routing stops after picking one model, while dense collaboration invokes peers on every query, wasting compute and sometimes corrupting correct answers. COMED's selective escalation could let production systems capture cross-model rescue benefits while spending fewer models and decoded tokens, which matters for cost-sensitive and latency-sensitive AI deployments. COMED formalizes the trade-off with a rescue-harm decomposition, showing that selective collaboration helps when rescued errors outweigh collaboration-induced harms. It accepts confident answers, verifies ambiguous cases, and escalates only when collaboration is likely beneficial, invoking fewer models and using fewer decoded tokens than dense collaboration.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-LLM inference systems typically either route each query to one model or combine outputs from several models. Routing is cheap but stops after the initial selection, while dense collaboration is expensive because it invokes peers on every query. Self-consistency is a common technique where a model samples multiple answers and picks the most common one, and router margin refers to how confidently a router prefers one model over another.

## [Hindsight Relabeling Often Harms Multi-Objective RL, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26918) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.26918) shows that extending hindsight relabeling to preference-conditioned multi-objective reinforcement learning degrades performance in 19 of 36 algorithm-environment settings, by as much as four standard deviations, while improving only one. The authors identify a failure mode called Preference Coverage Collapse and propose her_mix, a single-parameter convex combination that returns 16 of the 19 harmed settings to baseline and cuts abandoned preference mass from 69% to 6%. This counterintuitive result challenges a widely assumed transfer of a standard single-objective RL technique to multi-objective settings, warning MORL researchers and practitioners that naive hindsight relabeling can silently hurt performance. The proposed her_mix fix and the APM metric offer concrete tools that could reshape how preference-conditioned MORL algorithms are designed and evaluated. The study spans four preference-conditioned off-policy algorithms, two critic backbones, and two preference-sampling schemes on the continuous-control MO-Gymnasium suite; the harm is not caused by noisy relabels, since denoising recovers almost nothing, and neither prioritized sampling nor buffer-structure choices reproduce it. The authors quantify the collapse with abandoned preference mass (APM), a value-aware statistic that correlates with the harm at ρ = -0.73, whereas a purely structural coverage count does not.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Hindsight relabeling is a technique in reinforcement learning that retroactively replaces a transition's original goal with the outcome the agent actually achieved, which improves sample efficiency in single-objective RL. In preference-conditioned multi-objective RL, an agent is trained to produce policies that trade off conflicting objectives according to a preference vector, and the natural extension is to relabel transitions with the preference direction actually achieved rather than the one requested. MO-Gymnasium is a standard open-source benchmark library providing multi-objective environments with vectorized rewards for comparing such algorithms.

## [RCC-Align Uses Histopathology-CT Contrastive Learning for Kidney Cancer Grading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26920) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers developed RCC-Align, a cross-modal contrastive learning framework that aligns paired whole-slide histopathology images and CT scans to improve noninvasive clear cell renal cell carcinoma (ccRCC) grade prediction. Trained on paired TCGA and CPTAC cohorts, it achieved an AUC of 0.601 and AUPRC of 0.599, outperforming CT-only DINOv2-Finetuned baselines (AUC 0.545) with significantly better low-grade prediction (p = 0.004). This approach could enable noninvasive ccRCC grading when biopsy is unsafe or infeasible, and it demonstrates that pathology-guided contrastive learning can transfer microscopic grade information to macroscopic CT representations. It is relevant to researchers in computational pathology, radiology, and multimodal medical AI. RCC-Align requires only CT at inference, and cross-modal alignment was assessed via cosine similarity, showing stronger paired WSI-CT embedding alignment than baselines. However, its AUC of 0.601 remains below the WSI-based GigaPath reference (AUC 0.719), and the authors note that validation in larger, multi-institutional cohorts with external testing is needed before clinical translation.

**Background**: Clear cell renal cell carcinoma (ccRCC) is the most common kidney cancer and shows substantial clinical heterogeneity, so accurate grading is essential for risk stratification and treatment planning. Conventional grading relies on invasive tissue sampling and microscopic examination of whole-slide histopathology images (WSIs), which can be limited by intratumoral heterogeneity. Cross-modal contrastive learning is a self-supervised technique that aligns different data modalities in a shared latent space, and here it is used to transfer grade-discriminative information from pathology to CT.

## [Cross-Model LLM Disagreement Guides Expert Codebook Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26926) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.26926) compares three ways experts can feed back into LLM codebook revision: Codebook Verifying, Question Answering, and Rationale Labeling. On thousands of tutoring-session transcripts, Rationale Labeling of cross-model disagreement cases achieved the highest LLM-labeling accuracy at 64.9%, beating the expert-revised codebook baseline of 57.8%, with the best Question Answering setting also surpassing it at 60.5%. Codebook development traditionally takes months, and this work shows that LLMs can strategically target scarce expert attention at the cases where models disagree, potentially compressing that timeline to days without sacrificing labeling quality. It offers a practical human-AI collaboration recipe for large-scale annotation pipelines used in research and industry. The study evaluated three feedback protocols on thousands of tutoring-session transcripts, measuring LLM-labeling accuracy against expert labels; Rationale Labeling reached 64.9%, the best Question Answering setting 60.5%, and the expert-revised codebook 57.8%. The approach relies on surfacing cases with strong cross-LLM disagreement as the signal for where expert effort is most valuable.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: In large-scale text annotation, a codebook is a set of labeling instructions that AI annotators follow, and building a robust one is slow because it requires expert judgment on ambiguous cases. Cross-model disagreement means different LLMs produce different labels for the same input, which often signals genuine ambiguity or edge cases. Rationale labeling asks experts to not only assign a label but also explain why, giving the model richer supervision than a bare label.

## [LLMs Recognize Kinship Terms Far Better Than They Produce Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26942) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.26942) benchmarks five open-weight LLMs on kinship terms in Hindi, Tamil, and Korean, comparing a generation task against a matched multiple-choice selection baseline. GPT-OSS-120B selected the correct term in 90.67% of 75 valid cells but produced an accepted term in only 36.00% of corresponding attempts, while Llama-3.3-70B showed the same pattern (77.92% vs. 24.24%). The results expose an evaluation format gap: high scores on multiple-choice kinship benchmarks may overstate a model's real ability to produce culturally specific terms, which matters for multilingual NLP evaluation and for applications such as translation, dialogue, and cultural preservation. The authors argue that generation-based evaluation should accompany multiple-choice testing. On explicitly specified L3 prompts, accuracy ranged sharply from GLM-5.1 at 72.29% to Llama-3.3-70B at 24.24%, and the paternal-lineage advantage was language-specific—large in Hindi but weak or reversed in Korean. Because the four-option condition displays candidate terms and does not require script production, the authors interpret the gap as an evaluation format effect rather than proof that lexical knowledge is intact.

**Background**: Kinship terms are culturally specific words for family relationships, and many languages encode distinctions (such as paternal versus maternal relatives) that do not map cleanly onto English. Prior multilingual benchmarks typically test these terms through multiple-choice questions, which only require recognizing a correct option rather than recalling and writing the term in the target script. This paper instead asks models to generate kinship terms directly in Hindi, Tamil, and Korean, using a matched selection baseline to isolate the effect of the evaluation format.

Researchers introduced PolyBench26, an open benchmark containing nearly 250,000 polymer-property datapoints across eight physical properties drawn from experimental measurements, density functional theory, and molecular dynamics. It defines four evaluation tasks spanning homopolymers and alternating, random, and block copolymers, and compares language model, graph-based, and descriptor-based approaches, finding graph-based models achieve the lowest prediction errors. Polymer property prediction has lacked open, standardized benchmarks, with existing resources covering only narrow architectures such as homopolymers, which makes rigorous comparison of machine learning methods difficult. PolyBench26 provides a reproducible foundation that could accelerate and standardize research in polymer informatics and computational materials design. The benchmark supports four tasks: in-distribution property prediction, dataset-size scaling, repeat-unit complexity, and transfer to held-out polymer architectures. Graph-based models not only gave the lowest errors but also retained their advantage across training-set sizes and remained robust as repeat-unit complexity increased; the code and data are open-sourced at https://github.com/rlearsch/PolymerBenchmark2026.

EMA claims performance transparency for both borrowers, via prefetching that hides remote access costs, and lenders, whose borrowed resources remain reclaimable on demand. Multi-GPU servers running LLM inference often suffer from one GPU exhausting local memory while others sit idle, so an elastic pool could raise utilization and throughput without over-provisioning hardware. If validated, EMA's approach could influence how data centers schedule memory for dynamic AI workloads and extend to other shared GPU resources. Evaluation reports up to 52% higher individual user throughput, 96% of the throughput of a system provisioned with 2X capacity, and latency similar to a static local baseline. The paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review, and its design focuses on memory while noting the principle could extend to other GPU resources.

## [Non-Gaussian Priors Fail to Improve Fine-Tuning of Large Behavior Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27070) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27070) reports that replacing the standard Gaussian prior with non-Gaussian priors does not improve fine-tuning of pretrained Large Behavior Models (LBMs) such as LBM 1.0, π0.5, and GR00T N1.5, despite these priors being demonstrably closer to the target action distribution. The finding is supported by over 100K simulation rollouts across 40+ tasks in two simulation platforms and 1250 hardware rollouts on five bimanual manipulation tasks, showing statistically indistinguishable or worse performance compared to a Gaussian prior. This negative result challenges a natural assumption in robot imitation learning—that better priors should transfer from scratch training to fine-tuning—and could save the robotics community significant research effort by redirecting focus toward more impactful factors like encoder training. It provides practical guidance for practitioners fine-tuning large pretrained policies, suggesting that prior choice is not a worthwhile lever for improvement. Diagnostic analyses reveal that fine-tuned policies converge to similar action predictions across different priors, even though their fine-tuned encoder embeddings diverge substantially from the pretrained embeddings and from each other. A learning-rate ablation further confirms that encoder training is the dominant factor in fine-tuning performance, substantially outweighing the effect of prior choice.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Large Behavior Models (LBMs) are robot policies trained on large, heterogeneous datasets, analogous to Large Language Models but for robotic behaviors. Modern imitation learning often uses generative policies based on diffusion or flow-matching models, which generate actions by transforming samples from a prior distribution—typically a standard Gaussian. Prior work showed that using a non-Gaussian prior closer to the target distribution substantially improves performance when training from scratch, motivating the question of whether such gains transfer to fine-tuning pretrained LBMs.

## [Crossflow Makes Prefill-Decode Partitioning Elastic for Agentic LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27085) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27085) introduces Crossflow, a system that makes the prefill-decode (P/D) boundary elastic without changing node roles by having each decode node publish a short-lived, revocable lease that bounds local-prefill compute, KV capacity, transfer work, and projected output. Across public and internal traces, Crossflow improves token throughput by 16.2–17.4% on geometric mean over static P/D and by up to 43.4% at high load, while reducing mean TTFT at every evaluated point. Static P/D partitioning is a core assumption of modern disaggregated LLM serving, but real agentic traffic makes phase demand highly variable, so fixed pools either waste up to 17% of cluster capacity or convert the imbalance into queueing and lost throughput. Crossflow's elastic boundary could let production fleets serve more tokens with the same hardware, directly benefiting operators running large-scale agentic workloads. The paper's motivating measurements show the ratio of uncached input to output tokens has peak-to-mean ratios up to 4.7x at minute timescales, and a public agentic trace shows an hourly ratio spanning a median 24.5x within a single day, while reassigning a replica takes tens of minutes. Sizing each pool at its 95th percentile leaves up to 17% of cluster capacity unused, and sizing below it turns the same imbalance into queueing.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: LLM inference has two phases: prefill, which processes the input prompt and is compute-bound, and decode, which generates output tokens one at a time and is memory-bandwidth-bound. Prefill-decode disaggregation runs these phases on separate GPU pools so each can be optimized independently, improving time to first token (TTFT) and inter-token latency (ITL), but it traditionally relies on a fixed split of resources between the two pools. Agentic workloads — where AI agents make decisions and take actions with minimal human intervention — produce bursty, uneven traffic that makes such a fixed split inefficient.

## [Fed-ADR: Orchestrated Gradient Attacks Bypass Federated Learning Defenses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27124) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint introduces Fed-ADR, a holistic attack framework in which a malicious orchestrator server dynamically coordinates a heterogeneous set of adversarial clients, including both targeted and untargeted attackers, to adaptively manipulate gradient updates and evade defenses deployed by the parameter server. The paper also proposes a detection mechanism that estimates each client's true gradient from historical updates, plus an in-situ recovery mechanism that restores global model accuracy to over 90% within a few rounds at least 20x cheaper than retraining from scratch. Most existing federated learning defenses assume static or independently acting adversaries, so Fed-ADR reveals a new class of dynamically adaptive attacks that systematically bypass state-of-the-art protections and can drop global accuracy from over 90% to below 10%. This matters for anyone deploying federated learning in security- or privacy-sensitive settings, since it shows that a compromised orchestrator can turn coordination itself into an attack surface, while also offering a practical detection and recovery path. The attack is evaluated on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10, where it bypasses several state-of-the-art defenses and severely degrades or steers the global model; the detection module estimates true gradients from historical updates without additional overhead, and recovery preserves convergence without restarting training. As a preprint, the work has not yet undergone peer review, and its effectiveness may depend on assumptions about the orchestrator's access and the fraction of compromised clients.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Federated learning (FL) is a distributed training paradigm in which many clients collaboratively train a shared model by sending model updates, rather than raw data, to a central parameter server that aggregates them. Because the server cannot inspect client data, FL is vulnerable to poisoning and Byzantine attacks, in which malicious clients submit manipulated updates to corrupt the global model. Defenses typically rely on robust aggregation rules that try to filter out anomalous updates, but these generally assume attackers act statically or independently rather than coordinating in real time.

## [SurgHiBench and HyperSurg: Hierarchy-Aware Evaluation for Surgical Video-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27139) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SurgHiBench, the first hierarchy-aware evaluation suite for surgical video understanding, with three tasks measuring recognition, consistency, and severity across granularity levels, and HyperSurg, a hyperbolic model that enforces phase-step containment via entailment cones. Evaluated on four existing datasets spanning three procedure types, the suite shows that two models with identical accuracy can produce predictions of very different error severity, ranging from sibling confusions within the correct phase to unrelated cross-phase predictions. Flat per-level metrics have hidden the fact that surgical video-language models can be equally accurate yet differ drastically in how dangerous their mistakes are, so this work gives the medical AI community a way to measure and penalize error severity. Hyperbolic geometry shifts predictions toward the correct procedural neighborhood, and the gains scale with the tree-likeness of each dataset's annotation hierarchy, offering a principled indicator of when hierarchy-aware geometry helps. HyperSurg enforces phase-step containment through entailment cones, a technique originally developed for embedding directed acyclic graphs and tree-like hierarchies. The evaluation covers a general-purpose CLIP model, a Euclidean surgical model, and HyperSurg across four datasets spanning three procedure types, and the paper notes concurrent work HyperVLP, which also uses a Lorentz manifold and hyperbolic entailment for surgical video-language representation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Surgical procedures follow a phase-to-step hierarchy, for example a cholecystectomy has phases such as dissection and clipping, each containing finer steps, but video-language models that recognize these stages are typically scored with flat per-level accuracy that ignores cross-level coherence and error structure. Hyperbolic geometry is a non-Euclidean space that provably models tree-like structures better than Euclidean space, and entailment cones are a method that constrains embeddings so that a parent concept's region contains its descendants. This paper applies those ideas to surgical video understanding, where a mistake that stays within the correct phase is far less harmful than one that jumps to an unrelated phase.

## [Multi-Stage Poisoning Attack Exploits Like-Score Loops in Agent Recommendation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27155) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27155) introduces a multi-stage poisoning attack that exploits the like-score mechanism in OASIS, a similarity-based social media recommendation system, to steer LLM-based agents' feeds. Through theoretical analysis, the authors characterize the conditions under which a chain of poisoned posts can trigger a self-reinforcing feedback loop, and they develop an algorithm that crafts realistic poisoned posts to achieve this effect. This work reveals a subtle vulnerability in automated social media agents that goes beyond direct content exposure, showing that recommendation systems themselves can be weaponized to surface adversarial content. As LLM-based agents gain broader autonomy to act on users' behalf online, such indirect, self-reinforcing attacks could become harder to detect and mitigate, with implications for AI safety and platform security. The attack exploits the like-score feedback loop so that the recommendation system selects poisoned posts even when their user-post similarity falls below the retrieval threshold, meaning the content would normally not be surfaced. The paper's abstract is truncated, and the attack is demonstrated in the OASIS simulation environment rather than on a live production platform.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: OASIS is an open-source social media simulator that uses LLM-driven agents to mimic the behavior of up to one million users, making it a common testbed for studying large-scale social phenomena. Similarity-based recommendation systems rank content by how closely it matches a user's profile or past behavior, and the like-score mechanism adjusts that ranking based on engagement signals such as likes. Adversarial attacks on recommender systems aim to manipulate these rankings, and prior agent-poisoning research typically assumes the adversary can directly expose poisoned content to the agent.

## [Paper Argues Linear Representation Hypothesis Needs Group Action Formalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27158) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27158) argues that the Linear Representation Hypothesis (LRH) is not a single hypothesis but a family of claims distinguished by how representation equivalence is defined, and formalizes this using group actions. The framework specifies the representation object, the procedure producing it, and the property asserted, while accounting for equivalences imposed by model architecture. This conceptual clarification could influence how interpretability researchers design metrics, probes, and interventions, since apparently comparable analyses may actually test different hypotheses. It matters for anyone making claims about representations that are meant to generalize beyond a single trained model. The paper uses group actions to audit common representation quantities and recent interpretability analyses, showing how assumptions can shift across metrics, reading points, and analysis stages. It is a theoretical contribution without immediate practical tooling or empirical benchmarks.

**Background**: The Linear Representation Hypothesis is the idea that neural networks represent features as linear directions in activation space, a central assumption behind much mechanistic interpretability work such as probing classifiers and activation steering. Group theory provides a mathematical language for describing symmetries and transformations, and a group action formalizes how a group transforms an object. Representation equivalence concerns when two representations should be considered the same, a question that becomes important when comparing models or architectures.

## [Tempered Evidence Fusion Weights LLM Sentence Judgments for Long-Text Value Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27165) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Tempered Evidence Fusion (TEF), a training-free decision-fusion rule that weights each sentence's log-odds by its normalized information gain, derived from a generalized Bayesian posterior. It also introduces MIND, a benchmark of 8,358 Chinese and English posts spanning five years of public events and six value dimensions, where TEF outperforms Direct, Majority Vote, and Soft Vote baselines by an average of 4.5 accuracy points and 4.6 macro-F1 points across five LLMs and two languages. Long social media posts often mix background, quotations, and concessions with only a few stance-bearing sentences, so existing document-level or simple voting aggregation can be overconfident or treat all sentences as equally informative. TEF offers a principled, training-free way to improve LLM-based value measurement, which matters for NLP and social computing researchers who use LLMs to analyze public opinion and value orientations. TEF makes the fused score nearly vanish for uncertain sentences while preserving the Bayes-optimal weight of decisive evidence, and it is training-free, requiring no additional model fine-tuning. The MIND benchmark contains 8,358 Chinese and English posts covering five years of public events and six value dimensions, with dataset and code available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used to measure public value orientations from long social media posts, but such posts often mix background, quotations, concessions, and only a few stance-bearing sentences. Existing approaches either ask the model to predict a document-level label directly, which can be overconfident, or aggregate sentence-level predictions by majority or soft voting, which treat uncertain and decisive sentences as equally informative. This paper formulates long-text value measurement as a decision-fusion problem and applies Bayesian principles to weight sentence-level evidence by its information gain.

## [SEMV: Self-Evolving Multi-Agent Framework for Multimedia Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27175) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SEMV (Self-Evolving Multimedia Verification), a multi-agent framework that treats provenance-bearing arguments as the interface between evidence, reasoning, human contestation, and memory. On the COSMOS benchmark it reaches 91.88% accuracy versus 89.10% for the strongest baseline, cuts negative transfer from 5.7% to 0.2%, and on the CTR benchmark corrects 96.7% of initial errors while saving 52.8% compute. Multimedia verification systems increasingly need not just accurate verdicts but traceable evidence and safe reuse of past experience, and SEMV shows that verification-gated memory consolidation can let a system evolve without accumulating harmful knowledge. This matters for AI/ML practitioners and multimedia forensics teams building systems that must remain auditable, revisable, and contestable in real-world misinformation workflows. SEMV combines arena-based quantitative bipolar argumentation (A-QBAF), causal and scoped revision, and verification-gated memory consolidation with explicit conflict retention, so accumulated knowledge and subsequent decisions stay traceable. The framework is evaluated on COSMOS, a contestation-derived CTR benchmark, and the MV2026 Grand Challenge dataset, which supports evidence-grounded, temporally consistent reporting.

**Background**: Multimedia verification means deciding whether images, video, or claims are authentic, and it requires not only a final label but evidence that a human can inspect and correct. Multi-agent systems split this work among specialized agents, while argumentation frameworks such as A-QBAF structure competing claims and their support or attack relations so reasoning can be scored and contested. Memory consolidation refers to deciding which past experiences to store and reuse; without verification gates, reusing flawed experience can cause negative transfer, where prior errors degrade new decisions.

## [UGTPhon: First G2P Benchmark for User-Generated Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27205) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced UGTPhon, the first grapheme-to-phoneme (G2P) benchmark for user-generated text (UGT) in English, Vietnamese, and Korean, along with an inference-grounded taxonomy for fine-grained diagnosis. They also propose a compositional G2P baseline that uses exact-match lookup and staged decoding to incorporate canonical-form evidence, reducing non-canonical errors across ByT5 and Qwen2.5-0.5B backbones. Text-to-speech systems increasingly encounter informal user-generated text such as "ppl" and "imo," whose pronunciation must be inferred from canonical forms rather than surface spellings. This benchmark exposes a systematic canonical-to-non-canonical performance gap of up to 66.8 PER points in existing G2P models and frontier LLMs, highlighting an important blind spot for TTS and NLP pipelines. The proposed compositional approach explicitly models canonical-form inference through exact-match lookup and staged decoding, and the 0.5B Qwen2.5 variant performs competitively with much larger few-shot frontier LLMs. The benchmark covers three languages and provides a taxonomy for diagnosing error types, though it remains a specialized contribution rather than a paradigm shift.

**Background**: Grapheme-to-phoneme (G2P) conversion is the task of transducing orthographic symbols (graphemes) into phonemes, the units of a language's sound system, and it is a core component of text-to-speech synthesis and automatic speech recognition. Traditional G2P systems and models are typically trained on canonical, well-formed text, so they struggle with the noisy, informal spellings common in user-generated content on social media. ByT5 is a tokenizer-free extension of mT5 that operates directly on UTF-8 bytes, while Qwen2.5-0.5B is a small large language model; both serve as backbones in this work.

## [Resistance-Curvature Sampling Speeds Up GNN Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27209) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27209) introduces a resistance-curvature-guided subgraph sampling framework for graph neural networks, built on ERC-LG, a scalable curvature approximation method. ERC-LG combines Johnson-Lindenstrauss projections with regularized multi-GPU batched conjugate gradient solvers, and the resulting curvature informs node- and edge-sampling probabilities for constructing GNN training subgraphs. Subgraph sampling is a key technique for reducing the training cost of large-scale GNNs, but existing sampling criteria often ignore the geometric roles of edges. By incorporating resistance curvature into sampling probabilities, this work could improve both accuracy and efficiency of GNN training, with experiments showing the highest mean accuracy on six of seven real-world datasets for downstream node classification. ERC-LG avoids explicit computation of the Laplacian pseudoinverse and full embedding storage, instead using Johnson-Lindenstrauss projections and batched conjugate gradient solvers. Experiments show numerical agreement with pseudoinverse-based curvature and reduced runtime compared with CG-only computation, though the approach still relies on iterative solvers and multi-GPU resources.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Graph neural networks (GNNs) learn representations of nodes by aggregating information from neighbors, but training on very large graphs is expensive, so subgraph sampling is used to train on smaller subsets. Resistance curvature is a discrete graph analogue of curvature based on effective resistance between nodes, capturing how edges connect different regions of a graph. The Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma states that high-dimensional points can be embedded into much lower dimensions while approximately preserving distances, which enables efficient approximation. ERC-LG leverages these ideas to approximate curvature at scale without expensive matrix inversions.

## [KATOsuper Uses Sensitivity-Consistent FNO to Accelerate Topology Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27216) ⭐️ 7.0/10

KATOsuper introduces an objective-agnostic framework that couples neural-reparameterized topology optimization with a Sensitivity-Consistent Fourier Neural Operator (SC-FNO), using a forward_split architecture that derives deployed sensitivities via automatic differentiation through the predicted objective field. It reports 15–110x deployment-time speedup over MATLAB baselines across three 2D benchmarks and three 3D structures, with zero-shot extrapolation up to 64x beyond training resolution without retraining. Gradient inconsistency between predicted objectives and sensitivities has been a key obstacle to using neural surrogates in topology optimization, causing instability; by enforcing consistency, KATOsuper could make surrogate-accelerated design more reliable for computational mechanics and AI-for-engineering workflows. The reported speedups and resolution-invariant extrapolation suggest practical value for complex 3D and stress-minimization problems where repeated finite element analysis is most expensive. The framework uses a physics-informed multi-channel input encoding with Fourier position embedding for resolution-invariant learning, and extends to 3D via KATO3D with novel KANConv3D blocks featuring learnable B-spline activations. The authors note that sensitivity direction matters more than magnitude, enabling robust optimization even with approximate physics evaluation, though useful extrapolation performance is described as best at moderate scaling factors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Topology optimization is a computational method that finds the optimal distribution of material within a design domain for given loads and constraints, typically relying on repeated finite element analysis (FEA) at each iteration, which is computationally intensive. Neural operators such as the Fourier Neural Operator (FNO) learn mappings between function spaces and are resolution invariant, meaning a trained operator can be applied to data of different resolutions. Neural network surrogates can replace some FEA evaluations, but if the predicted objective and its gradients (sensitivities) are inconsistent, gradient-based optimizers can become unstable.

## [LatWeave Compiles Multi-Hop QA into Deterministic Lattice Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27225) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27225) introduces LatWeave, which organizes knowledge into a multidimensional knowledge lattice and compiles multi-hop question answering into three deterministic operators: meet (constraint intersection), compare (lattice-order comparison), and abstain (structural abstention). LLMs are used only for one-shot knowledge extraction and query planning, while the answer-generation path is zero-LLM, zero-task-training, and fully auditable. The work directly targets hallucination, unauditable evidence chains, and over-answering in probabilistic QA systems such as LLMs and RAG, showing that deterministic execution can match supervised KBQA performance within a defined operating envelope. If the paradigm generalizes, it could influence how reproducible and auditable question answering over web-published knowledge is built. On MetaQA (39,093 questions) meet chains are near-lossless over three hops with any-hit 0.9975, on 2WikiMultihopQA held-out (n=1,258) it reaches EM 0.865, and on IIRC it achieves abstain accuracy 0.971 with a leak rate of 0.029. The authors report honest degradation on MuSiQue and HotpotQA, attributing it to causes outside the lattice-algebra layer rather than claiming across-the-board SOTA.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-hop question answering requires combining evidence scattered across multiple sources or passages through sequential reasoning steps, and current systems typically learn this end-to-end with neural networks or LLMs without an explicit, inspectable reasoning trace. A knowledge lattice is a mathematical structure that organizes knowledge along multiple dimensions with a partial order, allowing operations like intersection and order comparison. Deterministic algorithms always produce the same output for the same input, which is the property LatWeave exploits to make each answer step recomputable and auditable.

## [Risk-Sensitive Schrödinger Bridge Breaks KL Projection Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27250) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper proves that the risk-sensitive Schrödinger bridge cannot be represented as a constrained Kullback-Leibler projection onto any fixed path-space reference, unlike the classical case. The obstruction is computed in closed form: the Gaussian bridge value violates, by exactly θ/2, a heat equation that any Gaussian smoothing of a fixed endpoint density must obey. This result reveals a fundamental limitation of the KL projection framework under entropic risk measures, affecting researchers in stochastic optimal control, diffusion-based generative models, and risk-aware machine learning. It implies that risk-sensitive extensions require a different theoretical foundation, namely a terminal-multiplier fixed point and an asymmetric factorisation penalising the score energy of the backward factor. The proof holds even for a class strictly larger than uniformly elliptic diffusion references, requiring no Markov property, and even allowing an additive normalisation depending on the initial marginal. Moreover, no single reference generates the one-parameter family in the risk parameter, so the projection structure fails across the entire family.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: The Schrödinger bridge problem seeks the most likely stochastic path connecting two given endpoint distributions, and classically it reduces to a Kullback-Leibler projection onto a fixed reference measure, solvable by alternating projections via Girsanov's theorem. The entropic risk measure is a convex risk measure based on exponential utility that quantifies risk aversion, and replacing expected path cost with it yields a risk-sensitive control problem. This paper shows that this risk-sensitive extension destroys the KL projection structure that makes the classical bridge computationally tractable.

## [KITE Enables KV-Invariant Transformer Expansion for Cheaper Agentic LLM Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27294) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.27294) introduces KV-Invariant Transformer Expansion (KITE), a scaling paradigm that upcycles a smaller model into a larger one while placing newly added parameters in regions that do not affect attention KV. As a concrete instantiation, the authors present Step Scale Transformer (SST), a two-tower decoder in which one tower produces KV and the other reads them; a 67B MoE SST with 2.15B active body parameters per decode token achieves lower training loss than 47B and 63B MoE Transformers at comparable cumulative training compute, while cutting estimated inference cost by 6.7% and 31.6%. This work targets a core bottleneck in LLM scaling: the trade-off between model quality and the compute spent on training, prompt processing, and autoregressive decoding. By keeping the KV cache invariant, KITE allows larger models to be built from smaller ones without proportionally increasing inference cost, which could directly benefit agentic workloads where long prompts and repeated decoding make KV cache memory and latency dominant. If validated, the approach could offer a practical alternative to training large models from scratch and to conventional upcycling into MoE. SST is a two-tower decoder where one tower produces the KV cache and the other reads it, so prefilling KV only depends on the smaller part of the model. The reported 67B MoE model has 2.15B active body parameters per decode token and is compared against 47B and 63B MoE Transformers with 1.48B and 2.02B active body parameters, respectively. The results are based on training loss and estimated inference cost, and as an arXiv preprint the work has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: KV caching is a standard technique in transformer inference: during autoregressive decoding, the key and value tensors of each layer are stored so they do not need to be recomputed for every new token. This KV cache becomes a major memory and latency bottleneck, especially for long prompts and agentic workloads. Model upcycling is a related idea in which a pre-trained smaller model is expanded into a larger one—often a sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE)—to save training compute. KITE combines these ideas by expanding the model while keeping the KV-producing part unchanged, so the larger model does not pay a larger KV cache cost.

## [Teach-to-Crash: Dual-LLM Framework Generates Collision Scenarios for AV Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27296) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Teach-to-Crash, a closed-loop student-teacher LLM framework that adaptively generates executable, diverse, safety-critical collision scenarios for validating autonomous driving systems in simulation. In a CARLA case study, it achieved a 90.79% Collision Hit Rate, an 18.31 s mean Time-to-Collision, and the highest scenario diversity (0.547) among compared methods. Rare, safety-critical corner cases are the hardest part of validating autonomous driving systems, and this work shows that closed-loop dual-LLM reasoning can steer adversarial simulation testing toward frequent, structurally diverse, and more avoidable failures. If it generalizes beyond the evaluated CARLA scope, it could make simulation-based safety validation more efficient and informative for AV developers. The Teacher LLM acts as an adaptive search controller and only intervenes when rolling collision rate and time-to-collision metrics stagnate, while the Student LLM emits simulator-executable scenarios in a strict JSON schema over a constrained ego-centric representation. PAFOT achieved a higher mean Collision Discovery Rate (179.44) but with substantially larger variance, and Teach-to-Crash's usefulness proxy of 60.04% is averaged across two speed-policy setups on the CARLA Traffic Manager controller.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Autonomous driving systems must be tested against dangerous situations, but real-world crash data is scarce and collecting it is costly, so developers rely on simulation. CARLA is an open-source urban driving simulator widely used for this purpose, providing a Python API and digital assets for reproducible testing. Large language models have recently been used to generate test scenarios, but many prior methods operate offline or overfit to common driving patterns, failing to expose rare corner cases.

<a id="item-116"></a>
## [Monte Carlo Study Finds Quasi-Experimental Power Calculations Overstate Achieved Power](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27299) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27299) reports a Monte Carlo study across 9,837 parameter conditions and roughly 9.8 million datasets, decomposing the gap between planned and achieved power in quasi-experimental information systems research. It finds that panel attrition alone costs approximately 8 to 11 percentage points of power at the few-hundred-to-thousand sample sizes typical of IS studies, and that staggered-adoption bias and parallel-trends pretesting are captured by no closed-form formula. The findings suggest that many published quasi-experimental IS studies may be underpowered relative to what their design-stage calculations claim, which could mean overstated confidence in causal estimates. This has direct implications for how researchers justify sample sizes and how reviewers and editors evaluate the credibility of difference-in-differences and instrumental-variable designs. The serial-correlation component of the power gap is recoverable by an AR(1)-aware calculator when rho is known and partially recoverable when rho must be estimated from short pre-periods, but attrition, staggered adoption, and pretesting remain unmodeled. For instrumental variables, holding the first-stage F fixed, larger N neither raises power nor curbs exclusion bias, so identification rests on instrument strength rather than sample size; treatment-correlated, outcome-dependent attrition induces bias rather than mere power loss.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Quasi-experimental methods such as difference-in-differences (DiD) and instrumental variables (IV) are widely used in information systems and related social sciences to estimate causal effects from observational panel data, where randomized experiments are infeasible. Researchers typically justify their designs with power calculations that assume independent and identically distributed errors, often with cluster-robust corrections. However, real panel data involve complications such as respondents dropping out over time (panel attrition), units adopting a treatment at different times (staggered adoption), and pre-testing of parallel trends, none of which standard closed-form power formulas account for.

## [Evaluation Pitfalls Inflate Encrypted C2 Detection F1 Scores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27311) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A leakage-controlled measurement study on 17,577 TLS flows from 62 real Cobalt Strike captures shows that a single preprocessing mistake—computing frequency encoding across the whole dataset instead of within each cross-validation fold—inflates F1 by 0.28, roughly ten times larger than the real benefit of fusing flow statistics with TLS handshake fingerprints (only 0.022 F1). The findings suggest that many reported gains in ML-based encrypted C2 detection may be artifacts of flawed evaluation design rather than genuine model improvements, which could mislead defenders and researchers about what actually works in production. Because labels and behavioral features depend on the destination address, the 17,577 flows collapse into only 2,132 independent groups, and the apparent 55.1% positive rate drops to 4.2% when counting endpoints; additionally, 20 of 62 captures (32%) contain no TLS flows to known C2 addresses, and when an attacker forges both feature surfaces, every model underperforms a trivial always-positive baseline (F1 = 0.711).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Command-and-control (C2) traffic is the communication channel attackers use to control compromised machines, and frameworks like Cobalt Strike are widely abused for this purpose; as such traffic increasingly hides inside TLS encryption, defenders turn to machine learning on traffic metadata such as flow statistics and TLS handshake fingerprints. Data leakage occurs when a model uses information during training that would not be available at prediction time, producing overly optimistic metrics that fail in the real world.

## [S2C: Safety-Filtered RL Yields Non-Exploitable Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27312) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Safety to Competence (S2C), a two-stage reinforcement learning framework that separates safety synthesis from competitive task learning. S2C learns a robust safety filter via adversarial RL, embeds it during task policy training, and retains the same filter at deployment, outperforming eight safe RL baselines in simulated touchdown games with the highest win rate and Elo rating and the lowest exploitability. This work addresses a core tension in safe RL: coupling safety and task objectives can leave policies exploitable by deliberate attacks. By proving that perfect filtering preserves non-exploitability when all players commit to safe maneuvers, S2C offers a principled path toward robot policies that are both competitive and provably robust, relevant to deployment in adversarial or human-shared environments. S2C formulates competitive interactions as safety-critical Markov games and provides a theoretical proof of non-exploitability under the assumption that all players commit to safe maneuvers. Empirical validation includes simulated touchdown games against eight safe RL baselines and hardware stress tests against a human opponent, though the non-exploitability guarantee depends on the stated commitment assumption.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Safe reinforcement learning extends standard RL, where an agent learns to maximize reward through interaction, by adding constraints or filters to avoid unsafe outcomes. Markov games (also called stochastic games) generalize Markov decision processes to multiple interacting decision makers whose joint actions affect state transitions. Adversarial RL trains agents against worst-case opponents to improve robustness, and S2C combines these ideas by learning a safety filter adversarially before training the competitive task policy.

## [LLM-Evolved Inspectable O-RAN Slicing xApps Validated on 5G Testbed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27337) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27337) uses a large language model to evolve O-RAN slicing controllers as compact, readable Python programs, validated on the NSF POWDER 5G testbed. The evolved controller improved best-effort throughput from 158.2 to 228.6 Mbps (a 44.5% gain over the best static allocation) and reduced SLA misses from 79.9% to 2.2% in one case. This work shows that LLM-driven program synthesis can replace opaque deep reinforcement learning policies in O-RAN slicing with decision logic that operators can directly read, predict, and edit. If it generalizes, it could make adaptive RAN control more trustworthy and maintainable, affecting how operators deploy and debug xApps on near-real-time RICs. The LLM proposes and revises candidate Python programs offline while a calibrated simulator scores them, and the selected decision module then runs unchanged in the O-RAN control path. In a four-slice trace-driven simulation, evolutionary search beat independent prompting at a matched proposal budget, with mean normalized gains on held-out traces of 16.3% for prompting alone, 32.1% for evolution from scratch, and 51.0% for evolution from a starting program.

Deep reinforcement learning can produce adaptive allocation policies, but its rules are buried in neural-network parameters, making them hard for operators to inspect or edit. ## [Quantization-Robust Unlearning via Loss Landscape Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27355) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces a quantization-robust unlearning framework that analyzes the curvature of the retain-forget loss landscape to identify sensitive weights responsible for non-robust forgetting. It then applies sensitivity-guided noisy regularization to steer convergence toward smoother minima and uses forget-critical optimization to update only forget-critical layers, achieving more quantization-resilient forgetting on MUSE and TOFU benchmarks across multiple LLM unlearning algorithms while maintaining utility. Machine unlearning is critical for LLM compliance with privacy and copyright regulations, but practical deployment almost always involves post-training quantization that can silently weaken forgetting. This work provides a principled solution to make unlearning robust to compression, which could help ensure that deployed compressed models actually respect data removal requests. The framework identifies sensitive weights via curvature-based criteria and applies noisy regularization specifically to those parameters, while forget-critical optimization limits updates to forget-critical layers to preserve useful knowledge. Experiments on MUSE and TOFU benchmarks show substantially more quantization-resilient forgetting than existing methods, though the approach assumes access to the unlearned model's loss landscape and may add computational overhead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Machine unlearning for LLMs aims to remove the influence of private or copyrighted training data without retraining the model from scratch, and it is evaluated on benchmarks like MUSE and TOFU. Quantization is a common post-training compression technique that reduces the numerical precision of weights and activations to lower memory and compute costs, but it introduces noise that can degrade model behavior. Loss landscape analysis studies how the curvature of the loss surface affects optimization and generalization, and this paper connects that lens to the interaction between forgetting and retention under quantization.

## [Depth-Aware Distillation Boosts Visual Place Recognition Without Depth Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27370) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Depth-Aware Distillation (DAD), which conditions the token representations of a pretrained Vision Foundation Model on geometry inferred by a Geometric Foundation Model without any depth sensor. On the WildCross benchmark, DAD improves average inter-sequence Recall@1 from 61.41% to 66.37% and Recall@5 from 65.86% to 72.49% over a matched appearance-only baseline. Visual place recognition is a core capability for robot localization, SLAM, and autonomous navigation, and it often fails in natural environments where vegetation is repetitive and appearance changes drastically. By showing that geometry inferred from a geometric foundation model can serve as a persistent structural prior, this work offers a practical path to more robust place recognition without adding depth hardware. DAD projects image-aligned depth into the VFM token space and selectively modulates visual representations through channel-wise geometric conditioning, rather than treating geometry as an extra input modality. A two-stage teacher-guided learning strategy first anchors the geometry-conditioned representation to the pretrained appearance space before refining it for place discrimination, with the largest gains under reverse traversal and long-term appearance variation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Visual Place Recognition (VPR) is a content-based image retrieval task: given a query image, the system returns the database image closest in geographic location, and it is widely used in robotics and self-driving for localization, mapping, and planning. Modern VPR methods rely on deep features from vision foundation models, but these appearance-based representations degrade when the same place looks very different across seasons, lighting, or viewpoints. Geometric foundation models (GFMs) such as Metric3D v2 can estimate metric depth and surface normals from a single RGB image, providing a geometry signal that is often more stable than appearance.

## [TACT Benchmark Scores Turn-Taking by Speaker Intent, Not Binary Windows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27372) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces TACT, a benchmark of 9,728 episodes and 73.2 hours drawn from five dyadic corpora, where each episode includes dialogue history, a per-speaker memory profile, and an annotator-derived posterior over six intent classes. It replaces binary fixed-window turn-taking rules with a strictly proper, intent-conditioned threshold-weighted continuous ranked probability score (CRPS), and across eleven systems the best model reaches 0.47 against a human topline of 0.86, agreeing with human judgments at Spearman 0.81 versus 0.46 for binary metrics. Turn-taking evaluation for full-duplex spoken dialogue models has largely relied on binary fixed-window rules that treat any silence or overlap as a failure, which misrepresents natural conversation where delayed silence or anticipatory overlap can be appropriate. By conditioning scoring on latent speaker intent, TACT offers a more human-aligned metric that could reshape how full-duplex dialogue systems are trained and compared, even though its impact remains concentrated within the spoken dialogue research community. The scoring method is proven to be bounded, consistent, and reducible to the binary case, with weights derived from intent-conditioned timing kernels fitted to human floor-transfer-offset distributions. Notably, the best system's score is nearly invariant to speaker profiles, suggesting current models do not yet exploit the per-speaker memory information that TACT provides.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Full-duplex spoken dialogue models can listen and speak simultaneously, unlike traditional half-duplex systems that process one turn at a time, enabling more natural real-time interaction with backchanneling and overlap. Evaluating such models is difficult because turn-taking timing is inherently continuous and context-dependent, and existing benchmarks like Full-Duplex-Bench still rely largely on fixed-window binary rules. The continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) is a strictly proper scoring rule from probabilistic forecasting that generalizes mean absolute error to predictive distributions, making it suitable for grading continuous timing predictions rather than binary outcomes.
