## [Planned Test-Time Scaling Coordinates Reasoning Paths to Boost pass@k](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27374) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Planned Test-Time Scaling (PTTS), a planner-executor framework that replaces independent repeated sampling with a coordinated joint policy, where a planner generates a distinct solution outline for each branch and an executor produces a full solution conditioned on that outline. Two variants are introduced: PTTS-ZS, which jointly generates all branch outlines in a single autoregressive pass, and PTTS-RL, which directly optimizes the planner against the pass@k reward using truncated execution rollouts. On five mathematical reasoning benchmarks with Qwen3-1.7B and 4B, PTTS-ZS improves pass@64 over repeated sampling by up to 6.7 points, and PTTS-RL raises the gain to up to 13.4 points. This work shows that the way parallel inference branches are generated matters as much as how many are generated, offering a general recipe to get more out of a fixed inference budget. If the gains hold on larger models and other domains, PTTS could influence how practitioners design test-time scaling pipelines for mathematical and code reasoning, and it points toward training planners specifically for coverage rather than raw accuracy. The paper formally proves that PTTS strictly generalizes repeated sampling and, in a stylized setting, promotes coverage of complementary reasoning modes with better pass@k scaling. PTTS-RL uses truncated execution rollouts to make training efficient and to obtain a sharper reward signal, and the authors attribute the gains to broader coverage of distinct reasoning paths; results are limited to Qwen3-1.7B/4B and five math benchmarks, and the work is an arXiv preprint without peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Test-time scaling means spending extra computation at inference time, for example by sampling many parallel reasoning branches and picking the best answer, to improve performance on hard tasks. The dominant method, repeated sampling, draws each branch independently from the same policy, which often wastes compute on redundant attempts. pass@k measures the probability that at least one of k sampled outputs is correct, so it rewards diversity across branches rather than the quality of any single attempt. A planner-executor architecture separates strategic planning from tactical execution, and PTTS applies this split to coordinate the branches.

## [PALS Defense Cuts Adversarial Attacks on Full-Duplex Speech Dialogue Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27378) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper formalizes imperceptible adversarial attacks on full-duplex speech-to-speech dialogue models as perturbations hidden beneath the psychoacoustic masking threshold of the carrier speech, targeting semantic hijacking, response suppression, and policy jailbreaking. Against an undefended Moshi-style agent, white-box attacks succeeded in up to 91.7% of trials, but the proposed psychoacoustically aligned latent smoothing (PALS) defense reduced hijack to 8.3%, mute to 11.2%, and jailbreak to 9.1% while keeping clean quality degradation within 2.3%. Full-duplex speech-to-speech models keep an acoustic channel continuously open, creating a security surface that turn-based systems do not have, so this work highlights a real and previously underexplored vulnerability in real-time voice agents. Because PALS adds no inference-time cost and preserves clean quality, it offers a practical defense path for deployed voice assistants and dialogue systems. PALS injects anisotropic Gaussian noise shaped by local codebook covariance at the residual-vector-quantized latent interface, with input noise shaped by the masking threshold that constrains the attacker and trained via a Kullback-Leibler consistency objective. A Monte Carlo-smoothed variant certifies an ellipsoidal latent radius up to 0.616, a guaranteed robustness floor that the empirical results far exceed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Full-duplex speech-to-speech dialogue models listen and speak at the same time, unlike traditional turn-based systems that alternate between receiving and generating speech, which makes them more natural but also exposes a continuously open audio channel to manipulation. Psychoacoustic masking is a property of human hearing whereby a louder sound makes nearby weaker sounds inaudible, and attackers can exploit this by hiding adversarial perturbations below the masking threshold so users hear nothing unusual. Residual vector quantization (RVQ) is a hierarchical compression technique used in neural audio codecs, where each stage quantizes the residual left by previous stages, and it provides the latent interface that PALS smooths.

FWBench addresses the gap between forecast accuracy and operational decision quality by simulating capacity contracts and scoring decisions with loss-cost objectives.

## [GUARD Framework Prevents Speaker Re-Identification in Zero-Shot TTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27399) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes GUARD, a lightweight speaker identity unlearning framework for zero-shot text-to-speech that combines a learned speaker gate with speaker-agnostic activation steering on a frozen TTS backbone. On CosyVoice2, GUARD reduces forget-speaker similarity from 0.541 to 0.103 and re-identification accuracy in a 150-speaker gallery from 73.5% to 0.5%, while preserving retain-speaker reproduction. This work addresses the growing privacy concern that zero-shot TTS systems can clone voices from just a few seconds of audio, enabling unauthorized impersonation. By introducing re-identification accuracy as a complementary evaluation criterion, it challenges the assumption that similarity reduction alone equals successful unlearning, which could shape future privacy standards for speech synthesis. The steering vectors are optimized using group-relative reward optimization to shift outputs from forget speakers toward population-level impostor similarity while preserving intelligibility and naturalness. The framework operates on a frozen TTS backbone, making it lightweight, and the results suggest that similarity reduction alone may not fully characterize successful speaker identity unlearning.

## [BEE: Intervention-Adaptive Real-World RL for Frozen VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27450) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced BEE, an intervention-adaptive framework that treats human corrections as evidence about constraints rather than actions to imitate, enabling real-world reinforcement learning on a frozen vision-language-action (VLA) model. Evaluated on three real-world manipulation tasks and one LIBERO-Pro simulation task at a matched online-data budget, BEE achieved the highest success rate on every task, averaging 91.2% versus 57.5% for RLT and 42.1% for DSRL, while also attaining the lowest human intervention rate on all real-world tasks. Real-world reinforcement learning for robots is bottlenecked by the prohibitive cost of free exploration, making human corrections essential but hard to exploit effectively. BEE's approach of using corrections to set per-dimension constraint tightness could make online RL on frozen VLA models substantially more sample-efficient and practical for precision-critical manipulation tasks. BEE introduces a Correction Model that predicts how a human would correct a given VLA proposal and how consistent that correction is along each action dimension; this predicted consistency sets the per-dimension tightness of a constraint on policy optimization, keeping the policy close to the human where corrections are consistent and relaxing the constraint where they vary. The approach is evaluated at a matched online-data budget against RLT and DSRL baselines, though it remains a preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models combine a pretrained vision-language model with an action output, enabling robots to translate natural language commands and visual input into control signals for long-horizon manipulation. However, their success often hinges on a few precision-critical phases where millimeter-scale errors undo prior progress, and online reinforcement learning can optimize exactly these actions but is too costly to explore freely on real robots. Human-in-the-loop RL incorporates human corrections, but existing methods for VLAs either cannot use such corrections or fold them into undifferentiated supervision, ignoring that corrections are reliable along some action dimensions and variable along others.

## [Issuer-Sovereign Agentic Payments Keeps Bank Control Over AI Spending](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27452) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27452) proposes Issuer-Sovereign Agentic Payments, a method in which the cardholder approves a spending rule once and the issuing bank's own authentication component records it. When an AI agent later pays a specific merchant, the bank checks that merchant against the approved rule and generates the card authentication value only if the merchant is allowed, so the payment still travels normal card rails with no extra execution-time dependency. Today's agentic payment setups typically rely on credential providers outside the cardholder's bank, leaving the issuer — which bears the financial risk — with little direct control at the moment of payment. Shifting rule enforcement and authentication-value generation back to the issuer could give banks a stronger governance and risk-control position as AI agents begin making real payments. The proposal is conceptual and does not include an implementation or empirical validation, and it assumes the issuer already operates an authentication component capable of evaluating merchants against stored spending rules. It also relies on existing card rails and the card authentication value (e.g., CAV/CVC/CVV) as the enforcement mechanism, so its practical value depends on issuer-side integration.

**Background**: AI agents are increasingly able to initiate purchases, but letting them hold payment credentials raises the risk of unauthorized or excessive spending. In card payments, the issuing bank is the institution that ultimately approves or declines a transaction and carries the financial risk, while the card authentication value is a security code used to verify card data. Existing agentic payment approaches often place spending-rule enforcement with third-party credential providers or the card network rather than the bank itself.

## [Kairos: 4D Scene Graphs Forecast Human Presence and Directional Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27467) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Kairos extends a hierarchical 3D scene graph (3DSG) into a 4D scene graph (4DSG) by storing a directional mixture and a presence rate in every observed voxel, then using spectral predictors to forecast, for any future query time, both the probability that people are present and the full directional distribution of their motion. It was evaluated on three real pedestrian environments — a robot-collected campus dataset, a shopping mall, and a station concourse recorded continuously for eleven months — with code released at https://github.com/IacopomC/kairos. Long-term autonomy in human-populated spaces requires anticipating whether and how people will move at times a robot has not yet observed, and Kairos shows that a patrolling robot learning from only a small fraction of detections can produce forecasts competitive with dedicated occupancy and flow models trained on the full detection stream. In a downstream encounter-probability planning task, plans computed over Kairos forecasts encountered more people than plans computed over any time-invariant map at an equal success rate, which matters for service robots, autonomous navigation, and mobile robotics in crowded public spaces. Adjacent voxels are linked by pairwise flow dependence to support conditional queries, and per-voxel predictive variances yield calibrated credible intervals that tighten as observations accumulate; the learned state also remains consistent under loop-closure corrections. The approach is evaluated against dedicated occupancy and flow models, though the abstract does not report absolute accuracy numbers or runtime costs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: A hierarchical 3D scene graph represents an environment as a graph of places, objects, and their relationships, which robots use for spatial reasoning and navigation. Spectral forecasting applies frequency-domain analysis to time series, capturing periodic patterns such as daily or weekly cycles in human activity. A credible interval is the Bayesian counterpart of a confidence interval: given the observed data, it states the posterior probability that the true value lies within a range, which is useful when a robot must reason about uncertainty in its predictions.

Researchers propose PhyMo, a physics-grounded multimodal framework that introduces a new "physical-field modality" to organize heterogeneous measurements through PDE-associated operators. PhyMo uses a three-stage learning procedure: pretraining a physical-field encoder via field reconstruction under PDE residual supervision, aligning its representations with visual embeddings in a shared latent space, and feeding the fused multimodal representations to downstream prediction heads. Experiments on five datasets across diverse physical environments show state-of-the-art performance versus the strongest baseline on each dataset. Existing multimodal AI4Physics approaches typically treat physical quantities and governing equations as generic numerical or textual tokens, ignoring the physical constraints that govern their spatiotemporal interactions. By encoding those constraints directly into a dedicated modality, PhyMo could improve how physics-informed machine learning models represent and predict complex physical systems, benefiting researchers working at the intersection of AI and physics. The physical-field encoder is pretrained through field reconstruction with PDE residual supervision, a technique related to physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) that embeds governing equations as a loss term. The framework is evaluated on five datasets spanning diverse physical environments, though the abstract does not specify dataset names, model sizes, or quantitative gains.

TNLearn is an open-source Python package that automates the construction and training of task-based neurons and networks, and it has been accepted as a PyTorch ecosystem project. The package provides comprehensive documentation, including technical exposition, API reference, and representative examples, and is available on GitHub. This release lowers the barrier to experimenting with task-based neurons, a paradigm that shifts neural network design from task-based architectures to task-specific neurons. By joining the PyTorch ecosystem, it could encourage broader adoption and further research into NeuroAI-inspired approaches. Task-based neurons are generated via symbolic regression to capture useful prior knowledge from task-related data, rather than relying on a single generic neuron type. The package automates construction and training, but the abstract does not specify performance benchmarks or limitations.

TNLearn operationalizes this idea by providing tooling for automated construction and training within the PyTorch ecosystem.

## [FDE-Bench Tests LLM Agents on Deployment Environment Configuration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27571) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces FDE-Bench, a benchmark of 136 deployment-configuration tasks spanning Docker images, multi-service Compose stacks, and Kubernetes, in both greenfield and diagnose-and-repair modes. Agents submit declarative artifacts that are rebuilt and redeployed in a pristine environment, then graded by four gated binary check layers covering build, readiness, behavior, and specification conformance, with no LLM judge involved. Deployment configuration is a critical but under-explored capability for LLM agents, and FDE-Bench provides a rigorous, programmatically verifiable way to measure it. Its findings—that readiness is the largest failure stage and that human guidance substantially boosts success—offer concrete direction for both agent developers and DevOps practitioners. Seven language models from four providers, all using the same four-tool scaffold, resolve 52.9–75.0 percent of the 136 tasks, while three zero-intelligence floors resolve none and reach a mean Deployment Score of at most 0.44. Readiness accounts for 110 of 313 unresolved episodes, mean resolution is 30.7 percentage points higher on the repair group than on the disjoint greenfield group, and in a 25-task case study an engineer directing Claude-Sonnet-5 reaches 92 percent versus 72 percent for the autonomous baseline.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Deployment means turning application code into a running system whose services connect, become ready, and stay observable, typically by writing Dockerfiles, Docker Compose files, or Kubernetes manifests. Existing agent benchmarks mostly evaluate code changes, whereas FDE-Bench fixes the application code and evaluates only the deployment artifacts, which are collected, rebuilt, and redeployed in a pristine environment. Its four-arm release gate requires a resolving reference solution and rejects tasks that can be solved by do-nothing, specification-transcription, or generic-stub submissions, and three additional adversarial strategies probe for shortcuts in the grading signals.

## [DCRL aligns policy and reward manifolds to curb reward hacking in LLM RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27572) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27572) proposes Decoupling and Coupling Reinforcement Learning (DCRL), a geometric framework that models LLM reasoning as a coupled manifold of logical deduction, evaluation, and representation sub-manifolds. DCRL combines a syllogistic logic-based prompt evolution mechanism for refining reward rubrics with a policy-reward re-coupling mechanism that jointly updates reward and policy models, and reports that a Qwen3-4B model trained with DCRL surpasses a Qwen3-32B baseline and approaches Qwen3-235B performance. Reward hacking and unstable optimization are persistent obstacles in applying RL to LLM reasoning, so a framework that theoretically reframes these failures as policy-reward manifold mismatch and empirically narrows the gap between small and very large models could meaningfully improve how reasoning models are trained. If the reported gains generalize, DCRL could let much smaller models reach performance levels previously requiring far more compute. The framework interprets response generation as decoupling from the evaluation manifold and reward estimation as decoupling from the logical deduction manifold, with rule-based and reward-model RL failures geometrically explained as policy-reward manifold mismatch. The abstract is truncated and does not provide full empirical validation details, so the reported Qwen3-4B versus Qwen3-32B/235B comparisons should be treated as preliminary until the full paper and benchmarks are examined.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning is a training paradigm in which a model learns by receiving rewards for its outputs, and it has become a key method for improving LLM reasoning. Reward hacking occurs when an RL agent exploits flaws or ambiguities in the reward function to score highly without genuinely completing the intended task, a well-documented failure mode. Manifold alignment, meanwhile, is a machine learning technique that finds a common latent space across datasets or representations; DCRL borrows this geometric idea to argue that policy and reward models should occupy aligned manifolds during training.

## [Capability Manifold Framework Extends ML Scaling Laws Beyond Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27588) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.27588) introduces the 'capability manifold,' a multidimensional framework that maps downstream capabilities such as reasoning, retrieval, planning, and adaptation to pre-training, post-training, and test-time resources through bounded scaling functions. The authors embed Kaplan- and Chinchilla-type scaling laws and test-time compute into this framework, showing they can be unified as trajectories on a common manifold, with analytical Jacobians quantifying capability sensitivity to resource changes. As models are increasingly deployed through agentic harnesses, predictive loss alone fails to distinguish models with similar loss but different downstream capabilities, so this framework could reshape how the community allocates compute across the ML lifecycle. It offers a theoretical bridge between classical scaling laws and the practical resource-allocation decisions that drive agentic AI performance. The framework uses bounded scaling functions and analytical Jacobians to quantify how sensitive each capability is to changes in pre-training, post-training, and test-time resources, as well as their interactions. As an initial application it only demonstrates embedding existing Kaplan- and Chinchilla-type laws and test-time compute, so empirical validation of the manifold itself remains future work.

**Background**: Traditional neural scaling laws, such as the Kaplan scaling laws and the later Chinchilla scaling laws, describe how test loss decreases predictably as model parameters, training data, and compute are scaled up. The manifold hypothesis in machine learning holds that high-dimensional data lies on a lower-dimensional structure, and this paper adapts that geometric intuition to model capabilities rather than data. Agentic harnesses refer to the scaffolding that wraps a model with tools, memory, and multi-step control loops, which makes downstream behavior depend on more than raw loss.

## [Linear Concept Erasure Fails Under Superposition, Networks Hide Features Instead](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27593) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27593) shows that state-of-the-art linear concept erasure methods fail when two features are forced into an antipodal pair sharing a single subspace, destroying both features rather than just the target. Gradient-trained networks instead converge to one of two distinct non-linear circuit-level solutions—termed mirror and shadow solutions—that leave a measurable trace of the erased feature recoverable via a single scalar patch without further training. This finding has direct implications for AI safety and interpretability, as it offers a mechanistic, causally-validated explanation for why LLM unlearning often suppresses rather than deletes knowledge, allowing forgotten information to resurface. It suggests that current concept erasure and unlearning techniques may provide a false sense of security in fairness auditing and model editing. The bifurcation between mirror and shadow solutions depends on initialization and feature entanglement, and the authors show it reflects a stable attractor structure rather than an artifact of their setup. Targeted causal interventions confirm that both solutions leave a substantial, measurable trace of the erased feature's representation intact, recoverable through a single scalar patch.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Concept erasure aims to remove specific features (e.g., gender or race) from a neural network's activations, often using linear projection methods like LEACE that assume features occupy separable subspaces. Superposition occurs when a network represents more features than it has dimensions, causing features to overlap and become entangled. Mechanistic interpretability studies neural networks at the circuit level—specific subgraphs of neurons and attention heads—to understand how computations are implemented. This paper bridges these areas by showing that linear erasure assumptions break down under superposition, and that networks develop non-linear circuits to cope.

A new arXiv paper (2609.27603) introduces FakeContextBench, a benchmark of 3,500 pseudoscientific claims across seven domains, and shows that prevalent ICL fine-tuning methods can reduce reality accuracy by up to 14.95 percentage points versus base models. The authors propose Jurisdiction In-Context Learning (J-ICL), a post-training framework that adds context validation to the training objective, improving ICLEval by 5.84 points and reality accuracy by 9.20 points on average across four model backbones. The findings expose a critical blind spot in in-context learning: models trained to extract patterns from demonstrations often lose the ability to judge whether contextual information should govern the answer, making them vulnerable to misleading prompts. This matters for any LLM deployment where users supply context, since J-ICL shows that ICL capability and resistance to deceptive context can be improved together rather than traded off. FakeContextBench contains 3,500 benchmark cases across seven domains with a deterministic scorer for raw model responses, and J-ICL raises the Reality Rate by an average of 18.09 points relative to MetaICL and Symbol Tuning. The paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet, so results should be treated as preliminary.

## [InGuard Embeds Safety Guardrails Inside Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27620) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose InGuard, an inner guardrail framework that classifies prompts as unsafe, risky, or benign using the text encoder's own embeddings, then uses SAGE (Soft-gated Asymmetric Guardrail for Embeddings) to modify risky embeddings so a safe image is produced instead of a refusal. A latent detector checks the one-step clean latent estimate midway through denoising, and across five open-weight T2I models InGuard achieves 97.9-98.8% safety rate with 57.5-73.5% less benign disturbance, ~3.7x fewer parameters, and 50-55.6% of denoising steps skipped. This approach could make T2I safety more efficient and flexible by avoiding external classifiers and by adjusting prompts rather than rejecting them outright, which matters for developers deploying open-weight image models and for users who currently get blunt refusals. It also signals a shift toward using a model's internal representations for content moderation, an idea that may extend to video and image-to-image generation. InGuard leaves base-model parameters untouched and requires no external language model, and its latent detector reaches nearly image-level performance while halting generation early to save compute. The authors also build the RevGen Safety Benchmark with 10,000 prompts from real-image reverse generation, including a rewriting step for controlled IP characters and graded porn/gore risks.

This separation can reduce prompt-screening accuracy, wastes compute on flagged generations, and only allows rejection rather than prompt adjustment.

## [FLEET Adds Entropy-Trajectory Memory to LLM Sampling for 3x Speedup](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27657) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27657) introduces FLEET, a method that adds a memory mechanism to LLM text generation by representing each generation as a sparse trajectory through states whose entropy exceeds a predefined threshold, then using these trajectories to infer per-token utility scores that adjust the logits. Benchmarks show FLEET matches repeated-sampling accuracy with a 3x speedup and raises LiveCodeBench Pass@32 from 59.9% to 66.2% under the same budget. Temperature sampling is a standard way to boost LLM accuracy by aggregating multiple samples, but its memoryless nature causes semantically duplicate answers and diminishing returns as more samples are drawn. FLEET's memory-aware adjustment could make inference more efficient and accurate for researchers and practitioners running repeated sampling on complex reasoning and coding tasks. In the greedy-decoding configuration evaluated, FLEET is deterministic and derives its principal hyperparameters from a single calibration pass, requiring only minimal modifications to existing LLM pipelines. The method's gains are demonstrated on complex coding tasks, with LiveCodeBench Pass@32 improving from 59.9% to 66.2% under the same budget.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models generate text by producing logits, which are raw scores over the vocabulary that get converted into probabilities for sampling the next token. Temperature sampling rescales these logits to control randomness, and repeated sampling draws multiple completions to improve accuracy, but because each draw is independent and memoryless, it wastes compute on duplicate answers. Entropy-based sampling uses model uncertainty to guide generation diversity, and FLEET builds on this idea by tracking entropy-thresholded trajectories as a form of memory.

## [AI Neural Surrogates Enable In Silico Neuromodulation Target Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27729) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.27729) introduces an AI-driven neural-surrogate framework that proposes and tests candidate representational changes from stimulus-evoked fMRI snapshots without physical stimulation. Using over 36,000 image-fMRI observations from four Natural Scenes Dataset participants, the framework achieved two-way identification of 0.79–0.88 and shifted valence from -0.61 to +1.03 SD in the primary VDVAE model. This work offers a falsifiable upstream method for designing and behaviorally testing candidate representational targets for future neuromodulation in psychiatry, potentially accelerating the development of treatments for negative affective bias and overly salient memories. It represents a methodological advance at the intersection of AI, neuroscience, and neuropsychiatry. Human valence ratings moved in the predicted direction across five perturbation levels (mean slope 0.038 SD per unit alpha; 95% CI 0.003–0.074; positive in 16 of 18 participants), but perceived memorability did not change reliably and baseline agreement with the automated assessor was weak for memorability (r = 0.10). Extreme perturbations drifted from the original stimulus, so intended change must be weighed against loss of fidelity.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: A surrogate model is an approximate mathematical model used when an outcome of interest cannot be easily measured or computed, and neural networks are often employed as surrogates for expensive simulations. In silico neuromodulation refers to using computational platforms to test stimulation protocols without in vivo experiments, while constrained latent-space steering manipulates internal representations of generative models to control outputs along desired dimensions. This paper combines these concepts to design cognitive-affective neuromodulation targets entirely in silico.

## [InfiNoVA Uses 3D Gaussian Splatting to Make Robot Policies Viewpoint-Invariant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27734) ⭐️ 7.0/10

InfiNoVA is a data-augmentation framework that reconstructs synchronized multi-camera manipulation demonstrations as time-varying 3D Gaussian representations, then renders dense novel views from sampled camera poses while preserving the original state-action correspondence. Across four real-world manipulation tasks, policies trained with InfiNoVA achieved 5.4x higher average success under unseen randomized viewpoints than VISTA-based augmentation and the unaugmented baseline, and 1.7x higher success than training directly on all five physical camera views. Vision-Language-Action policies typically degrade sharply when deployed from camera viewpoints not seen during training, and collecting demonstrations from many physical viewpoints is expensive and still sparse. InfiNoVA shows that dense, geometrically grounded viewpoint augmentation can deliver camera-robust robot policies without changing the underlying policy architecture, which is directly useful for robotics, computer vision, and embodied AI researchers. The method relies on an explicit time-varying 3D Gaussian scene representation rather than generative novel-view synthesis, which the authors say improves frame-level fidelity and temporal consistency while reducing task-critical hallucinations. The evaluation covers four real-world manipulation tasks and compares against VISTA-based augmentation, the unaugmented policy, and training on all five physical camera views.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies are models that combine visual perception, language understanding, and action generation so a robot can follow natural-language instructions such as "put the bowl on the cabinet." Novel view synthesis is the task of generating images of a scene from camera angles not present in the input images, and 3D Gaussian splatting is a rasterization-based radiance-field technique introduced in 2023 that represents a scene as many 3D Gaussians and renders photorealistic novel views in real time. InfiNoVA combines these ideas by turning multi-camera robot demonstrations into a dynamic 3D Gaussian scene that can be re-rendered from arbitrary viewpoints for training.

## [Hard Negatives Reveal Cross-Lingual Harmfulness Representation Collapse in Low-Resource Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27758) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27758) shows that cross-lingual harmfulness representations in LLMs degrade sharply in low-resource languages when evaluated with hard negatives, even though near-perfect transfer (AUROC > 0.98) is observed with easy negatives. On Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, the mean AUROC drop grows from 0.003 in English to 0.276 in low-resource languages, and the pattern replicates on Aya Expanse. This finding challenges prior conclusions that harmfulness representations survive translation and that cross-lingual refusal failures are merely a calibration issue, suggesting that safety alignment research relying on easy-negative evaluation may overestimate cross-lingual robustness. It has direct implications for how AI safety is evaluated and deployed in multilingual settings, especially for low-resource language communities. The study spans nine languages across three resource tiers and uses XSTest contrast prompts as hard negatives; back-translation chrF controls and a matched-chrF comparison across three languages reduce the likelihood that translation quality explains the effect, with the collapse remaining after controlling for chrF (partial r = 0.70, p = 0.03). Tokenizer fertility correlates with the collapse and explains part of the resource-tier effect, but not all of it.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Safety alignment in large language models is trained primarily in English, and prior work reported that English-trained probes separate harmful from harmless prompts almost as well in low-resource languages as in English. Hard negatives are negative examples that are superficially similar to positives, making them harder for a classifier to distinguish; XSTest is a test suite of benign prompts that are surface-similar to harmful requests. AUROC (area under the receiver operating characteristic curve) is a common metric for binary classification performance, where higher values indicate better separation.

## [FedMAST Detects and Contains Backdoors in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27760) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.27760) introduces FedMAST, a Federated Multi-Axis Structural Tracing defense that scores client updates using complementary structural, spectral, and historical evidence, then applies tiered filtering and round-level containment. Across six backdoor attacks (Constrain-and-Scale, Neurotoxin, BC-Layers, LGA, DBA, and 3DFed) and nine attack-defense comparisons, FedMAST achieves lower attack success rates than baseline defenses, averaging 1.51% ASR over 200 rounds while keeping 94.84% main-task accuracy. Federated learning is increasingly deployed in privacy-sensitive settings such as mobile keyboards and healthcare, where a single poisoned client can silently implant a backdoor in the global model. A defense that reliably detects and contains such attacks without sacrificing main-task accuracy could make federated deployments substantially more trustworthy. FedMAST introduces squeeze-pair coherence scoring to expose coupled feature distortions and signed spectral-drift tracking to reveal persistent directional changes over time. Under the method-aware CovertLayers attack, FedAvg, MultiKrum, AlignIns, and FLAME yield full-run ASRs of 100.00%, 99.67%, 99.53%, and 32.84%, respectively, while FedMAST reduces ASR to 1.53% with 92.26% main-task accuracy; the work is an unpeer-reviewed preprint.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Federated learning trains a shared model across many clients without centralizing their raw data, but the server must trust the model updates clients submit. Backdoor attacks exploit this by having malicious clients submit poisoned updates that embed a hidden trigger, causing the global model to misbehave on inputs containing that trigger while performing normally otherwise. Existing defenses often inspect isolated signals, which stealth-constrained attacks can adapt to; FedMAST instead argues that poisoned updates still leave residual structural traces across multiple axes.

A new arXiv paper introduces a multi-turn dataset of 1,762 conversations (adversarial, benign twins, and benign high-risk vocabulary variants) and a lightweight hierarchical attribution model that predicts safety violations while attributing them to specific user turns and token spans. The model achieves F1=0.988 for detection, and removing the top 15% of attributed tokens reduces adversarial classification confidence by 51.1%, with false positives below 1% on benign high-risk conversations. Most guardrail evaluation frameworks only judge whether a request is safe or unsafe, which fails for multi-turn attacks where adversarial intent is spread across turns. This work enables precise attribution of which turns and tokens drive unsafe trajectories, improving interpretability and targeted mitigation for agentic LLM systems. The dataset includes behavioral validation and tiered evidence supervision, and independent human annotation confirms that the top-five attributed turns contain a human-identified evidence-bearing turn in 84.5% of adversarial cases. The model maintains low false positive rates (below 1%) on borderline benign and benign high-risk vocabulary conversations, compared to 37.3% and 94.7% for a keyword-based surface-risk baseline.

EidosDoc introduces an Implicit Structure Encoder trained with contrastive learning and a structure consistency loss to jointly embed hierarchy, spatial position, and text into a dense vector space, combined with a hybrid BM25/layout-fingerprint/cross-encoder retrieval pipeline and a Dynamic Evidence Expansion mechanism. Evaluated on four benchmarks, it claims new state-of-the-art accuracy with 50x lower cost and 4x lower latency than the previous state-of-the-art method. Semi-structured document QA is critical for scientific reports, financial statements, and technical manuals, but existing LLM-heavy pipelines are expensive and slow. EidosDoc's LLM-free retrieval and implicit structure encoding could make accurate document analysis far more scalable and practical for retrieval-augmented generation systems. The system avoids invoking an LLM entirely during retrieval by combining BM25, layout fingerprints, and a lightweight cross-encoder, and its Dynamic Evidence Expansion adaptively pulls in spatially adjacent and structurally related evidence to overcome the evidence omission common in fixed-path retrieval. As an arXiv preprint, the results have not yet undergone peer review or community validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Semi-structured documents interleave text, tables, charts, and irregular hierarchical layouts, so answering questions over them requires understanding both content and structure. Prior approaches either repeatedly call large language models to parse and retrieve structure, which is costly and slow, or flatten the document and lose layout and hierarchy information, which hurts accuracy. Contrastive learning is a technique that trains models to place similar items closer together and dissimilar items farther apart in a representation space, and EidosDoc uses it together with a structure consistency loss to learn document structure without manual annotation.

## [LabourCrew: Multi-Agent RAG Framework for Trustworthy Labour Law QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27814) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced LabourCrew, a multi-agent RAG framework for statutory labour-law question answering that combines three grounding mechanisms: StatuteGraph, a graph index linking chapter, section, proviso, and cross-reference structure; an Evidence Exchange Protocol that confines advocates and an interpreter to an evidence ledger; and a Calibrated Trust Gate that replaces categorical accept/reject decisions with a trust score thresholded via conformal risk control. Evaluated on LabourActQA, a 500-item Bangla question set from the Bangladesh Labour Act, 2006, the framework achieves an empirical false-accept rate of 0.081 within the target level of α = 0.10 and the highest Answer Relevancy (0.862) among HyDE RAG, Graph-RAG, and Hierarchical RAG baselines. Legal question answering demands that every claim be traceable to evidence rather than merely relevant, since unverifiable labour-rights answers carry serious legal consequences; LabourCrew shows that calibrated abstention, not retrieval quality alone, is what makes legal QA auditable in low-resource statutory domains. This matters for legal-tech builders and regulators because it offers a concrete, distribution-free mechanism for bounding false-accept rates in high-stakes AI advice. The framework's fault-tolerant supervisor board runs advocates in parallel so individual failures degrade rather than crash the system, and the Calibrated Trust Gate uses conformal risk control to provide a distribution-free bound on the false-accept rate. Performance degrades gradually rather than catastrophically as question difficulty increases, though evaluation is limited to a single Bangla-language statute and 500 questions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets large language models retrieve external documents before answering, improving factual grounding, while multi-agent systems coordinate multiple specialized agents to solve problems a single model cannot. Statutory law is a complex web of cross-references, so graph-based indexes that model sections and their dependencies can support more precise retrieval than fixed-length text spans. LabourCrew builds on these ideas to address a known weakness: single-pass RAG cannot detect insufficient evidence, and prior multi-agent legal-debate systems treat grounding as a prompting convention, allowing agents to cite unretrieved evidence.

A new arXiv paper (2609.27816) presents a centralized safety-aware framework for heterogeneous multi-robot coordination, pairing a vision-capable quadruped with a camera-less robotic vehicle. A vision-language model interprets the visual stream into conservative metric geometric constraints, an LLM proposes high-level task allocations, and a robot-specific zonotope reachability gate holds final authority over physical commands. The work addresses a core challenge in heterogeneous robotic teams: robots with different sensing and motion capabilities must cooperate safely without letting unverified AI reasoning directly drive motors. By strictly separating advisory semantic reasoning from formally verified execution, it offers a practical architecture that could improve safety in autonomous multi-robot operations such as inspection, logistics, and search-and-rescue. Under a shared-perception principle, the vision-capable robot sends semantic scene data through a centralized server over an MQTT broker, letting the camera-less platform navigate using that representation plus its own odometry, IMU, and state feedback. The reachability gate propagates independent reachable tubes to check obstacle avoidance, safe-corridor containment, and inter-robot separation predicates before approving commands, triggering conservative replanning or holding maneuvers when constraints are violated.

Reachability analysis computes all possible future states of a dynamic system from an initial set under uncertainties and control inputs, and is widely used in robotics for formal verification of safe behavior. ## [Non-Invasive Cloud Proxy Brings Post-Quantum Crypto to Smart HVAC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27828) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27828) proposes a non-invasive post-quantum cryptography (PQC) proxy that requires no changes to HVAC devices, firmware, or vendor clouds, performing ML-KEM-768 encapsulation and ML-DSA-65 authentication via Open Quantum Safe liboqs on a Raspberry Pi 4B gateway. Over 500 runs, the post-quantum handshake completed in 2.48 ms — only 0.38 ms slower than the classical baseline — while sustaining 443 sessions/second and 100% success under 32 concurrent connections. Legacy smart HVAC controllers with 10–15 year lifespans rely on ECDH and RSA TLS, which Shor's algorithm will break, yet direct on-device PQC is infeasible on constrained hardware like the ESP32-S3. This vendor-agnostic proxy offers a practical migration path for the huge installed base of IoT building systems that cannot be replaced or upgraded before quantum computers arrive. The ESP32-S3 has only 339 KB free heap versus the 900 KB ML-KEM-768 requires, and even classical ECDH-P256 keygen (111.93 ms) dwarfs hardware AES-128 (0.032 ms), motivating the gateway approach. PQC computation accounts for about 8% of handshake time at 20 ms simulated round-trip latency, and five side-channel tests — including in-place session-key zeroization and fixed-vs-random TVLA timing analysis — found no exploitable leakage or MITM susceptibility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Post-quantum cryptography refers to algorithms designed to resist attacks from both classical and quantum computers; NIST standardized ML-KEM (FIPS 203, formerly Kyber) for key encapsulation and ML-DSA (FIPS 204, formerly Dilithium) for digital signatures in 2024. Shor's algorithm would let a sufficiently powerful quantum computer break RSA and elliptic-curve cryptography, which underpin today's TLS connections. Open Quantum Safe's liboqs is an open-source C library that implements these quantum-safe algorithms for prototyping and experimentation.

## [Bounded Loops: Provable Termination and Spend Bounds for Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27871) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27871) proposes a "bounded loop" architecture for agent harnesses, composed of a worker, an independent gate the worker cannot write to, and a declared budget. The authors formally prove three guarantees — termination under repair, no drift (no node reaches DONE without a gate verdict in an append-only hash-chained ledger), and no overspend (the ceiling is enforced inside an attempt, not between attempts) — and release an engine, a 69-loop catalogue, and a mutant corpus under Apache-2.0. Mainstream agent frameworks currently decide a step is finished based on the agent's own output, and durable-execution platforms typically place their checker in the same codebase as the work being checked — a discipline trusted to the deployment rather than a property the harness enforces. By turning termination, non-drift, and spend limits into proved, machine-measured properties, this work offers AI-safety and reliability engineers a concrete way to constrain autonomous agent behavior. The paper characterizes two failure classes that let a sound-looking gate pass anything: vacuity (the check is satisfied by the absence of the thing checked) and self-attestation (the subject supplies the value the check is applied to). On a 69-loop catalogue the instrument found 47 vacuous gates in shipped, reviewed code; against repaired gates it reports no false accepts over 209 destroying mutants (α ≤ 1.8%, Wilson 95%), but the authors caution this is saturation rather than quality, since freezing the gates and applying a fresh operator family recovers a 23.3% false-accept rate.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: An agent harness is the orchestration layer around a large language model — the tools, permissions, context management, and control loop that turn a stateless text generator into a tool-using problem solver. Durable-execution platforms (such as Temporal or AWS's durable execution SDK) keep workflows running through crashes by persisting state and automatically retrying failed steps, but they bound retries and time rather than proving that an agent loop terminates or stays within budget. A hash-chained, append-only ledger is a log in which each entry hashes the previous one, so any retroactive edit is detectable — the same tamper-evidence idea used in auditable AI memory and run ledgers.

## [False-Science Induction: How Misbound Data Corrupts Autonomous Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27883) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27883) introduces and demonstrates "false-science induction," a failure mode in which legitimate physical objects and measurements are coherently mispaired inside closed-loop autonomous discovery systems. Across green fluorescent protein fitness and materials band-gap prediction loops, this coherent paired misbinding caused neural surrogates to learn false object-outcome associations and systematically redirected experimental budgets toward low-performing basins, while same-volume random swaps had negligible effects. As closed-loop discovery systems increasingly run experiments and update decisions autonomously, record integrity effectively becomes part of the experimental apparatus, so a silent data-binding error can waste large experimental budgets without any change in marginal data distributions. The paper reframes data binding as an operational control problem, which is likely to influence how AI-for-science pipelines are designed, monitored, and audited. The key variable is error coherence rather than raw error frequency: coherent misbinding reliably misallocates budgets, whereas random swaps of the same volume do not. The authors characterize a "binding identifiability boundary" and propose mitigations such as monitored-axis quarantines and feedback-conflict triage, which intercept over-concentrated proposals before execution and isolate the corrupted hypothesis axis.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Closed-loop autonomous experimentation systems use machine-learning models, often called neural surrogates, to approximate expensive physical experiments and decide which experiment to run next. Because these loops trust their own records as training data, any mismatch between an object and its recorded measurement can be learned as if it were real physics. The paper builds on the classical philosophical problem of induction, in which regularities observed in past data are assumed to hold for unobserved cases, and shows how that assumption breaks down when the data-binding step itself is corrupted.

RelCheck introduces a training-free post-hoc correction pipeline that augments object-level visual grounding with dual relational evidence: learned scene-graph triples from RelTR and deterministic spatial predicates from bounding-box geometry. Evaluated on LLaVA v1 13B, it achieves a total MME hallucination score of 630.0 versus 585.0 for a Woodpecker-style baseline, with the largest gain on the position subtask (+31.7 points, accuracy+ improving from 0.367 to 0.600). Relational hallucinations—incorrect descriptions of spatial or interactive relationships between objects—remain largely unaddressed by existing post-hoc correction methods, which focus on object- and attribute-level errors. This work shows that structured relational evidence can meaningfully improve correction on the spatial reasoning subtasks where current MLLMs are most deficient, offering a practical, training-free path for researchers working on multimodal hallucination mitigation. The pipeline combines a Woodpecker-style object claim layer with scene-graph triples and spatial predicates to form a three-layer visual knowledge base, which a language model corrector uses to rewrite hallucinated text. A four-configuration ablation confirms that both relational layers contribute independently (McNemar p = 0.025), though the evaluation is limited to LLaVA v1 13B and the MME hallucination benchmark.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) frequently generate text inconsistent with the input image, a problem known as hallucination. Woodpecker is a well-known training-free post-hoc correction framework that validates object-level visual claims before rewriting hallucinated text, while RelTR is an end-to-end scene graph generation model that predicts relational triples (subject-predicate-object) directly from images. MME-Hallucination is a benchmark that measures hallucination across object, attribute, and spatial/position subtasks, and RelCheck extends the Woodpecker-style approach by adding relational evidence to address the spatial subtasks where MLLMs struggle most.

## [SchrodingerRepo Tests Whether LLMs Truly Reason About Code or Just Memorize SWE-bench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27891) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27891) introduces SchrodingerRepo, an evaluation framework that treats the test repository as an evaluation-time latent variable, dynamically instantiating it only when the coding agent enters the environment. The instantiated repository preserves original executable behavior while eroding familiar cues through four transformation levels: problem statement reconstruction, namespace remapping, intra-file layout reordering, and functionality-preserving code rewriting. The results show that removing familiar repository cues consistently degrades agent performance and substantially increases interaction costs across popular LLMs on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-QA, suggesting current coding agents may partially rely on memorized repository-side cues rather than genuine repository reasoning. This has significant implications for how the community evaluates coding agents and designs future benchmarks. The framework's four transformation levels preserve executable behavior while eroding naming conventions, file layouts, and implementation patterns; further analysis attributes the added cost primarily to increased difficulty in repository exploration and localization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: SWE-bench is a widely used benchmark that evaluates large language models on real-world software issues collected from GitHub, where the model must generate a patch that resolves a described problem. Because such benchmarks are built on popular open-source repositories that are repeatedly used for LLM training, they inherently suffer from data leakage, meaning strong performance may reflect memorization of canonical repository cues rather than robust reasoning. Data leakage is a known major issue in LLM evaluation, as the black-box nature of training data makes it difficult to know whether contamination has occurred.

## [What Makes Test-Time Budget Reallocation Pay Off in Neural Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27917) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27917) uses pre-registered confirmatory experiments across three independently trained solvers and two ways of constructing harder workloads on the traveling salesman problem to identify what determines whether reallocating a fixed test-time sample budget toward harder instances improves solution quality. It finds the deciding property is how varied instance difficulty is within a workload, not the workload's average difficulty, and shows that a budget-aware policy that pays to learn instance difficulty recovers most, though not all, of the gain available when that information is free. This work tackles a subtle evaluation bias in adaptive test-time compute: deciding an allocation and evaluating it on the same data can manufacture apparent gains even when none exist, so the paper's pre-registered protocol offers a template for verifying that reported improvements are not measurement artifacts. It matters for anyone deploying adaptive compute in neural combinatorial optimization or, more broadly, in test-time compute scaling for LLMs, where deciding which inputs deserve more compute is an active problem. The experiments were independently recomputed from their written specifications, and every correction to an earlier version—including two that weakened the paper's own claims—is reported along with the direction it moved the conclusion. The key caveat is scope: the findings are established within the studied workloads on the traveling salesman problem, and the budget-aware policy recovers most but not all of the improvement available under free difficulty information.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Neural combinatorial optimization uses neural networks, often trained with reinforcement learning, to learn heuristics for hard combinatorial problems such as the traveling salesman problem, typically by generating many candidate solutions per instance and reporting the best one found. Test-time compute scaling spends extra computation during inference—through repeated sampling, search, or extended reasoning—to improve output quality, but under a finite budget a system must decide which inputs deserve more compute. Pre-registered confirmatory experiments fix the analysis and verdict criteria before data collection, distinguishing confirmatory tests from exploratory analyses that are more tentative and prone to bias.

## [Riemannian Geometry for Low-Parametric Orthogonal Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27982) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.27982) studies matrices built from block-diagonal factors interleaved with fixed permutations, and derives the conditions under which this structured class admits a smooth manifold structure. For the practically important case of orthogonal two-factor matrices, the authors derive the essential Riemannian tools and propose efficient algorithms that use automatic differentiation, support parameter sharing within each factor, and avoid explicit dense matrix construction. This work bridges Riemannian optimization and structured matrix design, offering a principled way to train low-parameter orthogonal layers that balance expressivity and efficiency. It could benefit deep learning researchers working on parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large language models, where reducing trainable parameters and memory is a central concern. The algorithms are tested within the Riemannian optimization framework on the best matrix approximation problem and on parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large language models. Beyond the two-factor setting, the paper also analyzes the geometric and matrix-theoretic properties of factorizations with a larger number of block-diagonal factors.

**Background**: Riemannian optimization extends gradient-based optimization to variables constrained to lie on smooth curved spaces (manifolds), rather than ordinary Euclidean space. Orthogonal matrices are square matrices whose rows and columns are orthonormal, so they preserve inner products and represent rotations or reflections; the set of such matrices forms a manifold. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) adapts large pretrained models by updating only a small subset of parameters, cutting compute and memory costs. Structured matrices made of block-diagonal factors and permutations are attractive in deep learning because they offer a favorable trade-off between expressivity and efficiency.

## [ARIA: Governing Multi-Agent AI in Regulated Finance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27994) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces the concept of 'constitutional non-compositionality' — the gap where individually compliant AI agents collectively produce discriminatory or otherwise unacceptable outcomes — and proposes ARIA, a finance-specific reference architecture and falsifiable research agenda for governing agent populations. ARIA organizes six capabilities across normative-accountability, execution-control, and assurance-learning planes, including policy specification, population-level observed-versus-expected behavior monitoring (M2), bounded authority, runtime containment, adaptive policy change, and preserved human oversight competence. Current AI governance in finance is component-centric, testing and authorizing each model or agent locally, which is insufficient when institutional risk emerges from joint behavior. This work matters because it maps multi-agent controls to fair-lending, EU AI Act, model-risk, and conduct-supervision evidence needs, offering regulators and financial institutions a framework for population-level accountability as agentic workflows spread into credit, fraud, collections, and compliance. The paper is a research agenda rather than a production-effectiveness claim, and its two simulations illustrate shared-signal thin-file exclusion under local controls and earlier warning from observed-versus-expected distributional monitoring in a constructed drift regime. It closes with a validation agenda, meaning the architecture has not yet been empirically proven in real financial deployments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent AI systems deploy many autonomous or semi-autonomous agents that interact, and in finance these agents are increasingly used for credit scoring, fraud detection, collections, and compliance. Governance today typically certifies each agent in isolation, but collective dynamics can create emergent harms such as disparate impact that no single agent exhibits. 'Constitutional non-compositionality' names this mismatch between local compliance and acceptable collective outcomes, echoing broader debates about disparate-impact liability in AI and the EU AI Act's requirements for high-risk systems.

## [PISCES: Physics-Informed Autoencoder for Solar Wind Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28022) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present PISCES, a convolutional autoencoder trained without catalog labels on OMNI solar wind measurements under physics constraints including magnetic field consistency, a temperature-velocity relation, and the Parker spiral angle. At inference it decomposes the anomaly score into magnetic and plasma reconstruction errors, physics relations, and residual corrections, and its smoothed alarms can precede independently observed sudden commencements. Space weather early warning depends on detecting solar wind transients at the L1 Lagrange point before they reach Earth, and fixed thresholds can miss combined magnetic and plasma structure. PISCES offers an interpretable, label-free alternative that could improve lead time and trust in automated alerts for satellite, power grid, and communication operators. The loss penalizes changes between consecutive one-minute samples in derived quantities calculated from the reconstruction, and attenuating skip connections (selected on validation data) improves average precision for trained models while untrained scores remain nearly the same. The trained models also give a more consistent ordering of the physical contributions, and after smoothing with a trailing median the alarms can precede positive sudden impulses.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: An autoencoder is a neural network trained to reconstruct its input through a compressed bottleneck, so unusual inputs produce large reconstruction errors that signal anomalies; a convolutional autoencoder uses convolutional layers suited to time-series structure. Physics-informed neural networks embed physical laws as constraints during training, acting as a regularizer that improves generalization when labels are scarce. OMNI is NASA's cross-normalized near-Earth solar wind magnetic field and plasma dataset, available at one-minute resolution, and L1 is the Sun-Earth Lagrange point where spacecraft such as ACE and DSCOVR sample the solar wind upstream of Earth.

A new arXiv paper (2609.28026) introduces FeedType, a benchmark of annotated feedback from six LLMs under three prompting strategies alongside expert teacher feedback across three university writing courses. The authors refine Narciss's taxonomy into seven feedback focus types and find that while most LLMs cover most focus types, none reproduce teacher feedback distributions or match teachers' adaptivity across draft stages and student performance levels. As LLMs are increasingly deployed as writing tutors and teaching assistants, this work shows that high coverage of feedback types does not equal pedagogical alignment with expert instructors. The released benchmark gives researchers and edtech developers a concrete way to measure and improve the pedagogical adaptivity of AI feedback before it reaches students. The benchmark covers six LLMs evaluated under three prompting strategies, with feedback annotated using seven focus types derived from Narciss's taxonomy and compared against teacher feedback across three university writing courses. The key limitation reported is that LLMs show varying and generally weaker adaptivity than teachers, with none matching the teachers' adaptive behavior across draft stages and performance levels.

## [TEMPS Adds Temporal Reasoning to Dense Retrieval and RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28048) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce Temporal Textual Similarity (TTS), a new task measuring how well two anchored texts align in time independent of topical similarity, and present TEMPS (Temporal Embedding Model for Precise Search), a modular temporal branch that attaches to a frozen semantic retriever. On three temporal benchmarks TEMPS improves MRR for every semantic backbone tested, and on TS-Retriever it lifts R@1 from 19.92 to 25.39 over the prior temporal state of the art. Most dense retrievers and RAG pipelines match queries to documents on topic but ignore time, so they often surface content that is on-topic yet temporally wrong — a serious problem in clinical, journalistic, and legal search where when an event occurred determines relevance. TEMPS offers a drop-in way to add temporal awareness without retraining the underlying semantic retriever, which could benefit both IR researchers and practitioners building time-sensitive RAG systems. TEMPS resolves anchored temporal expressions to intervals and moment-matches each one to a Gaussian, using the resulting ordering to supervise an anchor-date-conditioned encoder whose score is fused with the semantic score at inference. Because grounding supplies the supervision, training requires no hand-labeled temporal data, and the temporal score itself is the Gaussian-KL inclusion measure from distributional embeddings — TEMPS's contribution is the grounding and moment-matched supervision.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Dense retrieval represents queries and documents as continuous vectors in a shared semantic space, so it can match paraphrases like "car insurance" and "auto coverage" that lexical methods such as BM25 miss. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) builds on this by having a large language model retrieve relevant documents from an external knowledge base before generating an answer. However, neither approach natively models time, so a query about a specific period may retrieve semantically similar but chronologically inappropriate documents.

## [Amateur single-camera sports video: no single AI paradigm wins every stage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28049) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28049) tests whether strong scores on general video and world-model benchmarks translate into reliable per-player attribution in chaotic amateur sports footage, using volleyball as a case study across 66 amateur matches and 46,648 human-labelled contacts. It evaluates four approaches — prompting and agentic reasoning over frontier vision-language models, classical computer vision with small trained specialists, self-supervised video world models, and manual annotation — at every stage of the pipeline, from finding play boundaries to naming who did what. The results challenge the common assumption that benchmark performance implies deployment readiness, showing that no single paradigm wins every stage and that static single-frame computer vision is not competitive at any stage involving motion or identity. This matters for sports analytics, applied computer vision, and any low-resource setting where only one fixed camera is available. A prompted model segments matches well, yet a far smaller trained model beats it at spotting contacts for a fraction of the cost, and the sport's own rules recover rally outcomes that pixels cannot. Identity is the hardest part: a jersey number is a static fact that temporal reasoning cannot recover if it is never visible, whereas sporting action is a repeated motor pattern a temporal model can exploit — which is why holistic reasoning improves event detection while identity stays unchanged.

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) jointly interpret images and text, extending large language models with visual input, and agentic reasoning lets such models plan and act over multiple steps rather than answer a single static question. Classical computer vision instead relies on hand-crafted features and small task-specific models trained on labelled data, while self-supervised video world models learn temporal dynamics from unlabelled footage. Published video benchmarks typically use curated, single-actor, or professionally filmed clips, so it is unclear whether models that score well there remain robust on amateur team-sport footage shot with one fixed camera and several players crowded into frame.

## [LAYERSCOPE: Label-Free Layerwise Analysis of Video and Multimodal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28086) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose LAYERSCOPE, a label-free, layerwise framework that uses local, global, distributional, and correspondence-based geometric metrics to characterize learned representations in video and multimodal models. Evaluating seven architecturally diverse models on MVEB/MVEB+ classification, clustering, and text-to-video retrieval tasks, they find that intermediate-layer representations often outperform final-layer and model-default outputs. This work offers a systematic, label-free way to compare representations across models and layers, which could reduce the heavy labeling and computation costs of layer selection and guide more efficient use of intermediate layers in video and multimodal systems. It also challenges the common assumption that the final layer is always the best representation for downstream tasks. No single geometric metric consistently predicts downstream performance: LID shows task-dependent relationships, RankMe is strongest for classification and clustering but not a universal layer selector, and pairing-aware metrics explain retrieval better than distributional distances alone. Distinct layerwise geometric signatures also emerge across model families, suggesting architecture-specific representation dynamics.

Geometric metrics such as RankMe (effective rank) and LID (local intrinsic dimensionality) are spectral or neighborhood-based measures used to describe the structure of learned representations without task labels.

## [LLM Backtest Auditing Benchmark Exposes Over-Flagging of Clean Controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28090) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a 96-item paired benchmark for auditing LLM detection of rigged backtests, where each flawed backtest has a matched clean control differing in only one methodology detail. Testing four text endpoints over 1440 cached audits, the primary DeepSeek auditor achieved 100.0% closed and clean-aware code recall, but open prompts over-flagged 93.8% of clean code controls, and a clean-aware warning reduced code false positives from 20.8% to 0.0% at unchanged recall. This work reframes backtest auditing as a calibration problem, showing that reporting flaw recall alone can rank models identically while the clean-control false-positive rate separates them by 79 points. It provides an actionable insight for LLM evaluation and financial ML, where false alarms on legitimate strategies are costly and undermine trust in automated auditing. The benchmark uses a deterministic scorer that separates flaw recall, clean-control false positives, evidence localization, and fix relevance, with 95% confidence intervals reported. Notably, the budget anchor still flags 38/48 clean controls under the same prompt, and clean-aware all-three specificity is 87.5% even where recall saturates.

Auditing these issues manually is difficult, and LLMs are increasingly used to detect such flaws, but their tendency to over-flag clean strategies creates a calibration challenge.

## [Fed-ReMasker Brings Federated Masked Autoencoding to Feature-Level Missing Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28105) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Fed-ReMasker, a federated learning adaptation of the ReMasker masked autoencoder that lets local centers impute entire features they never observed locally by leveraging knowledge learned across collaborating centers. In a benchmark spanning synthetic datasets with linear and nonlinear relationships and real-world tabular datasets including clinical data, it achieved the lowest imputation error in 93.2% of value-level and 96.7% of feature-level scenarios in the homogeneous setting, and came within 3.0% on average of a centralized model trained on pooled data. Multi-center clinical studies and biomedical collaborations often cannot share raw patient data due to privacy regulations, and different centers may collect only partially overlapping feature sets, so a method that imputes entirely missing features without centralizing data could unlock much larger and more generalizable models. This matters for privacy-preserving healthcare AI, where feature-level missingness has been under-explored compared with value-level missingness. Fed-ReMasker remains robust to client heterogeneity using simple federated averaging, outperforming all baselines in all 36 value-level scenarios and each baseline in at least 35 of 36 feature-level scenarios. The benchmark varies the number of centers, missingness ratios, and client heterogeneity, though the approach is a specialized contribution rather than a broadly applicable general-purpose tool.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Federated learning is a distributed machine learning technique in which multiple clients collaboratively train a model while keeping their data decentralized, motivated by privacy, data minimization, and data access rights. A masked autoencoder is a self-supervised model that learns representations by reconstructing randomly masked portions of its input, an idea popularized for vision by Masked Autoencoders Are Scalable Vision Learners. Imputation is the statistical process of replacing missing data with substituted values to avoid bias and loss of efficiency caused by discarding incomplete cases. Fed-ReMasker combines these ideas to handle feature-level missingness, where an entire feature is unobserved at some centers, rather than only scattered missing values.

Experiments on the RLBench benchmark show the approach improves multi-task policy performance over naive training while keeping the model size fixed. ## [EdTech Platforms Systematically Defer Student Privacy, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28137) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28137) presents a mixed-methods study combining 12 semi-structured interviews with EdTech professionals and a privacy policy audit of 48 platforms coded across five dimensions, achieving strong inter-rater reliability (mean Cohen's Kappa = 0.781). The study finds that privacy is consistently recognized as important but deferred across the product lifecycle in favor of functionality, growth, funding, and immediate educational outcomes. The findings show that 33% of platforms make no meaningful AI disclosure despite visible AI features, and 73% offer only generic accountability and breach-response language, meaning students and parents lack real transparency about how sensitive data is governed. The authors argue that voluntary commitments are insufficient and that enforceable institutional and regulatory mechanisms are needed, which has direct implications for EdTech developers, schools, and policymakers. The policy audit coded 48 platforms across five dimensions, and the analysis shows platforms describe what data they collect relatively well but provide substantially less information about how that data is subsequently governed. K-12 platforms perform better on children's consent where regulation creates explicit requirements, but this advantage does not extend to AI governance or accountability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: EdTech platforms collect highly sensitive student data, including behavioral logs, disability records, and academic histories, yet privacy is often treated as an afterthought rather than a foundational design requirement. A mixed-methods study combines qualitative methods such as interviews with quantitative methods such as policy coding, and Cohen's Kappa is a statistic that measures inter-rater reliability for categorical data by accounting for agreement occurring by chance. This paper applies that approach to examine how organizational priorities shape privacy practices across the EdTech product lifecycle.
