## [Center Bias in Pathology Foundation Models Propagates to Slide-Level Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28231) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28231) evaluates center-associated robustness in whole-slide image (WSI) classification using a controlled training setup with increasing class-center correlations quantified by Cramér's V. The authors benchmark six pathology foundation models (PFMs) across four datasets and two MIL aggregators, test ComBat as a robustification strategy, and introduce the Area Under the Cramér's V Curve (AUCC) metric to jointly capture absolute performance and its degradation as spurious correlation grows. This work shows that non-biological, center-associated signals encoded by PFMs can leak into downstream WSI predictions, which is a serious concern for fairness and generalization when such models are deployed across hospitals with different scanners and protocols. By providing a controlled benchmark and the AUCC metric, it gives the medical AI community a concrete way to measure and compare robustness before clinical deployment. The study uses Cramér's V to control the strength of class-center correlation during training, benchmarks six PFMs with two MIL aggregators on four datasets, and finds that robustness depends on both the PFM representation and the aggregation strategy. Notably, ComBat harmonization does not yield consistent robustness gains across datasets, suggesting that simple batch correction is not a reliable fix for center bias.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Pathology foundation models are large neural networks pretrained on histopathology images to produce general-purpose feature representations, which are then used in whole-slide image analysis. Because whole-slide images are gigapixel-scale, they are typically handled with multiple instance learning (MIL), where a slide is treated as a bag of small patches and a slide-level label is predicted from patch features. Cramér's V is a statistical measure of association between two categorical variables, and ComBat is a widely used empirical-Bayes method for correcting batch effects across known acquisition sites.

## [First Controlled Study Tests LLMs on Classical Arabic Maqama Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28245) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28245) presents the first controlled evaluation of large language models generating classical Arabic maqamat, comparing five models under zero-shot, few-shot, and rule-based prompting. Outputs were assessed via human annotation and an LLM-as-a-judge framework across rhetorical richness, saj density, structural coherence, and stylistic authenticity. This work extends LLM evaluation beyond modern language varieties and poetry into a culturally grounded, stylistically constrained classical prose tradition that has been largely unstudied. It provides a controlled benchmark and methodology that could inform future research on low-resource literary AI and culturally specific text generation. Few-shot prompting most consistently improved saj density, while its effects on rhetoric and coherence varied by model; the strongest models (GPT-4o and GPT-5.4-mini) benefited most from rule-based prompting on those dimensions, yet zero-shot prompting yielded the highest aggregate scores across all five models. Findings were corroborated with a second independent LLM judge, paired statistical significance testing, and non-LLM proxy measures of saj.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: The maqama is a classical Arabic literary genre that arose in the tenth century CE, characterized by rhymed prose (saj), dense rhetorical ornamentation, and episodic narrative structure. Saj is a highly artificial form of rhymed prose defined by end-rhyme, rhythm, and parallelism, making maqama a challenging testbed for evaluating whether LLMs can move beyond surface fluency toward deeper literary competence. LLM-as-a-judge is an evaluation method in which a large language model automatically scores another model's outputs against defined criteria.

## [COMPASS Uses Spatial Transformers to Control Large LLM Robot Collectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28247) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces COMPASS, a scalable decentralized multi-robot architecture that controls large collectives of LLM-based agentic robots using reasoning-space feedback generated locally by a spatial transformer. Experiments show COMPASS produces cohesive flocking formations and generalizes zero-shot to ambiguous instructions while commanding flocks up to 16 times its training scale, flying up to 1024 robots under natural language commands. This work tackles a key scalability bottleneck: LLM-based planners fail on even simple multi-robot tasks as team sizes grow, so a decentralized design that keeps control local could enable much larger autonomous fleets. It sits at the intersection of LLMs, robotics, and multi-agent systems, and the finding that structured input diversity cancels biases may inform how future agent collectives are prompted and coordinated. Feedback is generated locally on each robot by a spatial transformer that aggregates multi-hop messages across the fleet into a learned feedback token, and ablations show reasoning feedback works best when composed with this compact learned token. Hand-engineered feedback with raw state appearing in the language channel obliterates cohesion, and COMPASS decisively outperforms both a centralized frontier LLM policy and a language-only communication ablation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Spatial Transformer Networks, introduced in 2015, are learnable modules that let a neural network explicitly apply spatial transformations such as cropping, scaling, and rotation to input data, improving geometric invariance. LLM-based agents have recently been explored as decentralized decision-makers for multi-robot formation control, but prior work found that directly applying LLMs to such tasks often leads to unstable behavior. COMPASS builds on these ideas by combining spatial transformers with reasoning-space feedback to coordinate very large robot fleets.

## [MemBodied: Fixed-Size Episodic Memory for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28256) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MemBodied introduces a fixed-size episodic memory for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, combining an associative state that records interactions across policy calls with an episode anchor that preserves a compact representation of the initial scene. Across five RMBench tasks requiring memory, it achieves 7.81x the mean success rate of a stateless policy and 2.98x that of vanilla recurrent memory, while reaching 90.6% on LIBERO-Long, a 5.4% gain over the stateless pi_0 policy. Most VLA policies only condition on the current observation, which fails on history-dependent manipulation tasks where key information exists only in past frames. MemBodied shows that compact, fixed-size memory can recover this information without the growing context and inference latency of simply stuffing past observations into the prompt, making it a practical path toward more capable general-purpose robot control. Instead of directly feeding past observations into the context, the model conditions action generation on the current input plus the two memory components, keeping memory size constant. It outperforms the strongest memory-augmented baseline by 1.3x while adding 10x fewer parameters, though the abstract is truncated and full architectural and training details remain to be verified in the paper.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action models are multimodal foundation models that take a camera image and a natural language instruction and output low-level robot actions, replacing separate perception and control pipelines. Associative memory, as in Hopfield networks, is a content-addressable form of recurrent memory that stores and retrieves patterns by similarity rather than by address. Episodic memory refers to storing information tied to a specific episode or interaction history, which is exactly what history-dependent manipulation tasks require.

## [MicroQonv Cuts Quantization Cost in Convolutional Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28358) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MicroQonv introduces a method that combines microscaling quantization with convolutional forward and backward operations by quantizing each tensor only once and applying a modified 'channel-batch-first im2col' transformation after quantization. It reduces quantization cost by 2x for weights and gradients and up to 9x for activations, and cuts memory movement by up to 7.53x versus full precision. This matters because microscaling quantization is increasingly used to shrink neural network parameters to 8 bits or fewer while preserving accuracy, but applying it efficiently in convolutional layers has been a practical bottleneck. MicroQonv's gains could make low-bit training and inference more practical for edge and resource-constrained deployments, including object detection and continual learning. The method reduces microscaling-quantized activation memory movement by 3.5x for YOLOV8nano and 2.2x for YOLOV26nano, and enables 4-bit microscaling in a quantized latent replay strategy for continual learning at the edge, improving accuracy by +5.7% to +11% at negligible accuracy cost. The key trick is quantizing activations before the im2col transformation, avoiding the size blow-up that normally occurs when im2col is applied first.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Microscaling quantization represents neural network parameters with 8 bits or fewer by sharing a scale across small blocks of values, preserving near-full-precision accuracy. Convolutional neural networks process images through convolutional layers, and the im2col transformation is a common way to turn convolution into matrix multiplication for efficient hardware execution. However, im2col expands activation tensors substantially, and a naive quantization pipeline that transfers full-precision data and quantizes each tensor twice causes excessive memory movement.

## [AnchorReasoning Dataset Boosts VLM Reasoning in Long-Tail Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28366) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AnchorReasoning introduces a visually grounded reasoning dataset built on WOD-E2E, containing 416,119 annotated frames and 395,379 decision-critical elements across four major categories and 19 fine-grained types. The paper also proposes a curriculum supervised fine-tuning strategy and an object-size-aware grounding metric, with experiments across eight backbones showing 5-s ADE and FDE reductions of 7.84 and 11.86, plus RFS Frame and Cluster improvements of 1.66 and 1.70, while using 18.5 fewer reasoning tokens and 0.32 s/frame lower latency on average. Long-tail scenarios are a major bottleneck for autonomous driving, and this work provides decision-focused supervision that connects visual evidence to reasoning and planning, potentially improving VLM-based driving systems. The gains in trajectory prediction and reasoning efficiency suggest that grounded chain-of-thought supervision can make VLMs more reliable and faster in safety-critical rare events. The dataset organizes each frame as a visually grounded chain-of-thought (VG-CoT) linking decision-critical element identification and localization, element attributes and implications, driving-action rationale, and action and trajectory planning. The curriculum supervised fine-tuning progressively learns these hierarchical capabilities, and the object-size-aware grounding metric evaluates localization quality; results are achieved with fewer reasoning tokens and lower inference latency.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) combine visual perception with language understanding and are increasingly explored for autonomous driving, but existing driving datasets offer limited supervision for linking decision-critical visual evidence to reasoning and planning. Long-tail scenarios are rare, atypical road events that are difficult to cover with traditional data collection, and WOD-E2E is the Waymo Open Dataset for End-to-End Driving, which contains 4,021 driving segments (about 12 hours) curated for challenging long-tail scenarios occurring with a frequency of less than 0.03%. AnchorReasoning builds on WOD-E2E to provide grounded reasoning annotations and a training method for such rare events.

## [LLM Shopping Agents Fall Back on Human Pricing Heuristics Under Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28372) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28372) introduces "Tool-Lab," an adaptation of information-board process tracing that hides product attributes behind costly tool calls, and uses it to test eight commercially deployed LLMs from three providers. The study finds that under zero acquisition cost, marketing pricing cues such as just-below pricing and promotional framing rarely mislead agents, but once acquisition costs are imposed under a vague goal prompt, the models omit diagnostic attributes needed to compute unit price and make suboptimal choices resembling human heuristics. This matters because consumers are increasingly delegating purchasing decisions to LLM agents, and the paper shows that their vulnerability to marketing manipulation is governed by storefront information architecture rather than being an immutable model flaw. That reframes the problem as a design and consumer-protection issue: retailers and agent builders can reduce or amplify these failures through how product information is exposed and how goals are specified. The key mechanism is a "search-mediated vulnerability": a specific goal prompt largely preserves diagnostic search and choice optimality, while a vague goal prompt under acquisition costs triggers heuristic shortcuts. The evaluation covers eight deployed LLMs across three providers, and the authors emphasize that pricing cues alone rarely mislead when information is free.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Information-board process tracing is a classic experimental method, dating back to the 1980s Mouselab tool, in which decision information is hidden behind boxes that participants must actively open, allowing researchers to observe what information they seek and in what order. Just-below pricing (e.g., $19.99) and promotional framing are well-documented psychological pricing tactics that exploit human heuristics. This paper ports that methodology to LLM agents by turning each attribute reveal into a costly tool call, then measures whether the agents still gather the attributes needed for rational comparison.

## [UECR-GRPO unifies on-policy distillation and GRPO for better credit assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28385) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces UECR-GRPO (Unified Entropy-Calibrated Credit Redistribution for GRPO), which integrates verifier and teacher signals within a single GRPO-style update at both the response and token levels. It achieves average Avg@12 accuracies of 17.21% and 65.09% with Qwen3-1.7B and Qwen3-4B students, exceeding the strongest baseline by 0.89 and 0.56 percentage points across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks. This work addresses a key limitation in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) for mathematical reasoning: sparse final-answer rewards provide little token-level guidance. By unifying on-policy distillation and GRPO, it offers a more principled way to assign credit, potentially improving LLM reasoning training efficiency and performance. The method combines Path-Utility Unification (PUU), which merges verifier reward and a teacher-to-anchor path log-ratio in a single KL-regularized objective before group normalization and PPO clipping, with Entropy-Calibrated Redistribution (ECR), which uses the signed teacher-old-policy token gap to redistribute the verifier-derived component while preserving total task credit via a response-wise zero-sum projection. Full-vocabulary teacher entropy attenuates uncertain guidance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) trains LLMs on mathematical reasoning using only final-answer correctness, which is a sparse reward signal. On-policy distillation (OPD) provides dense token-level feedback from a teacher model on student-generated responses, but teacher preferences may not align with correctness. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm that normalizes rewards within groups of responses, and prior hybrids combine OPD and verifier advantages but apply teacher guidance after group normalization, potentially distorting credit assignment.

A new arXiv preprint (2609.28409) introduces Clifford-VAE, a variational autoencoder that learns to project data onto a Clifford torus in arbitrary dimensions. Experiments on MNIST, FashionMNIST, and CIFAR-10 show its representations are competitive with Gaussian and Hyperspherical VAEs for semi-supervised classification, while outperforming them on VSA benchmarks including self-binding, unbinding, role-filler recovery, and bundle capacity. This work offers a principled way to ground perceptual data into a symbolic reasoning framework, addressing a long-standing problem in the VSA literature. It could advance neuro-symbolic AI by bridging low-level perception and high-level symbolic manipulation, benefiting researchers in representation learning and cognitive computing. Clifford-VAE leverages Clifford algebra to project data onto a Clifford torus, enabling algebraic operations compatible with vector symbolic architectures. The paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet, so its claims await independent validation.

Vector Symbolic Architectures (VSA), also known as hyperdimensional computing, represent information as high-dimensional vectors and manipulate them with algebraic operators. Clifford algebras generalize complex numbers and quaternions, providing a rich algebraic structure for geometric and high-dimensional representations. Variational autoencoders (VAEs) are generative models that learn latent representations, commonly with Gaussian or hyperspherical priors.

## [DART Fixes Lost Motion in Frozen Latent-Flow World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28414) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28414) diagnoses why latent world models that integrate a flow in a frozen self-supervised latent space silently lose object motion, showing that the pretrained flow never moves the manipulated object and that latent-only retraining merely trades stillness for teleport-like motion. The authors trace the failure to the training signal rather than the representation and propose Decode-Augmented Rollout Training (DART), which keeps the representation frozen and retrains only the flow with decode-path supervision. Motion is the property that manipulation and control depend on most, so a world model that silently loses it is unreliable for model-based reinforcement learning and robotics planning. DART offers a cheap, targeted repair that avoids retraining the expensive frozen representation, and the paper's finding that pixel error alone rewards frozen predictions has implications for how world models are evaluated. DART outperforms its latent-only parent on the full protocol, restores the temporal structure of motion, and re-couples predicted motion to the scene; at larger scale it further improves prediction quality, closing nearly half the remaining gap to an oracle-informed interpolation reference. The authors also report the unexpected evaluation finding that pixel error alone rewards frozen predictions, and the abstract is truncated, so full experimental details are not yet visible.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Latent world models encode the underlying state and dynamics of an environment into compact latent variables so an agent can predict and plan efficiently, and Dreamer-style approaches use such latent imagination for control. In this setting a "flow" is a learned module that transports or transforms latent states over time, and it is often trained on top of a frozen self-supervised representation to keep training stable and cheap. The paper shows that this frozen, anchor-sparse, latent-only supervision never specifies where along the prediction horizon change should occur, which is why motion disappears.

## [AEWM: A World Model That Edits LLM Agent States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28416) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes the Agent-Editing World Model (AEWM), which models how reasoning and actions shape future task progress instead of simulating tool responses. It combines Action Judge, which classifies decisions as Critical, Exploratory, or Noisy, with State Revision, which edits noisy reasoning-action continuations, and integrates both into real execution via EditAct. Task-state contamination, where unsupported assumptions and outdated plans persist in an agent's history and distort later decisions, is a major failure mode for long-horizon LLM agents. By directly editing the state that underlies subsequent decisions rather than merely critiquing them, AEWM offers a practical way to improve agent reliability across search, terminal, and software engineering tasks. AEWM achieves 70.5% macro-F1 on the authors' Action Judge benchmark, exceeding the strongest frontier baseline by 10.6 points, and EditAct improves average scores by 3.2-6.7 points across six benchmarks and three agent backbones. Rejection sampling fine-tuning on verified EditAct trajectories (AEWM-RFT) further improves over Self-RFT by 2.2-2.6 points across three domains without online AEWM guidance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents tackle long-horizon tasks by interleaving reasoning, tool calls, and observations, and prior "language world models" typically try to predict the next environment observation or tool response. However, such responses are high-entropy and execution-dependent, so predicting them adds limited value when real feedback is available. Task-state contamination is a related problem in which failed or hallucinated steps remain in the conversation history and raise the per-step error rate on later attempts.

## [Order-Invariant Answers, Order-Sensitive Representations in Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28442) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28442) shows that across 16 language models from 1B to 8B parameters, models that solve reordered mathematical rule sets more accurately also represent different orderings more distinctly, with layer-averaged permutation signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) positively rank-correlated with accuracy (Spearman up to 0.86). The authors introduce permutation SNR to quantify how distinctly ordering patterns are encoded relative to variation across problem instances. This finding challenges the common assumption that correct mathematical reasoning requires order-invariant internal representations, and it motivates distinguishing answer invariance from representation invariance. It offers a representational perspective on mathematical reasoning that goes beyond answer accuracy alone, which is relevant to AI interpretability and reasoning research. The study uses synthetic multi-step function-composition problems, each presented under multiple rule orderings that share the same correct answer, and measures both accuracy and permutation SNR. The correlation holds in every synthetic setting evaluated, but the work is a preprint based on synthetic tasks and models up to 8B parameters, so generalization to larger models and real-world reasoning remains to be tested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Language models are often evaluated by whether they produce the correct answer, but it is unclear whether their internal representations stay the same when equivalent inputs are reordered. Function composition means applying several functions in sequence, and reordering rules that commute should not change the final result. Signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) is a general measure of how strong a signal is relative to background variation; here it is adapted to measure how distinctly a model encodes different rule orderings.

## [SWE-Flux: A Repository-Level Benchmark for LLM Runtime Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28449) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SWE-Flux, a repository-level benchmark for dynamic execution reasoning containing 480 execution-grounded instances across 12 real Python repositories, with gold answers automatically harvested from instrumented test executions rather than written manually or judged by LLMs. Evaluating five LLMs shows the task remains challenging, with the best model achieving only 37% accuracy. This benchmark addresses a gap in existing repository-level QA benchmarks that mainly evaluate static code understanding, providing a valuable evaluation resource for the community. The finding that even top models achieve only 37% accuracy highlights significant limitations in LLMs' ability to reason about runtime behavior, which is critical for real-world coding tasks. The benchmark covers single-test and multi-test questions over control flow, loops, program state, dataflow, exceptions, and program invariants; models perform better on localized behavior such as invariants, intra-procedural control flow, exceptions, and simple loops, but struggle with dataflow, inter-procedural execution, precise state reasoning, and suite-level aggregation. The oracle-harvesting pipeline can generate fresh benchmark variants using input perturbation, successfully harvesting valid variants for almost 90% of selected instances, and the resulting variants are substantially more challenging for the evaluated models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used in coding tasks, but their ability to reason about code execution remains unclear. Existing repository-level QA benchmarks mainly evaluate static code understanding and often rely on LLM-based evaluation, while execution-reasoning benchmarks are mostly limited to snippets or functions. Instrumented test executions layer tracing, logging, or observation onto a test run so that what the software actually does can be inspected, not only whether it passes or fails.

## [Language-Guided Robot Group Joining via Spectral Partitioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28467) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.28467) formulates language-grounded robot group joining, in which a robot uses a natural-language description to identify which people form a target group and then predicts socially compliant poses for joining them. The method generates candidate subsets via recursive spectral partitioning, ranks them with a language-conditioned image-geometry model, and uses a goal predictor that builds a multimodal energy-orientation map over feasible robot poses. This capability is important for assistive robotics such as robotic guide dogs and autonomous mobility scooters, which must decide where to insert themselves into human groups rather than simply navigate to a pre-specified goal. It extends social navigation from goal-reaching to semantic goal inference, a practical step toward robots that operate naturally in crowded human spaces. Experiments on conversations, queues, and audiences across varying group sizes, crowd densities, and visual ambiguities show competitive grounding accuracy with sub-second inference and better joining-pose prediction than all baselines. Real-robot experiments further demonstrate group joining in both static and dynamically changing interactions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Social navigation usually assumes the robot already knows its goal and focuses on reaching it while respecting social conventions. Robot group joining is harder because the robot must first infer which people belong together and where a socially acceptable position to join exists. Recursive spectral partitioning is a graph-partitioning technique that repeatedly splits a graph using spectral (eigenvector-based) information, and here it is used to propose structured candidate subsets of people for language grounding.

## [Anon Optimizer Extrapolates Adaptivity Beyond SGD and Adam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02317) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Anon (Adaptivity Non-restricted Optimizer with Novel convergence technique), an optimizer that allows continuous tuning of adaptivity γ across the entire real-number spectrum, rather than being confined to the 0-to-1 interpolation between SGD and Adam. To guarantee stability in these out-of-bound regimes, the authors propose Incremental Delay Update (IDU), a mechanism that bypasses hard max-tracking strategies like AMSGrad, and they theoretically establish convergence in both convex and non-convex settings. This work challenges the long-held assumption that adaptivity must lie between SGD and Adam, showing that optimal adaptivity often requires extrapolation—such as negative adaptivity for classical CNNs and γ ≥ 1 for Transformers. If validated, Anon could offer a more flexible and scalable optimizer for image classification, diffusion, and large language modeling tasks, potentially influencing how future adaptive optimizers are designed. The paper reveals that extrapolating adaptivity theoretically violates the strict non-decreasing preconditioner assumption, often causing divergence in existing methods; Anon addresses this via IDU, which is more flexible than AMSGrad's hard max-tracking and enhances robustness to gradient noise. Empirically, Anon demonstrates highly competitive and scalable performance among state-of-the-art element-wise optimizers on representative image classification, diffusion, and large language modeling tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Adaptive optimizers such as Adam and non-adaptive methods like SGD are the dominant tools for training deep neural networks, but they exhibit distinct generalization capabilities across different architectures. Prior tunable optimizers attempted to bridge this gap by strictly interpolating between SGD and Adam, confining adaptivity within a 0-to-1 bound. Anon breaks this barrier by allowing adaptivity to be any real number, and IDU ensures convergence even in out-of-bound regimes.

## [ANO: A New Policy Optimization Method Balancing Stability and Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02320) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Anchored Neighborhood Optimization (ANO), a new policy optimization method that directly designs the gain field to balance stability and efficiency, addressing limitations of PPO and SPO. ANO ranks first on Atari (40 games) and MuJoCo benchmarks, and under a learning-rate stress test it degrades by only 0.9% while PPO collapses by 54.5%. This work provides a novel theoretical framework for understanding policy optimization dynamics, which could influence future algorithm design in reinforcement learning and LLM alignment. It offers a more robust alternative to PPO, potentially improving training stability in large-scale applications. ANO uses a C∞ shaping kernel that anchors the identity map at r=1, peaks at a prescribed trust-region boundary 1+ε, bounds the push on severely off-policy samples by a tunable κ+, and exerts a bounded, redescending pull of tunable depth κ- on extreme outliers. The three hyperparameters have decoupled roles, and all internal constants are solved in closed form.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) is a popular reinforcement learning algorithm that uses a clipped objective to limit policy updates, but its hard clipping creates a dead zone where feedback is zero outside the trust region. SPO, an alternative, uses a quadratic penalty that grows unbounded, leading to instability under aggressive step sizes. ANO reframes the surrogate objective as a feedback law on the probability ratio, designing the gain field directly to achieve bounded, redescending behavior.

## [Warrant: Learned Gating Filters Attention Contributions Before Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30139) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.30139v4) introduces Warrant, a learned item-wise gating mechanism that controls how strongly attention-derived contributions are transmitted to prediction before aggregation, without renormalization. On three CyGNet datasets, ungated paths reduced MRR while learned permission partially or almost fully recovered the losses, and in a 5-seed HotpotQA/RoBERTa experiment mean Support MRR rose from .9111 to .9141 while the unsupported selection rate fell from .2271 to .2215. The work challenges the common assumption that attention relevance automatically benefits prediction, showing that selectively gating transmitted contributions can consistently improve performance across backbones and task families. This has implications for NLP and deep learning systems that rely on attention, including multi-hop question answering and knowledge graph completion. Warrant applies learned item-wise permission before aggregation without renormalization, jointly controlling relative allocation and total transmission mass, and interfaces these contributions to prediction scores or states. The paper reports performance comparisons and contribution-level interventions across five task families, revealing both the effects and limits of selective control.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Attention mechanisms let models read relevant information from a context, but the relevance of what is read does not guarantee that its contribution helps the current prediction. CyGNet is a dataset and method for knowledge graph completion that models future facts from history, while HotpotQA is a multi-hop question answering dataset with supporting-fact supervision. Warrant builds on this by learning how strongly each attention-derived item should be transmitted under the current query.

## [Frontier LLMs fail critical clinical criteria more than low-stakes ones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02175) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper presents a small clinician-authored dataset of five clinical scenarios spanning anaesthesia, internal/family medicine, emergency medicine, and obstetrics, each paired with an atomic, weighted, MECE rubric (25-62 criteria per task, 184 total). Evaluating GPT 5.4, Claude Opus 4.7, and Gemini 3.1 Pro, the authors found mean rubric pass rates of 0.47, 0.38, and 0.37 respectively, with the highest-weighted (weight-5, critical) criteria passing at only 32.4-41.7% while low-stakes weight-1 criteria passed at 80-90%. The finding reveals a dangerous inversion of clinical priority: models are most reliable on trivial criteria and least reliable on the critical ones that matter most for patient safety. This highlights a serious gap in medical AI reasoning that could mislead clinicians and harm patients if such models are deployed without rigorous, rubric-based evaluation. Of the 108 critical (weight-5) criteria, 55 (51%) were satisfied by no model at all, and three LLM autoraters reproduced expert met/not-met labels on 92.8-94.6% of 552 graded criteria. The authors position this as a methods-and-preliminary-findings contribution, noting the dataset is small (five scenarios) and intended to scale into a larger benchmark.

This paper applies that approach to deliberately difficult, clinician-authored scenarios to probe whether frontier models can handle high-stakes clinical reasoning.

## [Proper Betting Guarantees Profit in Order-Book Prediction Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06166) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.06166v3) derives a "proper" betting strategy that earns positive expected profit in any prediction market, including central limit order book (CLOB) exchanges, whenever the forecaster's prediction p outperforms the market price q under any proper scoring rule S and liquidity is sufficient. The authors prove this strategy is essentially the only one with such a robust profitability guarantee, and they demonstrate it in a monthlong live pilot on Kalshi. Classical results on profitable prediction-market trading only applied to specific automated market maker (AMM) designs, leaving today's popular CLOB-based exchanges like Kalshi and Polymarket without theoretical guidance. This paper generalizes the guarantee to arbitrary prediction markets, which could reshape how forecasters, AI models, and market designers convert forecasting accuracy into trading profit. The proof rests on a decomposition of expected profit that strictly generalizes the classical AMM guarantee and also explains how strategies can profit even without an accuracy edge. Empirically, across thousands of AI model forecasts, proper betting was the only strategy that reliably converted accuracy into profit, and the paper identifies systematic forecasting personas with differing optimal proper strategies; the live Kalshi pilot showed the approach can survive real-world spreads, fees, discrete fills, and limited liquidity.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Prediction markets aggregate dispersed beliefs into prices that act as probabilistic forecasts of uncertain events. A proper scoring rule is a scoring function under which a forecaster maximizes expected score by reporting their true belief, with examples like the logarithmic score. Classical theory showed a better-than-market forecast yields positive trading profit, but only under specific automated market maker (AMM) designs; most modern exchanges instead use a central limit order book (CLOB), which matches buy and sell limit orders by price-time priority.

It reports state-of-the-art 81.4% accuracy on OmniGAIA at roughly 43% of Gemini-3.1-Pro's cost per question, and 65.0% on WorldSense long-video understanding. This shifts attention toward context management and decision-level training as the key levers for building more capable and cheaper multimodal agents. A critic module reads each noisy observation and passes only usable content to the ledger, keeping the planner's context compact; each run records state, action, and verdict per step, and supervised fine-tuning plus decision-level reinforcement learning on these trajectories further improve the planner.

## [Paper Characterizes When Conditional Memory Helps Scientific Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23982) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.23982v2) systematically investigates when, where, and to what extent conditional memory should participate in scientific reasoning, characterizing the scientific knowledge boundary and running controlled interventions on memory-enabled knowledge-circuit nodes. Based on these analyses, the authors propose a Knowledge Boundary-Aware Router that uses task-specific input proxies available before generation to decide whether memory is activated, which layer-stage nodes receive memory signals, and how strongly those signals contribute. The work shows that memory effects vary substantially across inputs, tasks, and injection locations, meaning naive retrieval-augmented generation can actively harm reasoning the base model already performs correctly. By establishing selective memory allocation as a principle for reliable scientific reasoning, it offers a concrete routing mechanism that could influence future RAG and scientific-reasoning research. Experiments span biological and chemical reasoning benchmarks, covering two backbone model families and six task types, and the router is compared against static routing and activation-rate-matched random routing. The approach more consistently preserves beneficial memory contributions while suppressing memory-induced regressions, though the router relies on input proxies available before generation rather than on the model's internal state during decoding.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Conditional memory gives a language model an explicit lookup pathway that complements dense neural representations, letting it pull in stored knowledge only when useful rather than processing everything it knows at every token. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is the broader technique of connecting an LLM to an external knowledge source before generating a response, but retrieved information can either repair missing scientific associations or introduce distracting shortcuts. This paper asks when that lookup pathway should actually be engaged during multi-step scientific reasoning.

## [Sci-MMR Benchmark Tests Evidence-Grounded Scientific Reasoning in Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11243) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Sci-MMR, a benchmark of 235 multi-hop reasoning tasks across four scientific disciplines, built on structured argument graphs that link scientific claims, citation-grounded knowledge, visual evidence, and supporting regions. Evaluating eight frontier multimodal models, they found answer accuracy consistently exceeds complete-evidence recovery rate by more than 20%, showing that models often answer correctly without fully grounding predictions in traceable evidence. The benchmark reveals that current answer-centric evaluations substantially overestimate the evidence-grounded reasoning capabilities of multimodal research agents, which matters for autonomous research agents expected to search literature, analyze experimental evidence, and generate scientific hypotheses. It could spur new evaluation methods and model designs that prioritize traceable evidence over final-answer accuracy. Controlled interventions identify two bottlenecks: evidence acquisition, where models struggle to extract complete structured evidence from scientific figures (57.2% of failures), and evidence integration, where models fail to translate available evidence into correct conclusions (31.8% of failures). Cropping tools yield only modest gains (+4.5 points), while providing gold evidence improves accuracy by up to 37.0 points, and even with gold evidence the strongest model achieves only 69.1% accuracy on the hardest tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal agents are AI systems that process and act on multiple data types such as text, images, and tools, and they are increasingly used for scientific tasks. Evidence-grounded reasoning requires progressively acquiring, integrating, and verifying evidence before reaching a conclusion, while most existing benchmarks only check final-answer accuracy. Structured argument graphs formally represent support and attack relations among claims, enabling traceable evaluation of whether predictions are backed by scientific evidence.

## [Agentic AI Platform Builds Dual-Layer Knowledge Graph for CMC Process Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11493) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.11493v2) presents a modular agentic-AI platform that converts heterogeneous CMC process-development documents—including digital, scanned, handwritten, and multilingual files—into a queryable dual-layer knowledge graph. Evaluated on 505 questions curated from 38 development reports of a Sanofi small-molecule program, the system achieved 95% Tier-1 multiple-choice accuracy and an 85% Tier-2 LLM-judge pass rate. This work addresses a real-world pain point in pharmaceutical manufacturing: CMC knowledge is fragmented across functions and formats, causing traceability gaps and high knowledge-management costs during technology transfer and regulatory filing. By turning document silos into structured process intelligence, the platform could help pharma teams improve regulatory submissions and cross-functional knowledge reuse, and the proposed evaluation protocol may enable future agentic platforms to be benchmarked against nonpublic databases. The base knowledge layer builds a lexical graph with a Document-Section-Chunk hierarchy via lossless ingestion, while the intelligence layer extracts ontology-aligned entities and bridges cross-document concepts through a provenance-anchored domain graph; LLM agents and a router agent select the retrieval path best suited to each question. Notably, the stricter Tier-2 LLM-judge pass rate degrades on comparative and corpus-wide questions, revealing a failure taxonomy that Tier-1 accuracy alone fails to capture.

## [ERPBench: A Live ERP Benchmark for Screenshot-Only Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17885) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ERPBench, a benchmark that evaluates screenshot-only computer-use agents on a live, reproducible ERP system, scoring each task against ground-truth values stored in the system's database rather than against on-screen appearance. They also released a production-grade harness that gates agent actions behind human approval, and found that across six closed and open-source agents, general capability did not translate into enterprise reliability — some agents saved a record in up to 85% of runs but wrote the correct value in as few as 3%. Enterprise Resource Planning systems run finance, procurement, inventory, and customer operations for organizations worldwide, so unreliable agents that silently corrupt persistent business records pose real operational and financial risk. ERPBench shifts evaluation from screen-level task completion to database-level correctness, giving the field a way to measure the reliability gap that matters most before such agents are deployed in production. The benchmark targets screenshot-only agents, meaning they receive no DOM, API, or database access and must act purely through visual perception and simulated input, and it scores against database ground truth instead of screen state. The accompanying harness adds a human-approval gate before agent actions execute, and the paper further characterizes failure modes specific to enterprise workflows, such as reaching and saving the correct form while persisting an incorrect value.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Computer-use agents are AI systems that operate software through screenshots and simulated mouse and keyboard actions, similar to how a person uses a desktop. ERP (Enterprise Resource Planning) software such as SAP, Oracle, or ERPNext unifies core business processes like finance, procurement, and inventory into a single system of record, with dense interfaces and multi-step workflows. ## [QVAC Genesis III: 191B-Token Synthetic STEM Corpus for Edge AI Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19513) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released QVAC Genesis III, a 191.43B-token synthetic STEM corpus spanning 19 domains, built via a dual generation strategy that uses a weak edge-scale student model as a signal for targeted teacher distillation. In controlled from-scratch ablations with 1.7B-parameter models, training on this corpus outperformed Cosmopedia-v2 and Cosmo-1B on ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM, with gains up to +28.57% on ARC-E and +21.35% on ARC-C. High-quality pre-training data is a critical bottleneck for STEM-focused and educational language models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment, where token budgets are tightly constrained. This open synthetic corpus could help the open ecosystem build small, efficient STEM models without relying on private corpora, potentially lowering the barrier for on-device educational AI. The dual generation strategy converts the weak student model's failures into corrective explanations and expands its successes into contrastive option-level reasoning over all answer choices. The paper also introduces an LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol that extracts final answers from free-form outputs and tracks both accuracy and answer validity, reaching a Valid Answer Rate of up to 99.45%.

## [Fine-Tuning Reshapes LLM Representations, but Causal Components Sit Elsewhere](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21113) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21113v2) investigates how fine-tuning alters internal representations in large language models, including attention patterns and layer-wise activations, and compares these changes against task-relevant components identified by EAP (e.g., attention heads and logit-level activations). The authors find that EAP-identified components are concentrated in specific layers, indicating functional localisation, yet their distribution across layers is largely uncorrelated with the layers that undergo the largest representational shifts during fine-tuning. This work suggests that the layers where fine-tuning visibly changes representations are not necessarily the layers that causally drive task performance, which could reshape how researchers target interventions such as pruning, editing, or selective fine-tuning. It also warns that high overlap in EAP-identified components between two tasks does not guarantee positive transfer and can even cause performance degradation, a finding relevant to multi-task and continual fine-tuning practice. The study uses a causal attribution approach from mechanistic interpretability to locate causally relevant components in fine-tuned models, and it reports that overlap in EAP-identified components across tasks does not translate into cross-task performance transfer when the tasks differ in nature (e.g., classification versus generative tasks). More specifically, fine-tuning on one task can degrade performance on another when the two tasks exhibit a high degree of overlap in their EAP-identified components.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Fine-tuning is a standard way to adapt a pretrained large language model to a downstream task by continuing training on task-specific data, but exactly how it rewires the model's internal mechanisms is not well understood. Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer these mechanisms, often focusing on attention heads and the residual stream through which they communicate. EAP (a causal attribution method) is used here to identify which attention heads and logit-level activations causally matter for a task, distinguishing them from components that merely change during training.

## [DENSE Distills Agent Trajectories into Shortcut Trees for Self-Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21423) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21423v2) introduces DENSE (Distilling Evidence from Nested Subtask Executions), a method that organizes agent execution traces into evidence-grounded nested shortcut trees, condensing repeated attempts and preserving useful steps alongside unfinished requirements. The authors also introduce REFIT, a benchmark measuring how feedback improves retry success, and report that DENSE achieves the highest strict pass rate among methods without external outcome supervision across four agent models on Terminal-Bench 2.1, improving strict pass rate by 7.12-15.64 percentage points while using 19.0-43.6% fewer agent tokens. Online agent deployments generate abundant execution traces, but task-specific verification and expert annotation are expensive to scale, so a method that extracts useful experience without rewards or correctness labels could enable continual agent self-improvement at low cost. This matters for researchers and engineers building agentic systems, since it points toward self-refinement loops that reduce token usage and improve retry success without human supervision. DENSE works in two stages: it first identifies semantic boundaries in ordered queues to build a nested subtask tree, then grounds evidence from the trajectory into that structure. The evaluation uses Terminal-Bench 2.1, a benchmark of curated container-environment tasks spanning software engineering, system administration, data processing, model training, and security; in an exploratory hard-task extension, DENSE also outperformed a Self-reflection baseline in cumulative pass rate after multiple feedback iterations across all four models.

**Background**: Agentic AI systems solve multi-step tasks by executing actions and observing results, producing long execution traces that are usually discarded after a task ends. ## [Risk-Aware Occupancy Representation Boosts End-to-End Driving Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21470) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces risk-aware occupancy, a dense BEV representation that fuses geometric occupancy, map-derived traffic constraints, and future dynamic-agent occupancy as complementary risk signals, and builds ROIDrive, an instance-centric end-to-end driving framework with a dedicated risk-aware occupancy branch. It also contributes RiskOcc4D-nuScenes, a benchmark derived from nuScenes and Occ3D-nuScenes with four automated annotation pipelines for multi-dimensional risk supervision. Conventional end-to-end driving systems model the environment with sparse objects and lane elements, which discards planning-critical information in crowded and occluded scenarios, especially for unstructured obstacles, ambiguous free space, and complex interactions. By explicitly encoding risk into a dense occupancy representation, this work could improve safety in exactly the scenarios where sparse object-based modeling fails, and experiments show it reduces collision rates by 35.0% (UniAD metric) and 52.9% (ST-P3 metric) when integrated with GenAD. The predicted occupancy is tokenized via sliding-window sampling and injected into planning queries via cross-attention, while temporal query consistency mitigates unreliable flickering queries. Experiments on representative occupancy architectures verify the learnability and transferability of the representation, though the abstract does not report latency or compute overhead, which matters for real-time deployment.

Occupancy prediction extends this by assigning occupancy state and semantic labels to voxels, which can describe irregularly shaped or out-of-vocabulary obstacles that sparse object detection misses. nuScenes is a large-scale multimodal autonomous driving dataset (LiDAR, radar, cameras, IMU, GPS) widely used for 3D perception benchmarks, and Occ3D-nuScenes builds dense occupancy annotations on top of it.

## [Leaky-integrator fix tames error accumulation in recursive differenced forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23378) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.23378v2) shows that recursive differenced forecasting is a discrete integrator with a pole on the unit circle, causing biased increment errors to accumulate without bound and rollouts to diverge — at 336 steps normalized MAE reaches 1.6–3.8 for every neural architecture tested, versus 0.80 for a stable linear recursion. The authors introduce leaky-integrator reconstruction, a training-free post-hoc fix using H(z) = 1/(1 - gamma z^-1) with a single fixed gamma = 0.9, which bounds error accumulation and improves long-horizon accuracy across seven diverging architectures and twenty datasets. This identifies a fundamental structural flaw in a widely used forecasting paradigm and offers a two-line, retraining-free remedy that any deployed one-step or foundation-model forecaster can adopt immediately. The gains grow with horizon — from ~3% at H=24 to 51% at H=336 — which matters greatly for long-horizon applications like energy load, weather, and demand planning where recursive differencing is standard practice. The fix is applied purely at reconstruction time with one scalar gamma = 0.9, requires no retraining, and beats the traditional recursive integrator at every horizon, with mean gains over seven diverging architectures and twenty datasets growing from ~3% at H=24 to 23% at H=96, 37% at H=192, and 51% at H=336 (43–75% across architectures; 78% with an oracle pole), bringing all to 0.87–0.97. The paper is a preprint and the fixed gamma is a heuristic rather than a per-model optimum, though an oracle pole shows further headroom.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Many time-series models handle non-stationarity by differencing — predicting one-step changes rather than raw values — and then reconstructing the series by cumulative summation. This reconstruction is mathematically a discrete integrator whose transfer function has a pole on the unit circle, meaning any small bias in the learned increments is added up indefinitely. In signal processing, a leaky integrator moves that pole slightly inside the unit circle so the system forgets old errors over time, and this paper applies that classical idea to modern neural forecasters.

## [Optimizing LLM Watermarks via Multi-Objective Pareto Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.17295) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper (arXiv:2312.17295v2) formulates the trade-off between watermark identifiability and text quality in large language models as a multi-objective optimization problem. For a large class of robust, efficient watermarks, the authors identify Pareto optimal solutions and show they outperform the currently default watermark. Watermarking is increasingly important for detecting AI-generated text and is being mandated by regulations such as the EU AI Act and the California AI Transparency Act. A principled way to improve the identifiability-quality trade-off could make watermarks both more reliable and less harmful to text quality, benefiting model providers, regulators, and content detectors. The approach applies multi-objective optimization to a broad class of robust, efficient watermarks, yielding a set of Pareto optimal solutions rather than a single design. The paper is a specialized academic contribution and does not include community discussion or reported production deployment details.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: LLM watermarking modifies a model's output so that AI-generated text can later be identified, typically using statistical techniques introduced in 2022 and first deployed in production in 2024. Multi-objective optimization deals with problems involving several conflicting objectives, where no single solution optimizes all of them at once. A solution is Pareto optimal if no objective can be improved without worsening another, and the set of all such solutions forms the Pareto front.

## [AdaDim Adapts Dimensionality for Self-Supervised Learning Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12576) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose AdaDim, a training strategy that adaptively balances increasing representation dimensionality H(R) through feature decorrelation and sample uniformity while gradually regularizing mutual information I(R;Z) between representations and the projection head. The method achieves up to 3% performance improvement over common SSL baselines without using queues, clustering, predictor networks, or student-teacher architectures. This work challenges the prevailing assumption in self-supervised learning that optimal representations should have the highest possible dimensionality and the lowest mutual information with the projection head. By showing that the best-performing SSL models achieve a balance between these two factors, it provides a new theoretical and empirical framework that could influence how future SSL algorithms are designed. AdaDim leverages observed training dynamics to adaptively weight between feature decorrelation and uniform sample spread losses, and gradually regularizes I(R;Z) as training progresses. The method avoids expensive techniques such as queues, clustering, predictor networks, or student-teacher architectures, making it computationally efficient.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Self-supervised learning (SSL) trains models to learn useful representations without human-labeled data, often by maximizing agreement between different views of the same input. A key challenge is dimensional collapse, where high-dimensional representation spaces collapse into lower-dimensional subspaces, limiting feature diversity. SSL methods typically use a projection head that maps representations R to an embedding space Z, and recent work suggests the projection head acts as an information bottleneck by reducing mutual information I(R;Z).

## [Discrete Optimal Transport Emerges as Strong Black-Box Audio Adversarial Attack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14959) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2509.14959v4) introduces a black-box adversarial attack that uses discrete optimal transport (DOT) to align frame-level WavLM embeddings of generated or other-person speech to an unpaired pool of bona fide recordings, followed by neural vocoding. Experiments on ASVspoof2019 and ASVspoof5 show the attack substantially raises countermeasure EER and degrades automatic speaker verification across multiple spoofing attacks, transferring across datasets and remaining effective after CM fine-tuning. This reveals a previously underexplored attack vector that requires no access to model parameters, gradients, or training data, making it practical for real-world adversaries. It could affect the security of voice authentication and anti-spoofing systems widely used in banking, device unlocking, and identity verification. The attack operates as a post-processing distribution-alignment step using entropic optimal transport and a top-k barycentric projection, then neural vocoding; analysis with speaker similarity, Fréchet Audio Distance, and embedding visualizations indicates it succeeds by shifting source speech toward bona fide regions of the representation space rather than by maximizing speaker similarity.

WavLM is a self-supervised speech model from Microsoft that produces rich frame-level embeddings, and discrete optimal transport is a mathematical framework for optimally reallocating mass between discrete distributions. Adversarial attacks aim to fool such systems, and black-box attacks are those that do not require knowledge of the target model's internals.

<a id="item-199"></a>
## [WAInjectBench: First Benchmark for Prompt Injection Detection in Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01354) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces WAInjectBench, the first comprehensive benchmark for evaluating prompt injection detection methods specifically targeting web agents, featuring a fine-grained attack categorization based on threat models and datasets containing both malicious and benign text and image samples. It systematizes text-based and image-based detection methods and evaluates their performance across multiple scenarios, with datasets and code released on GitHub. Web agents are increasingly deployed to browse and interact with the web, making them vulnerable to indirect prompt injection attacks embedded in web content, yet no systematic evaluation of detection methods existed for this domain. This benchmark fills that gap, providing a foundation for security research and helping developers choose and improve defenses for web agents. The benchmark reveals that while some detectors can identify attacks relying on explicit textual instructions or visible image perturbations with moderate to high accuracy, they largely fail against attacks that omit explicit instructions or employ imperceptible perturbations. The datasets include malicious text segments from different attacks, benign text from four categories, malicious images from attacks, and benign images from two categories.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Prompt injection is a cybersecurity exploit where crafted inputs cause large language models to follow unintended instructions, bypassing safeguards. Web agents, which are LLM-based systems that browse and interact with websites, are especially susceptible to indirect prompt injection, where malicious prompts are hidden in webpage content and executed when the agent processes the page. Detection methods aim to identify such attacks, but their effectiveness for web agents had not been systematically studied before this work.

## [UniShield: Multi-Agent Framework Unifies Forgery Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03161) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers posted arXiv paper 2510.03161v3 introducing UniShield, an adaptive multi-agent framework that unifies forgery image detection and localization across image manipulation, document manipulation, DeepFake, and AI-generated images. UniShield pairs a perception agent that dynamically selects suitable detection models with a detection agent that consolidates expert detectors into one framework and produces interpretable reports. Existing forgery detectors are typically domain-specific, generalize poorly across domains, and lack an integrated adaptive framework, which limits their real-world deployment. A unified system that works across manipulation, document, DeepFake, and AI-generated domains could improve information integrity and societal security as synthetic media becomes more realistic. The paper reports that UniShield achieves state-of-the-art results, surpassing both existing unified approaches and domain-specific detectors, while emphasizing practicality, adaptiveness, and scalability. However, it is an arXiv preprint (v3, replace-cross) without peer review or community discussion yet, so the claims should be treated with appropriate caution.

DeepFakes are images, videos, or audio edited or generated with AI techniques such as GANs and autoencoders, and they raise concerns about misinformation, fraud, and privacy. Multi-agent systems divide a complex task among specialized agents that coordinate, an approach UniShield applies by combining a perception agent with a detection agent.

## [LLMs Generate Synthetic Preferences for Offline A/B Testing of Slate Recommenders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04541) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2511.04541v2) investigates whether large language models can articulate pairwise preferences between slates — ordered sets of items such as playlists — to enable synthetic A/B testing of slate recommender systems. The authors introduce a validation protocol that measures alignment between LLM-generated preferences and classical RecSys metrics, and checks compliance with preference axioms, then combines these synthetic preferences with the generalized Rao-Kupper model to recover stable rankings across utility weightings. Live A/B testing is expensive and slow, while off-policy evaluation is only reliable when the target utility matches the logged behavior, so LLM-based synthetic testing could serve as a cheap screening stage that reserves costly live experiments for the most promising candidates. This matters for recommender system practitioners who need faster iteration on slate-level ranking decisions without collecting large amounts of fresh user interaction data. The paper positions LLM-based synthetic A/B testing not as a replacement for live experiments but as a screening stage between off-policy evaluation and live A/B testing, and it characterizes how LLM pre-training and configuration affect slate preference articulation. A key caveat is that off-policy estimators were found reliable only when the target utility matches the logged behavior, whereas the LLM-based approach combined with the generalized Rao-Kupper model produced rankings stable across utility weightings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Slate recommendation systems present users with ordered sets of interacting items, such as playlists or product bundles, and must optimize the whole slate rather than individual items, accounting for positional bias and inter-item dependencies. Off-policy evaluation (OPE) estimates the value of a new policy using only previously logged interaction data, avoiding the cost of live experiments, but it is sensitive to distribution mismatch and variance. The Rao-Kupper model is a generalization of the Bradley-Terry pairwise comparison model that allows for tied outcomes via a threshold parameter, and it is commonly used to infer rankings from pairwise preference data.

## [PIECE: Parameter Importance-Driven Continual Learning for Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.15375) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2511.15375v2) introduces PIECE, a parameter importance estimation-based continual learning method that selectively updates only 0.1% of a foundation model's core parameters most relevant to a new task. It offers two importance estimators — PIECE-F based on Fisher Information and PIECE-S based on a second-order normalization combining gradient and curvature information — and demonstrates state-of-the-art continual learning results across three language models and two multimodal models. Catastrophic forgetting is a central obstacle to deploying foundation models in dynamic real-world settings, where models must keep acquiring domain knowledge without losing general reasoning ability. PIECE's ability to preserve general capabilities while learning new domains without accessing historical data or adding parameters could make domain adaptation far more practical and scalable for large language and multimodal models. PIECE updates only 0.1% of core parameters, avoiding the additional parameter overhead of architectural isolation methods and the reliance on inaccessible historical data of replay-based approaches. Its effectiveness hinges on the choice of importance estimator, with PIECE-F and PIECE-S offering two distinct ways to identify task-relevant parameters.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Catastrophic forgetting is the tendency of neural networks to abruptly lose previously learned information when trained on new data, a long-standing problem in continual learning. Parameter-efficient tuning (PEFT) methods such as LoRA address this by updating only a small subset of a pre-trained model's parameters, but their success depends heavily on which parameters are chosen and how they are updated. PIECE builds on this idea by using importance estimation to decide precisely which parameters to update.

## [Fine-Tune, Then Rectify: A Two-Stage Framework for LLM Surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19486) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2511.19486v3) proposes a two-stage framework that combines fine-tuning and rectification of large language models and optimally allocates limited labeled samples across both stages. Its key insight is that the conventional fine-tuning objective of minimizing mean squared prediction error is misaligned with the downstream rectification stage; instead, the authors propose minimizing the variance of prediction errors for mean estimation and a scalarized variance metric for general M-estimation. This work shows that simply minimizing mean squared error during fine-tuning can be suboptimal when the model output is later rectified, which could change how practitioners design fine-tuning objectives and allocate scarce human-labeled data. The reported efficiency gains in estimation and inference translate into significant cost savings for making reliable decisions, making it relevant to anyone using LLMs as scalable surrogates for human responses in market research or social science. The framework leverages the scaling law of fine-tuning to derive an optimal allocation rule for splitting limited labeled human data between the fine-tuning and rectification stages, and empirical analysis validates both the scaling law and the allocation rule. The authors report substantial efficiency gains relative to fine-tuning alone, rectification alone, or using the conventional mean squared error objective within the same two-stage pipeline.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used as scalable surrogates that generate human-like responses, but their outputs contain biases that must be corrected. Two common remedies are fine-tuning, which aligns the model more closely with human responses, and rectification, which corrects biases in the model's outputs. M-estimation is a broad class of statistical estimators defined by optimizing a sample average objective, and it includes maximum likelihood and non-linear least squares as special cases.

## [Parameter-Efficient Method Explores Rashomon Set of Concept Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19636) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2511.19636) introduces a parameter-efficient framework that constructs the Rashomon slice for Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) from a single training process. The method combines a specialized parallel parameter-efficient adaptation module, a checkpointing scheme, and a concept diversity objective to generate multiple equally accurate CBMs with diverse internal logic. This work addresses a key challenge in interpretable machine learning: standard training yields only one model, hiding alternative equally accurate models that may better suit downstream needs. By making the Rashomon set of CBMs practically explorable with low memory cost, it enables trustworthy model selection, resolution of inter-class confusion, and reliable abstention in decision-making. The framework generates multiple equally accurate CBMs from a single training run, achieving better diversity than baselines while using much less memory. It leverages parameter-efficient adaptation and checkpointing to avoid the prohibitive cost of retraining models separately for each member of the Rashomon set.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: The Rashomon set refers to the collection of models that achieve nearly identical predictive performance but rely on fundamentally different internal logic; exploring it is hard in large hypothesis spaces. Concept Bottleneck Models are inherently interpretable architectures that first predict human-understandable concepts and then use them for the final task prediction, making their internal reasoning inspectable. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning adapts large pre-trained models by updating only a small fraction of parameters, reducing computational and memory costs.

<a id="item-205"></a>
## [LoRA-CRAFT Applies Frozen Tucker Decomposition to Cross-Layer Attention Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17510) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LoRA-CRAFT (Cross-layer Rank Adaptation via Frozen Tucker) introduces a PEFT method that stacks pre-trained attention weight matrices across transformer layers into a 3D tensor, applies full Tucker decomposition via Higher-Order SVD (HOSVD), freezes all resulting factors, and trains only small square adaptation matrices on each factor. On LLaMA3-8B it exceeds LoRA's average accuracy using hundreds of times fewer parameters, and on LLaMA2-7B it does the same at a 0.252M parameter budget. This work bridges two previously separate lines of PEFT research—tensor decomposition of gradient updates (LoTR, SuperLoRA) and per-layer SVD of pre-trained weights (PiSSA)—by decomposing pre-trained weights across layers instead of gradients. Its efficiency advantage appears to grow with model scale, suggesting a promising direction for fine-tuning increasingly large language models under tight parameter budgets. CRAFT freezes all Tucker factors obtained from HOSVD and adapts the model through lightweight trainable transformations applied to each factor matrix, rather than training low-rank update matrices as in standard LoRA. Experiments cover the GLUE benchmark with RoBERTa-base and RoBERTa-large, plus commonsense reasoning benchmarks with LLaMA2-7B and LLaMA3-8B, showing competitive performance at extremely low Tucker adaptation parameter counts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 24, 04:00

**Background**: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) adapts large pre-trained models to downstream tasks while training only a small fraction of parameters; LoRA, introduced by Microsoft researchers in 2021, is the best-known example, freezing pre-trained weights and injecting trainable low-rank matrices into each transformer layer. Tucker decomposition factorizes a tensor into a smaller core tensor and a set of factor matrices, and Higher-Order SVD (HOSVD) is a standard algorithm for computing it, extending matrix SVD to higher-order arrays. CRAFT combines these ideas by treating attention weights across layers as a single 3D tensor and decomposing that tensor rather than per-layer matrices or gradient updates.

A new arXiv paper introduces a benchmark for evaluating whether vision-language models (VLMs) can generate structured JSON configurations for plant simulations from imagery using in-context learning. Open-source models from the Gemma 4 and Qwen3.5 families were tested on synthetic and real drone orthophoto datasets of cowpea plots, showing they can produce valid JSON and estimate parameters like days after planting, plant counts, locations, sun angles, and leaf chlorophyll content. This work positions image-to-simulation JSON generation as a promising but challenging task, offering a systematic benchmark that could accelerate AI applications in agriculture and plant phenotyping. It highlights how multimodal reasoning and prompt design affect real-world simulation tasks, potentially inspiring similar approaches in other domains. Five in-context learning methods were evaluated, ranging from format restriction instructions to few-shot image examples with auxiliary grounding information. While VLMs can render approximate simulations, error metrics fluctuate across model families and often remain worse than dataset baselines, especially when pretrained knowledge dominates over weak visual evidence.

## [MessyKitchens: Contact-rich object-level 3D scene reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16868) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces MessyKitchens, a new dataset of real-world cluttered kitchen scenes that provides high-fidelity object-level ground truth for 3D shapes, poses, and accurate object contacts. It also extends the recent SAM 3D single-object reconstruction approach with a Multi-Object Decoder (MOD) for joint object-level scene reconstruction, reporting consistent improvements over the state of the art across three datasets. Physically plausible object-level reconstruction is critical for robotics and animation, where objects must obey non-penetration and realistic contact constraints. By releasing a cluttered-scene dataset with contact-level ground truth and a joint multi-object decoder, this work gives the community a new benchmark for evaluating registration accuracy and inter-object penetration. The dataset is shown to significantly improve over previous datasets in registration accuracy and inter-object penetration, and MOD is evaluated on three datasets with consistent gains over prior methods. The benchmark, code, and pre-trained models are promised to be publicly released on the project website.
